## A

REPLY TO THE OBJECTIONS

Robert Nelson, Efq;

Anonymous Author,

Against $\mathrm{D}^{r}$ CLARKE's Scripture- Doctrine of the Trinity.

Being a COMMENTARY upon Forty Select Texts of Scripture.

An ANSWER to the REMARKS of the Author of, Some Confiderations concerning the Trinity, and the Ways of managing that Controverfy.

By SAMUEL CLARKE, D. D. Rector of St James's Westminster, and Chaplain in Ordinary to Her Majesty.

London, Printed for James Knapton, at the Crown in St Paul's Church-Yard. 1714.



A REPLY to Mr Nelfon.

# TO ROBERT NELSON Efq;

S I R,

T

HE Remarks upon my Scripture-Doctrine of the Trinity, which you published in your Life of the Right Reverend Bishop Bull; and the Letter

which you prefixed to a Book entitled, The Scripture-Doctrine of the most Holy and Undivided Trinity vindicated from the Misinterpretations of Dr Clarke; are written with so much Candour and Civility, with so much Fairness and Temper, that they may well become an Example to Clergymen and Others, of writing Controversy in such a manner, as that Truth may by weighing Arguments on both sides be fully searched after, and our common Christianity suffer nothing in the mean time through want of Meekness and Charity. The Book itself also is written with so good a Spirit, and so very different from any that have hitherto appeared, that though I think the Notions it contains are for the most part unintelligible,

4

## A REPLY

telligible, and the Arguments of small force, yet, upon account of the good Spirit wherewith it is written, and the particular recommendation you have been pleased to give it, it has a just claim to be considered with all Fairness and Calmness.

IN your Life of the Right Reverend Bishop Bull, the principal, and indeed the Only Considerable Objection you allege against my Book, is; that almost all my Citations out of that Learned Author are represented in a very different View from That which the Author had certainly in writing those Passages; as are also the Citations out of the Fathers themselves, which Dr. Clarke here met with, and both accommodated to his own purpose, and That frequently without so much as the least Notice taken of the Explications and Answers given to them by Bishop Bull. This Objection, you are sensible, I had indeavoured to prevent; by declaring beforehand, that I cited Modern Authors and Fathers too, not with any intention to show what was in the whole the Opinion of those Authors; (for the Question was to be determined by Scripture only, and not by Human Authority;) but I cited them, only to show what Important Concessions they were obliged to make; even such Concessions, as of Necessity and in strictness

## to Mr Nelfon.

ness of Argument inferred my Conclusion, whether the Authors themselves made any such Inference or no. And This you do indeed very justly and fairly acknowledge. But still, notwithstanding this reasonable Acknowledgment, you continue to allege, that it is enough to have shewn what manner of Judgement we ought to make of Dr Clarke's Citations, for they are generally applied much after the same Manner and with the same Views; and you call it (in a less kind manner than usual,) the Doctors Artful Way of citing Authors and Books, and infinate that therefore my Quotations are not much to be depended upon. That sometimes they are not indeed much to be depended upon, for knowing (as you say) the Opinion or Judgment of any Writer; is very true, because they were not brought for That Purpose: But then they are very much to be depended upon for another and far more material reason, viz. for knowing the Truth itself of Things. And in order to This End, they are always so much the more to be depended upon, and the more to be regarded; as the Author, from whom they are cited, was upon the whole more different in his Opinion, from what those particular Citations seem to express. For Writers upon controversial Points, feldom make more Concessions in favour of any opinion different from their own, than the Weight of Truth and

A 3 Reason

5

pag. 332.

pag. 326.

pa. 326.

6

## A REPLY

Reason necessarily extorts from them. Wherefore in citing and making Use of such Concessions, there is no other Artfulness, than that most justifiable one of drawing Arguments strong and conclusive from such Premises, as even an Adversary is of necessity forced to grant. Nor needs it at all to be regarded, that some of the Writers, from whom such Citations are taken, do themselves indeavour sometimes to draw Conclusions very different. For in all Argumentation whatsoever, it is well known that the Premises only are of any Weight; and that Conclusions always stand for nothing, except the Reader finds himself compelled to draw them, as well as the Writer. Nevertheless, though the Truth of the Scripture-Doctrine does not at all depend upon it, yet, for the satisfaction of such as are apt to pay a greater deference to Authority than is reasonable, I shall not omit taking this occasion to assure you, that, as far as I am able to judge from the whole Course and Tenour of the Writings of the Antients, All the passages cited in my whole Book from the following Principal Writers, viz. Clemens Romanus, Ignatius, Justin, Irenaeus, Origen, Novatian and Eusebius, are not only particular Concessions, but do express what seems moreover to have been in the whole the real Sentiments of the respective Writers themselves: And in the First Part

## to Mr Nelfon,

Part of my Book, the Passages cited out of Other both Antient and Later Writers concerning particular Texts, whatever different Opinions the same Authors perhaps elsewhere contend for, yet seem always to express their real Sentiments, what was in Their Days understood to be the meaning of those particular Texts.

Your next Objection is as follows: D'Clarke afferts expressly, that the Scripture, when it mentions God absolutely and by way of Eminence, means the Person of the Father; as likewise when it mentioneth the One God, or the Only God: though he could not, after having read the Defense of the Nicene Faith, be ignorant that this was contrary to the Mind of the Catholick Fathers. Now, how contrary soever This Assertion had indeed been to the Mind of the Catholick Fathers, or of Any other Writers whatsoever; yet it would nevertheless have been most necessarily, and most evidently True; because it is a Matter of Fact, that shows itself in full Light to every Mans own Eyes at the first Sight; there appearing, in my Collection, Chap. 1, Seet. 1 and 2, more than 300 Texts, wherein either the word [GOD] absolutely, or the title [One God,] is, in the express words of the Text itself, put in contradistinction, not to the Creature, or False Gods, but to

That the Father of Jesus Christ (fays he) is Alone the One True God; if it be understood of That Prebeminence of the Father, by which He Alone is of Himself [by Self-existence] the True

God; we confess that This Assertion is most true. And again: We readily grant, that for this Cause the Father [the Father Alone,

fo he expresses it in the beginning of the very same Sentence,] is most usually stiled properly [or peculiarly] the True God, both in the Holy Scriptures, and in the Writings of the Antients; the Divine Persons are mentioned Together. And again: The Ante-Nicene Writers commonly call God the Father, by way of distinction, sometimes GOD absolutely, sometimes The One God, sometimes The God and Father of All, (according to the Texts, 1 Cor. 8, 4; Eph. 4,

## to Mr Nelfon.

6; Joh. 17, 3;) Namely, because the Father Alone is God of Himself, [by Self-existence;] but the Son, is only God of God. This is very plainly the Doctrine of Scripture, whatever had been the Opinion of the Catholick Fathers. But neither, in Truth, is This Assertion at all contrary (as you conceive it to be) to the Mind of the Catholick Fathers; much less, from the Defense of the Nicene Faith doth it appear (as you suppose,) to be contrary to the Mind of those Fathers. For however the Fathers after the Council of Nice express themselves at other times, and whatever opinions they profess; yet in This Point, even They, (as well as the Antienter Writers who spake more uniformly,) generally confirm my Assertion in the fullest and clearest Terms that are possible to be used. As you may find in above 50 passages cited in my Second Part, §. 9 and 11; And as the Author himself of the Defense of the Nicene Faith fully acknowledges, both in the Place I just now cited, and in many other passages: As where he says

; I believe in One God, the Father Almighty, c. And again ; that even the Fathers after the Council of Nice, make no scruple to stile the Father the Origin, the Cause, the Author of the

Son; nay to call the Father therefore The One God. The Evidence of This whole matter is so exceedingly clear, that there is no possibility of evading it otherwise, than

## to Mr Nelfon.

will very much wonder at your citing this Chapter of his; which, instead of containing what You think you find there, does on the contrary very clearly and fully express the doctrine in the same manner as I have done; infomuch that there are Few more remarkable Testimonies in all Antiquity, of the opinion of the Primitive Church in this whole matter. It is a large Chapter; and the Design of it throughout, is to explain how Christ is truly God and Lord, and yet that at the same time the Father is strictly and emphatically the One and Only God. The whole Chapter is worth every learned persons perusal: But the Sum of it, is This. When the Scripture speaks of False Gods, it always does it, says he [cum aliquo additamento & significatione, per quam ostenduntur non esse dii,] with the Addition of some word which shows them to be indeed No Gods; as when it calls them the Gods of the Gentiles, or the like: But whenever it uses the word [God or Lord] absolutely, and without any such additional explication; then (says he) it always means a person who is truly and really God or Lord; That is to say, either God the Father [qui dominatur omnium] who ruleth over All, or the Son [qui dominium ACCEPIT a Patre suo omnis conditionis] who RECIEVED from his Father dominion over the whole Creation: According

The Argument therefore of Ireneus, is plainly This: The Gods of the Heathen are False Gods, or no Gods at all: God the Father is True God or Lord, because he originally Ruleth over All: The Son also is True God or Lord, because he has Truly and Really RECIEVED from his Father Divine Power and Dominion over the whole Creation. But still the Father Alone

## to Mr Nelson.

Alone is strictly and properly the One and Only God. For so he concludes his whole Argument : I pray to Thee, O Lord, the God of Abraham, the God of Isaac, and the God of Jacob, the FATHER of our Lord Jesus Christ ; who hast made Heaven and Earth, and Rulest over All; who art [Solus & Verus Deus] the ONLY and the True God, above whom there is no other God : Who [ \* praeter dominum nostrum Jesum Christum, dominione quoq; dominaris Spiritus Sancti, ] besides [that thou governest by] our Lord Jesus Christ, rulest also by the Dominion of the Holy Spirit : Grant that every one that readeth this my Book, may [cognoscere Te, quia Solus Deus es] know Thee to be the ONLY God, &c. And he alleges finally That Text of St. Paul, 1 Cor. 1, 4, We know that an Idol is nothing in the world, and that there is None other God but One ; For though there be that are Called

* These words are obscure, and seem not to have been well understood by the Latin Translator, and are perhaps corrupt: But however they be understood, they plainly refer to the Supremacy of the Father, with regard both to the Son and Holy Spirit. The later Editions of Irenaeus read this passage, per dominum nostrum Jesum Christum, dominationem [or donationem] quoq; dona Spiritus Sancti. But this, though it makes Sense in it self, yet does not at all suit the connexion of the Authors Whole Discourse; Neither does the word quoq; come in at all better with the Benedictines dominationem, than with Dr Grabes donationem. The old Editions, though corrupt, yet in all probability retain something that is nearer to the Authors true meaning.

You could not have picked a Chapter out of the whole works of Irenaus, or indeed out of all Antiquity, more contrary to your own purpose, or more pertinent to mine.

But however This might be, CERTAIN it is, you say, that Dr Clarke, who had so ample a Collection of Testimonies concerning the Trinity before him in this Treatise [of Bp. Bulls,] as well as in Petavius; bath not made That Use of them, which this indefatigable and judicious Collector [Bp. Bull] did; or which might have been expected from a Person of so great a Character in the Church and learned world, as Dr Clarke. That I did not make the Same Use of my Collections, that is, did not draw from them the same Conclusions, which some very learned and judicious Men had formerly done; is indeed CERTAIN, but no part of the Question between us. But, that I did not make That Use of them, which might reasonably have been expected from the Character you are pleased with great Civility to bestow upon me; this, it might have been expected a Person of so great a Character in the learned world as Mr Nelson.

## to $M^{r}$ Nelfon.

son, should not have Concluded, but have left to the Reader to judge whether it followed from his Premises or not.

The List you have drawn up of the Passages cited by me out of the learned Bp. Bull, and ranked in Columns referring both to His book and Mine, that the Reader may at leisure compare and consider them; is a very fair and reasonable Method of setting that matter in a true Light. There are in this List of yours, Thirty-eight Passages, from which you leave the learned to judge what Conclusion rightly follows: And so I also am willing to leave them. Only, since you have thought fit, as a Specimen, to make some short Remarks upon the first Four of them; you will give me leave briefly to consider those Remarks.

The first Passage, you say, is a Remark of Bp. Bull, upon certain words of Origen, wherein the Bp. has shown (against Huetius) that Origen spake of Christ as an Exemplar in his Humane Nature, and not with respect to his Divine Nature: And not the least word is said, that can justly be interpreted of the Son's Inferiority to the Father in Nature, but rather the contrary: For he there sheweth, by clear and undoubted Testimonies taken from the Book against Celfus, that Origen did hold and teach the Son to be very God, Uncreated, Immortal, Immutable,

## A REPLY

ble, &c. I have abridged your words, but I am perfwaded your Candour will not complain that I have in the least altered your Sense. Now in every part of this Reasoning of yours, there is (I think) something defective. For how many Other Attributes and Perfections soever, Origen in his Book ascribes to the Son; yet unless he ascribed Supremacy also, (which he no where does, but on the contrary every where expressly and with great caution excludes it,) this cannot at all affect the Question: See my Scripture-doctrine, pag. 359 and 266. And though he had indeed in Other clear and undoubted passages (which yet he no where has) asserted the Son to be Supreme as well as the Father; yet it would not at all follow (unless Origen were a Writer infallibly consistent with himself,) that he did not interpret the particular text referred to, in the manner that I affirmed him to interpret it. And though Bp. Bull does indeed indeavour to show, that Origen in the place here referred to, speaks of Christ as an Exemplar in his humane Nature; yet he expressly acknowledges [si daremus Origenem ibi loqui de Christo quatenus Deus est,—baud minus recte ea ratione Patri primas tribuere potuit,] that if it be granted that Origen there speaks concerning Christ as God, yet—as deriving—from the Fountain of the Father, he might rightly.

## to Mr Nelfon.

rightly, even in this sense, yield the prebeminence to the Father: Which acknowledgment is all that I cited him for; and therefore it ought not to be suggested that I had cited him unfairly. As to the distinction you allege, that here is not the least Word said, that can justly be interpreted of the Son's Inferiority to the Father in NATURE, but rather the contrary: I shall not contend with you about metaphysical Words, which since I have constantly avoided because of their Abstract and Ambiguous Signification, you should not have chosen to use them in representing my Sense. Bishop Bull expressly owns the Person of the Son to be, in his Highest Capacity, subordinate to the Person of the Father: Which is a Notion very intelligible, (whatever be the Nature, Substance or Essence of either;) and is all (I think) that the Honour of God and the Whole Doctrine of Scripture obliges us to contend for. The Word, Nature, (as it signifies the Nature of any Person, abstract from the Person himself,) is a Metaphysical Term, of great Ambiguity and Obscureness, and of no Use that I know of in any Question, but to introduce more Difficulties by dark Expressions, than are really found in the Things themselves.

17

The next Passage, you fay, cited by Dr Pag. 330. Clarke, is no more for an Inferiority of Nature in the Son, than the first is; the plain Mean-

A REPLY

ing of it being, that——Christ appearing to the Holy Men under the Old Testament, received from them divine Honours, and was manifested to them by the most High Name of God. Mr Bull first proveth &c. — And there is nothing in this whole Method, nor in any particular Argument under it, which doth tend in the least to favour such an Inequality of NATURE in Father and Son, as is included in That Scheme which it is brought to support. The passage here referred to, are those words of the learned Bp. Bull, wherein he declares and approves the opinion of the Antient Fathers, that the Angel of the Lord (Acts 7, 30,) which appeared to Moses in the Bush, and said, I am the God of thy Fathers, was the Son appearing in his Fathers Name. Now since the Scripture every where represents the Father, as absolutely Invisible; and those Antient Writers, whose Opinions Bp Bull cites and approves, always suppose it impious to imagine that the Supreme God and Father of All ever appeared visibly in his own Person; the Inference I designed should be drawn from hence, is, that the Person of the Son is Subordinate to the Person of the Father, whose Angel or Messenger he is, and in whose Name and by whose Mission he appears. All this, the learned Bp. makes no scruple to grant; and therefore 'twas no misrepresentation of his words, to cite them

to Mr Nelfon.

19

them as granting it. Concerning the Metaphysical and obscure Notion of NATURE indeed; as the Scripture * no where mentions it, so neither have I any where drawn any consequence at all concerning it. And what Notion the learned Bishop had of that matter, was not my business to inquire; since my Scheme was entire, without entring into that metaphysical part; and, whatever was his Notion in other respects, yet That which I cited him as granting, he did expressly intend to grant.

The Third passage, you say, is certainly pag. 331. no better applied, than the former; the Design of that whole Chapter from whence it is taken, being to answer a principal Objection, which had even shocked Mr Bull himself for a good while, that would infer a Difference in the divine nature of the Son from that of the Father, the one Manifestable, the other Not manifestable. To which I answer as before: whatever was the Design of the whole Chapter, yet the Passage cited by me is neither misrepresented nor misapplied, because the Author in That passage expressly intended to grant, what I cited him as granting.

The Fourth passage, [which is as follows; Quod Origenes in loco citato dicit, Filium etiam quà Deus est, hoc est, Deus ex Deo, Patre minorem esse; planè Catholicum esse, atq; etiam a Patribus, qui post B2 Nicænum

Nicænum Concilium Arianam hæresin acerrimè impugnárunt, defensum oftendemus. i.e. "What Origen affirms in the place before-cited, viz. that the Son, even as he is God, (that is, God of God,) is Less than the Father; this we shall show to be very Catholick, and maintained even by the Fathers After the Council of Nice, who most strongly opposed the Arian Herefy". Bp. Bulls Defense of the Nicene Faith. Sect. 2, chap.

9, § 12. This Fourth passage, you say, seemeth indeed to be very much to the Purpose; and every one that reads it as it is cited, and will not be at the pains to consult either what follows it, or what is there distinctly referred to, may be easily led to think, that our Author [Bp. Bull] was not a Defender, but an Underminer of the Nicene Faith, by maintaining the Son, even AS HE IS GOD, to be LESS than the Father: Which though it be most true in a certain sense which He hath explained, in conformity to Primitive Testimonies, and to the Confession of the Council of Nice itself, as He is God of God; yet is both diametrically opposite to his plain Meaning, and to what he defended for the Catholick Faith with so much strength, if thereby it be understood that there is Greater and Less in the divine Nature and Essence. What the meaning is of Greater and Less in Abstract metaphysical

# to Mr Nelfon.

metaphysical Notions, which have no real Existence, such as are Nature and Essence, I confess I understand not. But that the Son, the Intelligent Agent or Person himself If, is in his Highest Capacity Subordinate to the Person of the Father; this the learned Bishop does, in the passage now cited, most expressly grant; and nothing that he has said concerning the obscurer Notions of Nature and Essence, in the Other parts of his Book to which both You and the Author himself refers us, does in any wife revoke so plain and intelligible a Conceffion.

All the rest of the passages which I cited out of this Learned Writer, you leave to the Judgment of the Reader, without making any further Remarks upon them: And I also am willing there to leave them: Being fully perfwaded, that any careful and intelligent Reader, who shall give himself the Trouble to compare them, will find I have nowhere misrepresented the Sense of the Author: All the Citations being so many plain Concessions, which he really intended to make in the manner I have represented them; though be did not indeed always draw such Inferences from them, as I think follow by just and necessary Argumentation. And the like is to be understood of my Citations out of the Later Fathers, and out of such other Writers, as were not of the same opinion with B 3 me

to $M^{r}$ Nelfon.

in every part: Your candidly allowing, that certainly the Design of digesting with care and pains under proper heads the Texts of Scripture which relate to this Doctrine, is very commendable; and then drawing up a Scheme of the Whole, and reducing and explaining it, in a great number of particular and distinct Propositions: Your declaring, that there is no sufficient Reason in your Opinion, for any Learned Person who may think me mistaken, to treat me angrily and in the Spirit of Popery; as if we were not to use our own Understandings in Matters of Religion, as well as in other Matters; but must always plead for what Notions happen at any time to prevail, as if they were Therefore true because they prevail: And your wishing that the Spirit of Meekness and Christianity did more universally influence the Management of all our Disputes both Religious and Civil: In all these Instances, Your Fairness and Justice can never sufficiently be commended; And the Personal Esteem you are pleased to express for Me, is more than perhaps will by some be thought excusable. Nevertheless, since in the other parts of your Letter, though the Whole be indeed written with an excellent Spirit, yet there are some particulars wherein I cannot but differ from you in opinion; I know you will not be displeased

# A REPLY

nor take it at all amifs, if I freely communicate my Thoughts to you thereupon.

In the first place, the mention you make of drawing in Authors and Authorities to speak that which we are certain enough they could never mean, feels to refer to the Objection you had before made, in the Life of Bishop Bull, concerning my manner of quoting Him and other Writers. To which Objection I have already answered at large

See above, above; viz. that fuch Concessions, as any Writer is by the Weight of Truth obliged and does expressly mean to grant, however he may not perhaps mean to draw the fame Inferences from them as I do; fuch Concessions, far from being unfair Citations, are of all others the most pertinent Testimonies that can be alleged in favour of any Assertion whatsoever.

The Commendation you give the Author whose Book you publish, viz. that he has applied the Rules of Criticism not Against but For the Faith, of which the Catholic Church is in Possession; seems not altogether agreeable to that exact Impartiality, which you express in many other parts of your Letter. For the thing you here commend him for, is his contending for an Opinion, upon account of its being (or being vulgarly supposed to be) a Common one. (Which Argument,

to $M^{r}$ Nelfon.

Argument, you well know, holds equally in All Religions, and particularly in that of the Church of Rome with the greatest Advantage, who allow None to be Members of the Catholick Church but Themselves.) Whereas a good Writer ought not to intend beforehand to apply the Rules of Criticism for or against any thing; but to find, by using the right Rules of Criticism in understanding the Scripture, what Doctrine is true or false, and consequently what opinions he ought to declare for or against. How far I myself observed this Rule, the candid Reader will judge, by observing that I not only omitted none of the Texts which Others had alleged, but moreover mentioned some Texts seemingly against me, (such as Luke 1; 16, 17; See Scripture-doctrine, pag. 84;) which I could not find that the Writers on the other side of the question had taken any notice of for Themselves.

There are, (you say, p. 9,) about Forty Texts, upon which the main stress of Dr Clarke's Theory depends, that are here examined. What Advantage your learned Friend can make of these Forty Texts, will appear in the Sequel. But had he been able to prove ever so strongly, that the Truth of my Theory did not at all follow from these Forty Texts; yet it would have availed

25

26

p. 1g. 14.

A REPLY

availed him nothing. For I alleged, besides these, above 300 other Texts, of which he takes not the least Notice, in which All that I contend for is as clearly expressed, as any thing can be expressed in words.

What you allege (pag. 19,) concerning the Wounds which Dr Clarke's Book may have given his Mother the Church, (which has been so Kind to him, pag. 9;) is also what I cannot perfectly reconcile with the other parts of your Letter. The Kindness which has been shown to me both in other respects, and in That likewise which I guess you had in your Thoughts, I acknowledge most readily with all gratefulness: And God forbid that I should ever, so much as by Accident, much less by Design, give any Wound to the Church: For Wo be to him, by whom (even though it be but through Carelessness only,) any Offence cometh. But since you with all fairness acknowledge, and the Church itself constantly declares, that to the Gospel and to the Testimony the Appeal must always be made; that is, that the Scripture is the only Rule of Truth in Matters of Revelation; I cannot imagine upon what ground you can suppose a careful Examination into the Whole Doctrine of Scripture, made with Sobriety and Seriousness, with Modefty and all possible

## to $M^{r}$ Nelfon.

النموذج التالي :

sible Decency, is giving Wounds to the Church. On the contrary, it seems to Me one of the greatest possible Expressions of true Zeal and Concern for That Church, which is to be glorious, not having spot or wrinkle or any such thing, but holy and without blemish; and one of the Best Methods, if not the Only effectual one, of healing, rather than enlarging, the Wounds of the visible Church; that every one contribute, as much as in him lies, towards making the Scriptures of God, in reality and in Effect, as well as in Profession, the only Standard of Faith and Practise; and that all Opinions, and Explications of opinions, be Perpetually compared with and tried by That uncontested Rule. To which purpose you yourself have excellently discoursed, in some parts of Bishop Bull's Life.

You will give me leave from a Passage which I meet with in your Letter a little lower, to observe how strange and unaccountable an Influence the Use of Metaphysical and Scholaftick Terms sometimes has upon the Understandings even of Wife and Good Men. The Divine Oeconomy of Father, Son, and Holy Ghost, IN THE UNITY OF THE DIVINE ESSENCE, is taught us, you say, by our excellent Church in her most publick and authentic Acts, and is the Common Faith both of

27

pag. 24.

28

## AREPLY

of Protestants and Papists, or the Common Salvation as delivered in all the Churches Reformed and Unreformed. A Notion expressed with such Solemnity as This, is what an ignorant and unprejudiced person, hearing it thus delivered, would immediately expect to find in every page of his Bible, and of all Antient Christian Authors, and in every part of the Forms now used in the Church: Whereas, in Truth, both the word [Essence] itself, and the Phrase [in the Unity of the Divine Essence,] are merely Scholastick and Metaphysical Terms, of very uncertain Signification, (as any one will find, when he indeavours to declare what he means by them;) not Once found in the whole New Testament, nor in the Orthodox Fathers of the First Ages, nor in any part of the Articles or Liturgy of the Church of England, which are its most publick and authentic Acts. Now of What Use can the introducing such new Terms be? For either they have no determinate Signification at all: Or else they are intended to express the same Doctrine, which is taught in Scripture; And then, why could not That Doctrine have been as well and better expresst by you in those same words, which the Wisdom of God thought most proper to express it in? Or else, lastly, they express something different from what is taught in Scripture; and then

## to Mr Nelfon.

then they are very bad and dangerous expressions indeed. Concerning This matter also, you yourself have discoursed most excellently, in your Life of Bishop Bull; and shown with all Strength and Clearness the mischief of such Scholastick and Systematical Terms, in the passages referred to in the Conclusion of my Letter to Dr Wells.

In the passage next following in your Letter, I cannot but observe, that by your changing your Expression in one and the same Sentence, you insensibly drop the whole Force of the Argument you intended to make use of. For when you say, many Learned and Good Men apprehend me, Not to have represented the True Scripture-doctrine of the Trinity, but to have substituted in its room another of my own, Against——(Against the True Scripture-doctrine, your Argument required you to say; But instead of That, you only say) Against the True Apostolical TRADITION of that Doctrine, and the COMMON INTERPRETATION of the Scriptures throughout all the Ages of the Church of Christ. Now, against Tradition, and against common Interpretation, is against no body can tell what. For, other Apostolical Tradition, than what is delivered in Scripture, there is None to be found that can at all be depended upon; and common Interpretation signifies

29

pag. 25.

## A REPLY

pag. 24,
25, 26.

signifies as many different Doctrines, as there are or ever have been different Churches in the World. As I have shown at large in my Letter to Dr Wells.

The Excuse, which you (who know well how to write with the greatest Temper yourself) are willing to make for Others who write without That Spirit of Meekness and Christianity, is a very charitable one; viz. that they think they obey the Apostolical Injunction of Contending Earnestly for the Faith. But This, though it will indeed excuse much Zeal, yet it will not excuse Wrath and Uncharitableness. For, the Wrath of Man worketh not the Righteousness of God. And, as far as I have been able to observe, those persons have always been in proportion most hot and angry, whose Doctrine has been least agreeable to Scripture, and who have been much less concerned for the Doctrine delivered TO, than for That which (as you well observe, pag. 24,) was delivered BY the Saints, that is, by those Writers whose opinions they happened most to like.

But the most important Objection in your whole Letter, is That which follows.

fig. 15.

Too many, you say, think themselves able to overturn Any Foundations whatsoever, if such a Method, as Dr Clarke proposes, be allowable,

to Mr Nelfon.

allowable, with respect to the most solemn Acts and Deeds of That Church and Community whereof we are Members, and to substitute what they please in their Room: That from a Method of This Nature, we are threatened with the overturning of Foundations both Sacred and CIVIL: That if the Judges and Others learned in the Law, shall follow the same Method of interpreting the Laws of the Land, and accommodating the CIVIL Oaths and Engagements, as Dr Clarke has taken in interpreting and accommodating the Sense of the CHURCH, in her most authentic Forms and Declarations before God and Man, and of the Venerable Fathers of the Catholic Church; there are Many of the Opinion, that every thing might be easily leaped over, and that no Establishment could be so strong as to last long: And, Who knows whereabouts his Religion, Liberty, or Property may be, if such a Latitude of Interpretation be defensible? I am sure I have represented this Objection of yours, in its full force. The Reader will observe, that 'tis an Objection ad hominem only, and nothing at all to the Merits of any question concerning the Truth or Erroneousness of any Opinion or Doctrine. Nevertheless, because 'tis indeed a very Important Objection ad hominem, I will indeavour to explain myself very distinctly to you upon it. With respect to Civil matters,

31

pag. 19.

pag. 21.

pag. 22.

32

A REPLY

matters, which are in their own Nature indifferent, there is lodged in every Government a Legislative Power, which makes what Laws it thinks fit, and may, in things which are the proper Subject of Civil Authority, either establish one thing by Law, or another thing quite different from it: And the Subject shall equally be bound in either Case, because in such Instances no Superiour Authority hath required him to act otherwise: Nor can there, in this Case, be any other Rule, by which to interpret the Law; but only by discovering, from the obvious Signification of the words, what was in the Whole the Real Sense and Intent of the Legislators. But now in Ecclesiastical matters, (excepting Rites and Ceremonies, which are of a Civil nature,) the Case is very different. The Church, in matters of Doctrine, has no Legislative Power: The Protestant Church pretends to no

Article 20. such Power; but only to be a Witness and a Keeper of Holy Writ. Christ and his Apostles have delivered and unalterably established That whole Doctrine, which is to be, in Matters of Revelation, the Rule of our Faith: And to this Rule no humane Power can add any thing, nor diminish from it: For he that preacheth any Other Gospel, is accursed, Gal. 1, 9. Particular Churches require Mens Assent to, and Use of, certain forms of words; not as the Rule of their Faith,

.

## to Mr Nelfon.

Faith, but as prudential Means of Uniformity, and of preventing Disorder and Confusion among themselves. And when in such Forms there be (as there generally are) Expressions which at first sight look different ways, (as I have shown there are more Expressions in the Liturgy of the Church of England expressly for me in the present Controversy, than there are which seem to be against me;) it cannot be, but Men must be allowed to interpret what is obscure, by that which seems to them more plain and scriptural. Every Man that (for the sake of Peace and Order) affents to, or makes use of, any such Forms of Humane Appointment; is obliged to reconcile them with what appears to Him to be the Doctrine of Scripture, and take care to understand them in such a Sense only, as is consistent with That Doctrine: Otherwise he parts with his Christianity, for the sake of a Civil and political Religion. It becomes a sincere Man, (especially if he varies from Notions commonly received,) to Declare plainly in what Sense he understands any words of humane Institution; that his Inferiors and Equals may not be imposed upon by him, and that his Superiours may judge of such Declaration. That the Sense in which any Human Forms appear to a Mans self to be consistent with Scripture, and not the presumed Meaning of the Compilers,

33

34

## AREPLY

pilers, is to be the Rule and Measure of his understanding them; is both evident in Reason, (because otherwise every Humane Government makes a new Rule of Faith,) and is moreover by All Protestants agreed upon without controversy in Praetice. For, the Article in the Apostles Creed concerning Christs Descent into Hell, is now universally understood in a Sense probably different from what the Composers of the Creed intended. And the damnatory Clauses in the Athanasian Creed, are now by very Few understood in that Sense, which in all probability the Compiler of it in that very dark and ignorant Age designed to express. And the Procession of the Holy Ghost, set forth in the Nicene and Athanasian Creeds in one Sense; (not to mention Bp. Pearson's apologizing for the Greek Church,) is by Mr Bennet in his Explication of his own Sense concerning that point, shown to be Now understood by Many (without any Suipicion of Insincerity) in a different Sense. And the Doctrines of Predeestination and Original Sin, are at this day by all eminent Divines (after the example of Arch-Bishop Laud, and of the Learned Bishop Bull, whom you yourself have excellently vindicated,) understood in a Sense, which there is no appearance the Composers of the XXXIX Articles meant to teach; and which, there is all appearance the Compo-

## to $M^{r}$ Nelfon.

fers of the Homilies intended should not be taught. And That Article in the Nicene Creed, [of One Substance with the Father,] is now (through the Ambiguity of the Latin and English Translation,) by most Men taken much otherwise, than the Council intended it: For the greater part of Modern Christians, (if we may judge by the Writings of eminent Divines,) understand it (as if it had been παυτοῦς) to signify, of one INDIVIDUAL Substance with the Father; Whereas all learned Men know, that the Greek word *[ομοθέσας] never had any such Signification, and that the Council meant no such thing; but, of the same KIND of Substance with the Father: [Εὐκ ἐν διαίας τῶν πάλεος, so the Council of Nice explained themselves, though those words are now left out of the Creed;] The Son was, they said, γεννητείς οἱ παλείως, τῶν περὶ τῶν πάλεος. begotten of the Father, that is, from the Substance of the Father: And therefore was not (which Notion was then universally condemned) himself That individual Substance from which he was begotten. But their meaning was; he was produced, not from any Other Substance, (as Man was formed from the Dust of the Earth,) but, after an ineffable manner, from the Substance of the Father only. Which Sense of theirs, is Now generally mistaken. I see no possible Remedy for These

36

## A Commentary upon

Inconveniencies, but either plainly to declare (as the Church of Rome declared against the Beginners of the Reformation,) Humane Forms of speaking to be absolutely a Rule of Faith and Opinion, (which is indeed the fame with Mr Hobbs's Scheme of professedly abolishing all Religion;) or else, on the contrary, to indeavour perpetually by all just and reasonable means, to bring back words of obscure and uncertain Signification, to the more plain and intelligible Rule of Scripture: Which is holding fast the Form of sound Words, 2 Tim. 1, 13.

I Proceed now to the Consideration of your Anonymous Friend's Book; which consifing of Forty select Texts, I shall consider those Forty Texts in the same Order that He has done, after I have premised the two following general Observations.

First, I observe, (as before,) that if those Forty Texts could have been accommodated to His Notion, as I think not one of them can; yet it would not at all have followed that That Notion was right; because I have alleged more than three Hundred other Texts, besides those Forty, which do all of them clearly express the contrary. Now it has (you know) usually been observed,

## Forty Select Texts.

that Men of All Seets and Opinions whatsoever, are apt to plead Scripture in their own Defense. The true Meaning of which observation is, that Men of almost Any Opinion may pick out some single Texts, which, when taken by themselves, shall seem to look in Favour of That Opinion. But the Method I used, was to set forth in One View ALL the Texts that in any manner related to the matter in Question; and, by comparing them together, I showed how they might All be reconciled in one uniform and consistent Scheme. Your learned Friend has not taken That Method; And therefore, had there been more Texts alleged, and more favourable to him, than Any of them really are; yet This would not have Proved any thing.

Secondly; I observe, that from those Texts which he does allege, he does not so much as attempt to Prove his Notion to be true; but only indeavours to reconcile the Texts he alleges, to the Notion or Supposition which he had before laid down in his own Mind. To which, if All the Texts he alleges, could really be reconciled, as very Many of them cannot; yet still here would be Nothing proved. Besides: What That Notion or Supposition is, which he intends to establish; is very hard to guess. For sometimes he affirms the Person of the Son pag. 58 to act subordinately to the Person of the Father.

37

38

## A Commentary

ther, and denies him to be Self-existent, which (he fays) would be falling into Sabellianism : At other times he says, the Father and the Son are one and the same individual Being, or two persons in one and the same individual Being : And at another time he makes them All one and the same Person,

when he says, OF him as HE is Father, THROUGH him as HE is Son, TO him as HE is the Holy Ghost. All which Notions are inconsistent with, and contradictory to, each other. For if they be All one and the same individual Being, how can one and the same individual Being be subordinate to itself? And if HE who is Father, be also himself both Son and Holy Ghost; then it will follow, that there is no divine Nature of Christ at all, but that Christ was only a great Prophet, in whom God the Father manifested himself extraordinarily; Which is downright Socinianism. So that indeed it is impossible your Friend's Arguments should be conclusive to prove any thing, when he does not so much as know distinctly what 'tis he intends to prove. Neither can he allege, that these things are a Mystery: For the most evident Contradictions are not a whit more mysterious, than the most evident Truths.

But to proceed to his Texts in particular.

# N° 1.

MATT. XIX, 17. There is none Good, but One, that is, God.

Upon This Text I observed, that the word, One, according to the Nature of the Greek and Latin Languages, [εις, Unus,] must of necessity signify, One Person.

This, your learned Friend denies; and contends that it may as well or better signify, One Being. His Reafons are,

That the word, One, $[\varepsilon_{\mathrm{os}}]$ is Masculine, by reason of its relation to God, $[\Theta \varepsilon_{\mathrm{os}}]$

That it is no less fitted to represent Being, than Person; since they are neither of them Masculine.

That the fame word, $[\tilde{\epsilon}_{i s}, ]$ is used for One Thing, Gal. 3, 28, Ye are all One, $[\tilde{\epsilon}_{i s}]$ in Christ Jesus. Not One Person, for That (he says) is impossible; but, One Thing.

That the Vulgar Latin renders it fo, [Unum estis,] Ye are One thing or body.

40

## A Commentary

Lastly, that as the same words in Mar. 2,7,[ei μὲν εἰς, ὀθεὸς.] are translated, but God only; so here also they might better have been rendred, but God only or God alone.

And This (he says) puts an End to the Criticism of Personality founded upon the Term [ς, Umus,] One.

But the Observation is not so soon put an End to, as He conceives: For, his Conclusion runs much faster than his Premises. And were it ever so true, that the word $[\tilde{\epsilon}_{is}]$ could justly be rendred, One Being; it would still amount to the same thing: For One Being, when spoken of an Intelligent Agent, is the very same as One Person; Neither is there in Nature any other Notion of a Person, than as it signifies an Intelligent Agent or Intelligent Being: Whenever the word is used otherwise, no Man can tell what it signifies: And of What Use are words, when they have no Signification? Could therefore the word $[\tilde{\epsilon}_{is}]$ possibly have been rendred, as your Friend would have it, One Being; it would have availed him nothing, against any thing I had affirmed. But that it cannot possibly be so rendred, I still affirm; and I am willing to put it upon This short and plain issue: If there be Any One Passage in Any One Greek or Latin Writer in

on Matt. 19, 17.

in the World, accurate or inaccurate, wherein the word [ἐς, Unus] or any other masculine Adjective, placed absolutely without any antecedent Substantive, (as ἐδὲς and ἐς are placed in this Text,) can possibly signify either Thing or Being, or anything else besides Person; I will acknowledge my Explication of This Text to be erroneous. Your Friend might as well have affirmed that, in English, the word, Man, signifies a House or a Ship; or that any other word signifies any other thing whatsoever; as that the word, [ἐς, Unus] can signify Thing or Being. 'Tis certain that Arguments run very low indeed, when all Grammar is forced to be reversed in such a manner, as would make every Language a mere Babel of words, without Any determinate Signification. By Grammar, I mean, not only the artificial Rules of Grammarians, but the common and natural Sense of Mankind. As, when in English we use the word, He; 'tis impossible to mean thereby a Thing or a Being in general, but only an Intelligent Agent or Being, that is, a Person. Thus likewise in Greek, ὀν ὑντες, can signify nothing but Persons; παν ὑντας, nothing but Things. When the Platonists speak of God, considered merely as the Self-existent Being, abstract from the consideration of Life, Action and Government; they then call him ὂν, The Being: But when

41

42

## A Commentary

when it is, o'wv, He that exists, it then always represents him as an Agent or Person. And the same Observation holds universally true in all cases, without exception.

But the word [ἐς] One, may in this Text (he says) be Masculine, by reason of its relation to God, [ὁεῖς.] I answer; This cannot possibly be, because Then (the same being to be said of ἐς also,) the full construction would be, There is None Good, [὇δεις αγαθός, there is no God Good,] but One God, that is God: Whereas the plain construction is, There is no Person Good, but One Person, which is God. Had our Saviour meant to say, (as he might, very properly,) There is no Being Good, but One, that is, God; (which yet would not have denoted That sort of Goodness which is Moral, but that which is Natural or Metaphysical;) he must have expressed it thus, ἐδεν αγαθόν, εἰ μη εν, οὐ θεὸς: in like manner as the Philosopher says, ἐδεν γολαςιπυς, εἰ μη εν, οὐ ανρρωπος, [Nibil risibile est, nift Unum, nempe Homo;] Nothing is capable of Laughter, but only One thing, namely Man, or, the Species of Mankind. Had he said, ἐδειες γολαςιπυς εἰ μη εν, οὐ ανρρωπιο, [Nemo risibilis, nift Unus, nempe Homo;] No One [that is, No Person] is capable of Laughter, but One, namely Man, or Mankind; the Expression had been manifestly absurd:

on Matt. 19, 17.

abfurd: Which shows that these Two manners of Expression cannot possibly be confounded. And though Two different Sentences, may sometimes by Accident be nearly of the same import; (as, There is No Being Good, but One, that is God; or, There is No Person Good, but One, that is God;) yet the same Words that express One of these Sentences, cannot possibly express the Other.

Yes; the word [εις, Unus, One,] is no less fitted (he says) to represent Being, than Person; because Neither of them are Masculine. But This is a great mistake: For the word, Person, is always express both in Greek and Latin by the Masculine Adjective, and by It Only; there being no other word either in Greek or Latin, by which it ever was or can possibly be express. Persona, and დენისთი, and მორჩალი, are all of them words of a quite different Sense, and never used by any good Author (unless in very figurative Constructions) in this Signification. The Schoolmen have indeed, in their barbarous Language, made Hypostasis to signify Person; but what they mean by the word, they themselves know not.

But the same word $[\tilde{\epsilon}is,]$ is ufed (he says) for One Thing, Gal. 3, 28, Ye are all One $[\tilde{\epsilon}is]$ in Christ Jesus: "Not one Person; for That (he says) is impossible; but,

44

## A Commentary

but, One Thing". I answer: Literally speaking, the whole Number of Christ's Disciples can no more properly be called One Thing, than One Person; and figuratively speaking, they may as well be called One Person, as One Thing. And that St Paul does in this place intend to call the Church, in such a figurative manner of speaking, One Person; (besides that the word, ἐς, necessarily so signifies,) will appear from the following considerations. In the whole New Testament it is very usual to compare the Christian Church to a Body, whereof Christ is the Head; and particular Christians (faith the Apostle) are Members of his Body, of his Flesh, and of his Bones, Eph. 5, 30. In puruance of which elegant Similitude, the Church is often represented under the Notion of a Person, under the Character of the Spouse of Christ: 2 Cor. 11, 2, I have espoused you to one Husband, that I may present you a chaste Virgin [ὑμῶς παρὰν αγυπτ] to Christ: And Rev. 21, 9; 19, 7, I will show thee the Bride, the Lambs Wife;—The marriage of the Lamb is come, and his Wife has made herself ready; and to Her was granted, that she should be arrayed in fine Linen, clean and white; for the fine Linen is the Righteousness of Saints. Again; Eph. 2, 15, To make in himself, of twain, (viz. of Jews and

on Matt. 19, 17.

45

and Gentiles,) one new Man, ἐπὶ ἀγνόν ἀν-

Spw που. Thus likewise in the passage before us, Gal. 3, 27, Ye have put on Christ; There is neither Jew nor Greek, there is neither Bond nor Free, there is neither Male nor Female; for ye are all [ἐς,] considered as One Person in Christ Jesus. Your learned Friend did not at all perceive the Elegancy and Beauty of the Apostle's Expression in this Place. Consider the Church as One Thing, as a Congregation or Body of Men, and all these natural Relations still continue distinct; But consider it under the Notion of one Person, the Spouse of Christ, and then all these differences and distinctions vanish. By the same Figure of Speech, the Jewish Church is stiled in Scripture the Daughter of Sion: Nations and Empires are, in the Prophecy of Daniel, personated as Kings: The Church in the Revelation, under Persecution, is a Woman flying into the Wilderness: The False Church abusing the temporal Power of Princes, to persecute good Christians; is the Woman, the Whore, riding upon a Scarlet-coloured (or bloody) Beast. Deceivers and False-Teachers, are the False-Prophet, Rev. 19, 20, The Beast—and the False-Prophet were cast alive into a Lake of Fire: The Body of corrupt Christians in the latter Ages of the World, are The Man of Sin, 2 Th. 2, 3; and Anti-Christ; 2 Joh. 7, Many Deceivers are entred into the World.

46

## A Commentary

World, who confess not that Jesus Christ is come in the Flesh; This is [ο πλανῶς οὐ αντίχειδος] The Deceiver and The Anti-Christ. You see, Sir, your learned Friend concluded somewhat too fast, when he thought he had put an end to the Criticism of Personality founded upon the Term, εἰς, Unus.

Well; but the Vulgar Latin (he says) renders it, [Unum estis,] Ye are One Thing or Body. I acknowledge it: And 'tis a great confirmation of what I have said. For, the Reason why the Author of That Translation did not render it Unus, as Beza rightly does; was because he knew that Unus could not possibly signify any thing else than One Person: Which He not apprehending how it could stand in that place, nor perceiving the Beauty of St Paul's similitude; instead therefore of rendring the Apostles word which he understood not, he put in another of his own which he understood better, and thereby altered and quite spoiled the Apostles Sense.

Theodoret and Theophylae, were not Translators, but Commentators. They therefore might justly paraphrase the Apostles Expression, by the Phrase [ἐν σώμα] One Body. But though This be of the same import, as to the main of the Sense, with the Apostles word [εὐς] One Person; yet it does not therefore follow, that, in any possible

on Matt. 19, 17.

fible Construction, the word εἰς signifies ἐν σῶμα; any more than it follows that the word Paulus signifies in Latin an Apostle, because a Commentator may happen (without spoiling the Senfe) to put the word Apostle instead of Paul.

Lastly, because the same words [εἰ μὴ εἶς, οἱ θεοὺς.] are in Mar. 2, 7, paraphrastically rendred, but God only; therefore here also (he says) they might better have been rendred after the same manner. But, in right reasoning, just the contrary is true. Because these words in the place before us are rendred, not paraphrastically, but literally and exactly; therefore in That other passage of St Mark, they ought to have been rendred so likewise.

I add only, (though I depend not on Authorities,) that Clemens Alexandrinus, (when he paraphraseth, One that is God, by the words, ο παπάρ μυθ οὐν ποιῶς ἀεανοις, My Father which is in Heaven;) and Origen, (when he recites the words thus, εἰ μὴ εἰς, ο θεός ο παπάρ, there is none Good, but One, which is God, even the Father;) and Novatian, and Athanasius himself, interpret This Text exactly as I do. As I have shown in my Scripture-Doctrine, No 340. To which may be further added Irenæus, who cites the words thus, εἰς ἐσυν αγαχος, ο Γα-πάρ ο νοῖς ἀεανοις. There is One that is Good, even the Father which is in Heaven, lib.

## $\mathrm{N}^{0}$ 2.

MARK XII, 29. The first of all the Commandments, is; Hear, O Israel, the Lord our God is one Lord. [or, the Lord our God, even the Lord, is One.]

The only Observation your learned Friend makes upon This Text, is; that God is here said to be One, only in opposition to Idols or the False Gods of the Nations. Be it so: The Question then remains, Who That God is, of whom this is spoken. The Jews to be sure, when these words were spoken by Moses, could understand them of no other than of the Almighty Father, the Creator and Governor of all things: And the natural Apprehension, or common Sense of Mankind, obviously leads all Men to understand them after the same manner. In the New

## on Mark 12, 29.

New Testament, whenever these words are referred to, they are still always understood in the same sense. Our Lord himself, speaking of his Father, calls him the Only True God, Joh. 17, 3. And St. Paul, after One Spirit, and One Lord, mentioning in the last and highest place One God, calls Him [viz. That One God,] The Father of All, who is above all, Eph. 4, 6. And when he had said, that to Us Christians there is but One God; he adds, by way of Explication, The Father, of whom are all things, 1 Cor. 8, 6. And in more than 300 other places of the New Testament, the word, God, is by the construction of the words with which it is joyned, of necessity confined to the Person of the Father singly: As ap pears in the Passages themselves, collected in my Scripture-doctrine, Part I, Ch. I, Sect. 2. And Athanasius himself, expressly interprets the words of the Text now before us, to be meant of God the Father; in two passages cited in my Scripture-doctrine, Part I, No. 2. And not only the same Author in other places, but Clemens Romanus also, and Ignatius, and Justin, and Irenaeus, and Clemens Alexandrinus, and Tertullian, and Origen, and Novatian, and Eusebius, and Hilary, (if the matter was to be decided by Authority,) speak after the same manner, in the passages which I have cited from them in my Scripture-doctrine, Part D II,

49

50

## A Commentary

II, § 9. Well; But if This be so, is Christ then excluded also among the False Gods? I answer: When God the Father is thus stiled The One God; Idols, in opposition to him, are excluded hereby from being Gods at all; and the Son, in subordination to him, is excluded, not from being truly God, but from being That Person, That Supreme, Independent, Self-existent Governour of All, who, upon these accounts, is stiled, by way of Eminence, the One and Only God, while the Son is nevertheless, by communication of divine Power and Dominion from the Father, really and truly God. This is very consistent and intelligible: And the Passages your Friend cites out of Athanasius, must either thus be reconciled with the express words which I had before cited from him, wherein he interprets these Texts exactly as I do; or else he unavoidably contradicts himself. Irenaeus, as I have already shown, explains this whole matly stiled the Only God and Lord; (unlefs the Greek perhaps signified, that He who

- already shown, explains this whole matter at large, in his whole Sixth Chapter of his Third Book, with as much accuracy, as if he had written on purpose against your

Ira ut is quidem, qui omnia fecerit, cum Verbo suo,

jusfè dicatur Deus & Domi-

zua Solus. lib. 3. cap. 8.

Friends unintelligible Notion. And therefore, when he fays in another place, that He who made all things, is together with his Word, justly stiled the Only God and Lord; (unlefs the Greek perhaps signified, that He who

made

## on Mark 12, 32.

made all things by his Word, is justly stiled the Only God and Lord; in like manner as he had been showing a little before in That same Chapter, that He who is the God over All, made all things by [Chrift] his word;) 'tis evident his Meaning, to speak consistently with Himself, must be, (not such a Confusion of Persons as your Friend introduces, but) what he explicitly and copiously declares in the fore-cited * Sixth Chapter of the same Book, and in numerous other places; viz. that the Son is truly God and Lord, by having RECIEVED Dominion over the whole Creation, from the Father, who being of Himself absolute Lord of All, is by way of Eminence the One and Only God. But still you will always remember, that the Question must finally be decided, not by Humane Authority, but by the Words of Scripture.

## $\mathrm{N}^{\circ}$ 3.

MARK XII, 32. There is One God, and there is None other but He.

It cannot be doubted but the Scribe, when he fpake these words, meant the One Supreme Governour of the Universe, even Him who was known to the Jews by the D2 Title

51

* See a-
bove, pag;
11.

In the Scripture doctrine,
N° 3. In
the Answer, pagi
6.

52

## A Commentary

Title of the Creator of Heaven and Earth, and who is declared to us Christians by the Title of the God and Father of our Lord Jesus Christ. That the Writers of the New Testament did not alter the known Signification of the words, One God; I have shown under the fore-going Head. That the Christian Writers in all Antiquity understood the words, one God, to signify the Father; I have shown by a very large Collection of Testimonies, in my Scripture-doctrine, Part II, § 9. Your Learned Friend Himself acknowledges in this very place, that the Father Alone is ['Aumbe] He who derives his Being and Godhead from no Cause; and consequently that He alone is, in that sense, the One God. But however, the Terms One God (he says) are used in no such Meaning in This Text. And Why not in this Text? Why, because the words are used here (he says) in opposition only to False Gods, and therefore ought not to be confined to the Father Alone, so as to exclude the Son. So as to exclude him from what? From being He who alone derives his Being and Godhead from no Cause, your Friend expressly allows that He is always excluded: And God forbid that I should ever argue, for excluding him from being God in any other respect. He is really, and truly God (which no false Gods are,) by deriving real and true Divinity from the

## on Mark 12, 32.

the ineffable Power of Him, who Alone has an unoriginated Being and Godhead. Wher-in then does your Learned Friend and I differ? I think, in This only; that, after allowing a clear distinction, he still contends for such an unintelligible way of speaking, as either necessarily introduces, or at least cannot be vindicated from introducing, a Confusion of Persons.

As to his Citations from the Fathers upon this Head; though I do not think myself at all concerned to reconcile to each other the different ways of speaking found in the Fathers; yet I cannot but desire the Reader to compare the Few Places here cited by your Friend out of Three Fathers, with ten times the Number of places cited by Me out of the Same and many More Fathers, in my Scripture-doctrine, Part II, § 9. By which it will appear, that Much the greater Number of Ancient Writers declare themselves with the greatest possible distinctness to have understood This and the like Texts, exactly as I do; and that Those who speak more confusedly, (as when Tertullian says, Deus se Unicum, fed cum Filio, oftendit; God declares himself to be One only, but yet together with his Son;) did not mean, as your learned Friend does, [Deum & Filium esse unicum,] that God and the Son of God were One Individual; but, that God was so One, [ut tamen Filium ba-beret,]

53

## 54

## A Commentary

beret,] that yet nevertheless it was to be maintained, against Jews and Heathens, that he had a Son to whom he had communicated true Divine Dominion over the whole Creation. Tertullian himself, (though, as to the metaphysical Point, he introduced indeed a Confused Notion of the Son's being a PART of the Father's Substance, in like manner as a Branch is Part of a Tree;) yet, as to the present Question, he expressly asserts God to be One, in the same Sense that the Monarch of a Country is the One and Only King, notwithstanding that he be supposed to have an Only Son, whom he has receiv'd into the Whole Administration of the Government with himself; See Scripture-doctrine, pag. 334. Novatian, out of whom your Friend here cites one single Sentence, does every where so expressly assert all that I contend for; that a Reader, who pleases to peruse his Book, will be amazed to find the Whole of it, from the Beginning to the End, to be written on purpose to establish the very Notion I am pleading for. Athanasius himself understands the Words One God, in the Text before us, to be meant of the Person of the Father; though he adds, (and so far indeed very rightly,) that This is not to be understood [εἰς αναμεν τὸ γὰρ] so as to destroy the Divinity of the Son. Lastly, the Passage of Origen, (one Scrap of which

your

## on Mark 12, 32.

your Friend cites, and thus translates, We worship One God, who is both Father and Son;) is itself, when the Whole of it appears together, a large Explication what He thought the Sense of the Church to have been in His days concerning this matter :

If any one (fays he) is disturbed at these Expressions, as if we favoured the Opinion of Those [the Sabellians] who deny the Father and the Son to be Two distinct Subsistencies; let him consider That Text (Aets 4, 32,) All that believed, were of One Heart and of One Soul; and then He will understand This, I and my Father are One Thing. We serve therefore, in the Sense I have

now explained, One God, the Father, and the Son. We worship the Father of the Truth, and [also] the Son who is the Truth; being indeed Two things in Subsistence ; but in Agreement and Consent and Sameness of Will, they are One. There are in My Book Many Citations of Passages, wherein Authors expressly grant (and intended to grant) what perhaps is very difficult to reconcile with some of their own Conclusions : And in citing such Passages, there is (as I have largely shown) no unfairness, but D4 the

## $\mathrm{N}^{\circ}$ 4.

JO H. XVII, 3. That they might know Thee, the Only True God; and Jesus Christ, whom thou hast sent.

These words, it hath been supposed, may be understood Two ways.

Either Thus : That they might know Thee, the Only True God ; and [that they might know] Jesus Christ, whom thou hast sent.

Or Thus: That they might know Thee, the Only True God; and Jesus Christ whom thou hast sent,[the Only True God also.]

The former is, I think, the True Sense of the words; your learned Friend thinks the latter to be so.

The

on Joh. 17, 3.

57

The Reasons for My Opinion, are:

1. That the obvious and natural Construction of the words, is This : That they might know Thee, [who art] the Only True God; and [that they might also know] Jesus Christ, [who is] He whom thou hast sent, [viz. the True Messiah.] Especially, if it be observed how the latter words are disposed in the Greek ; [ὑν απελεγόμενος, Ιποστήρειαν, and Him whom thou hast sent, even Jesus Christ.] The other Interpretation of the words, is so forced and unnatural; that an unprejudiced Reader will easily be determined, even by That consideration alone, to reject it.

2. In each of the other places of the New Testament, where the True God is mentioned, it signifies, by way of Eminence, the Person of the Father. In 1 Thess. 1,9,'tis evidently and indisputably so, because expressly contradistinguished from the Person of the Son in the very words of the Text it self: Returned to God from Idols, to serve the Living and True God, and to wait for His Son from Heaven. The other passage, 1 Job. 5, 20, shall be considered by and by, in its proper place. In like manner, the other places of Scripture where the One or Only God is mentioned, do All of them really, and some of them most evidently, mean thereby the Person of the Father singly. Thus

58

See Scripture-doctrine, pag. 4. & 254.

## A Commentary

Thus 1 Cor. 8, 6, To Us there is but One God, the Father; and One Lord, Jesus Christ. And Eph. 4; 4, 5, 6, One Spirit,—One Lord,—One God and Father of all, who is above all &c. And Jude 4, The Only Lord God, and our Lord Jesus Christ.

3. All the Writers in the Three First Centuries, (if the Reader thinks fit to be determined by Authorities,) whenever they refer to the Text before us, understand it as I do. Your Friend mentions two of these Writers, Origen and Novatian. Origen, he yields me : and Novatian interprets the Text just as I do, in those very words which your Friend cites as an Argument to the contrary.

4. Many even of the Later Fathers also, understand this Text, as I do; Which nothing but the mere Evidence of the thing itself, could have obliged them to. Athanasius thus; οὐτε μόνον λέγεται ο πατήρ ἔτους,

When the Father is stiled the Only God, &c.

Τον αλληδινόν ἔουν, Τον τῆς χειτῶν πατήρα,

The True God, even the Father of Christ,

&c. Μόνον ἔουν αλληδή. — τις δ' ἔν εἰσίν ἔτος,

ἀλλά η οὐ τεχιεύε πάτηρ; The Only True God,—Who is He but the Father of Christ?

Τον ενα πὸ μόνον αλληδινόν ἔουν.—λείω γὰρ τῆς χειτῶν πατήρα, The One and Only True God, I mean the Father of Christ,

&c.

## on John 17, 3.

&c. "Οπὸ σαρκωθθείς πιέμος ὑθεός πημῶν 'Iστως χελιάς ο διατηρ σήμεν, ἐδὰ' (ως διεθροί φαιεν) ο μονὴ Θεοὺς, απασου μαρτυρὰσαν αὐ τέλεια χαραφὴ, That Jesus Christ our Lord and God incarnate, is not the Father; and that He is not (as the Sabellians would have it,) That Only God; the Holy Scriptures everywhere testify. Also Hilary thus; Non Patri adimitur, quod Deus Unus fit, quia & Filius Deus fit; ob id Unus Deus, quia ex se Deus: The Son's being God, does not hinder the Father from being the One God; For He is therefore the One God, because He is Self-existent God.

5. The Learned Bishop Pearson, (Expof on the Creed, pag. 40;) and the Learned Bishop Bull, (Defenf. Sect. 4, cap. 1, §. 2,) both of them expressly acknowledge these words, The Only True God, to be meant, in This Text, of the Father only, by way of supreme Eminence, in contradistinction to the Son who was sent by him.

The Reasons your Learned Friend alleges for understanding this Text the other way, are:

1. That the Term, Only, does not always exclude every thing else but the Subject to which it is applied.

2. That the Doctrine the Text contains according to His Interpretation of it, is found

59

pag. 11.

p.1g. 12.

## 60

## A Commentary

found evidently in another place of Scripture, viz. 1 fob. 5, 20.

Pag. 13 &
14.

3. That the word, Only, is added merely in opposition to Idols and false Gods.

4. That several Fathers understand the Words in His sense : That Athanafius in other places explains himself, as to his Meaning in the places I cited from him : And that Origen's sense, which he acknowledges to be the same with mine, may be as difficult to be proved out of Scripture, as his critical Remark, that the Article [o] prefixed to $[ \Theta \epsilon \omega s, ]$ appropriates the Name to God the Father.

Now to these Reasons, I reply :

1. To deny that the Term, Only, always excludes every thing else, but the Subject it is applied to; is to deny that Words have any Signification. For if the word, Only or Alone, does not appropriate; there is no word in any Language, which can appropriate any thing to any Subject.

But He alleges an Instance : " Ecclus. 24, 5, Wisdom says of herself, I alone compassed the Circuit of Heaven : Which surely, says be, does not exclude the Fa-

"ther;——And if the Term, Only, does

" not exclude the Father, we cannot ne-

"ceffirily infer that it does the Son". But now the Difference is This: The Wisdom of God the Father, does not exclude God

God

## on Joh. 17, 3.

God the Father, because it is itself nothing but an Attribute of God the Father; and to say that the Wisdom of God does any thing, is only saying in other words that God does it himself. But affirming any thing to belong to One Person Alone, is of necessity denying that same thing to belong to any Other Person in the same sense. Whatever therefore is appropriated to the Father alone, is of necessity denied to belong to the Son in that same sense, in which it is appropriated to the Father. Unless it be declared (according to the Sabellian Notion, which upon the Whole amounts to the very same with Socinianism;) that the Son is not a real Person, but a mere Attribute of the Father. Which Opinion your Friend disclaims, though his Reasoning here necessarily supposes it.

2. That the words, This is the True God, 1 Job. 5, 20, are not spoken of the Son; I shall show by and by, in its proper place, $N^{o}$ 14.

3. That the word, Only, is added merely in opposition to Idols or False Gods, is a great Mistake. For the word, Only, of necessity always appropriates somewhat to the Subject it is applied to, not only exclusive of every thing which That Subject is opposed to, but also of every thing which That Subject is contradistinguished from. The Son, is not (as False Gods are) opposite

61

to

62

## A Commentary

to the Father, but contradistinguished from him: And when the Father is, in this Prayer of our Saviour, stiled the Only True God; this Title is appropriated to him not only because Idols are False Gods, but because He only is the Subject whom our Saviour was speaking of in these Words. In that passage of St Paul, I Thess. 1, 9, Te turned to God from Idols, to serve the Living and True God, and to wait for His Son from Heaven; the Father is stiled the Living and True God, not only in opposition to Idols which are No Gods, but expressly also in contradistinction to his Son expected from Heaven; who is True God by communication of Divinity from the Father, while at the same time the Father is therefore (as Hilary and most other Fathers express it) justly stiled by way of eminence the One and Only True God, as having Alone his Divinity (or Supreme Dominion) of Himself absolute and underived.

4. As to Authorities; your Friend mentions none in the First Three Centuries, but Origen, whom he gives up; and Novatian, whose words prove just the contrary to what he cites them for: We must believe, faith That Father, in the Lord, the Only True God; and, by consequence, in Jesus Christ whom He [viz. the Only True God] bath sent. Novatian does indeed add, that our Lord would

## on John 17, 3.

would not have added the latter part of the words, unless he had expected that He himself also should have been believed to be God. But Novatian's meaning herein is, that Christ is God by having received True Divine Dominion over the whole Creation, from and in Subordination to the Father. For the Design of his whole Book from the Beginning to the End, is to prove this very thing, and in the very same manner as I have explained it. The Reader, when he peruses Novatian's Book, will be furprized to find how exceeding clearly and distinctly he explains this whole matter.

The Passages your Friend cites out of Hilary, I desire the Reader to compare with That which I have cited just above, [pag. 59,] out of the fame Author.

The Passages I cited out of Athanafius, your Friend desires the Reader to compare with some other passages of the same Author: Which I also desire him to do; And if he cannot reconcile these with the others which I first cited, he will then observe that those others are so much the stronger Evidences of what I alleged them for, as being the Concessions of a person who was himself of a different Opinion.

Nazianzen's Argument is this: If the former part of the words [that they might know Thee the Only True God,] had been meant of the Father Only; [ανπδίρητο,]

63

if

64

## A Commentary

if it had been meant of him in contradistinction to the Son; then there would not have been added these latter words, [and Jesus Christ whom thou hast sent.] Where the Force of This Argument lies, I confess I understand not, but leave it to be considered by the Reader. One would naturally think, that these latter words were added for That very Reason, for which the good Father supposed they ought not to have been added. For, taking the True God to be meant in opposition to Idols or False Gods, (as Nazianzen explains it;) then our Saviour's prayer is, that Men forsaking all Idols and False Gods, may know The Only True God, and Him who was Truly sent forth from That Only True God.

Ambrose's Argument is, that the Conjunction [And,] never separates things, but joins them together. By the same Argument, when the Scripture says that the people feared the Lord AND Samuel, it would follow that the Lord and Samuel were one and the same Individual. Were I an Admirer of Ambrose, I would certainly have forborn citing out of him so ridiculous an Argument.

Origen, your Learned Friend allows to be clearly on my side. His words I shall here repeat, because I am willing to take This opportunity of correcting a Mistake, which (though your Friend was so kind as not

on John 17, 3.

65

not to take notice of it) I was guilty of in my Translation of That passage. Hence

the Father :) This scruple, I say, of many Pious persons, may thus be solved. ## A Commentary

Divine Person. It is not possible that Any Words should express the Sense of the Primitive Church more fully, more clearly, more distinctly, than these do. Your Friend therefore fairly gives up Origen, and makes only the following Reply. Perhaps (says he) it may be as difficult to prove This sense of the words, [viz. that The only true God signifies the Father, as being αὐτῶν God unoriginated,] out of Scripture, (which the Doctor professes to be his Rule in these Matters;) as it is to make good the critical Remark of the same Father [Origen,] that the Article [ο] prefixed to the word [Θεός,] appropriates the Name to God the Father; when it is evident, that the Son is called God with the same Article, by his Disciple Thomas, Joh. 20, 28, ο' Θεός μὲν And by St Paul also in his Epistle to the Hebrews, ch. 1, 8, which is taken from Ps. 45, 6: To say nothing of Writers Older than Origen; and even of Origen himself, who destroys his Criticism in his own Writings, as will appear from One Instance that may serve for others, μεπερφροφή ο' Θεός πμων, which he uses of the Son, lib. 4, contr. Celfum. All This, is exceeding strange indeed. To prove out of Scripture, what the Sense of the words in question is, was what I indeavoured in my Scripture-doctrine. To That, it has been answered, that we ought also to take in the Sense of the Primitive

on Joh. 17, 3.

67

mitive Church. What the Sense of the Primitive Church was, is with all possible Accuracy here described by Origen. And to This, it is answered, that Perhaps it may be difficult to prove this Sense out of Scripture. And how difficult? why, as difficult as to make good the critical Remark of † Origen, that the Name [o' Θεός] is appropriated to God the Father. I made no Use of that critical Remark, in My Book; and yet your learned Friend is very unfortunate, in asserting that That Remark is evidently false. 'Tis not good to be confident about an Universal Negative, and therefore I will not presume to say that o' θεός was never used by any Writer concerning any other than God the Father: But This I can affirm, that, as far as I have been able to observe, I could never find That Title given absolutely to * any Other person, either in the Scripture, or in Any Writer before Origen, or in Origen himself, or in Any Writer after Origen. All Men that understand Language, know that o' θεός μυλ, is quite a different thing from o' θεός absolutely. St Thomas's o' θεός μυλ, is spoken of Him, whose God and Father is o' θεός. St Paul's o' θεós, is the Vocative Case from θεός. And Origen's o' θεός ἡμῶν, is just as different from o' θεός absolutely, as was St Thomas's o' θεός μυλ. 'O θεός absolutely, is the same Remark is largely insisted on by Fusebius, de Ecclesiast. Theol. lib. 2, cap. 17, commenting upon Job. 1, 1.

* In Clemens Alexandrinus it is given to the Father and Son considered together, as God and his Word; in such a manner as to make them but One and the same person: wherein That Father is not consistent with himself.

## A Commentary

the same as o' παντοκεῖπωρ. or o' διθίπη πάντων θεὺς. Wherefore He who is absolutely o' θεὺς, may very well be, and often is called, θεὺς : But it does not therefore follow, that He who is truly θεὺς, may on the other side be as properly stiled o' θεὺς. The Passage which comes nearest it, is one omitted by your Friend, Rom. 9, 5, o' υν διθίπη πάντων θεὺς : which, if allowed to be certainly spoken of Christ, yet is not the same as if the Apostle had said, o' υν ο' διθίπη πάντων θεὺς.
