## $\mathrm{N}^{\circ}$ 5.

1 C O R. VIII; 4, 5, 6. We know that an Idol is nothing in the World, and that there is None other God but One. For though there be that are called Gods many and Lords many ; to Us there is but One God, [viz.] the Father, of whom are all things and we in him; and One Lord, [viz.] Jesus Christ, by whom are all things, and we by Him.

These words are so express and full a declaration of God the Father's being by way of Eminence the One God, that there are no possible words by which it could have been afferted more explicitly. Had the

on 1 Cor. 8; 4, 5, 6.

the Apostle said only, To Us there is but One God, and one Lord Jesus Christ; there had really, according to the Analogy of Scripture, been no Ambiguity: But when he adds by way of Interpretation, as it were on purpose to prevent all possibility of mistake, there is but One God, the FATHER; and one Lord, Jesus Christ; it is a very furprizing thing, to find a Learned and Sincere Man indeavouring to put another Sense upon the words, One God; different from That which the Apostle in such express words declares himself to mean.

But (fays your learned Friend) it is plain here, that the Unity of God is affirmed in opposition to Idols. Very true: But it is plain also, that the God, whose Unity is here so affirmed, is by the Apostle expressly declared to be the Father.

But (adds your Friend,) though the Father be said to be the One God, yet this is not spoken to the exclusion of the Son, who has been proved to be comprehended in the One God. That the Father's being by way of Eminence the One God, does not exclude the Son from being truly God by true communication of Divinity from that one unoriginated God the Father, I readily acknowledge. But to say that the One God the Father is the Son also, and not the Father only; or that the Son is not excluded from E 3 being

69

pag. 14.

pag. 15.

70

## A Commentary

being That One God, which is the Person of the Father; this is directly affirming that Two Persons are one and the same Person; Which is the utmost Confusion.

But (fays your Friend further.) the Son is comprehended in the One God the Father, as He is the Word: But forasmuch as the Son is not only God, but God and Man consisting of two Natures; and, by reason of That Union, cannot be comprehended, as to his whole Person, under any One of them; therefore does he seem to be mentioned after God the Father, as a Being distinct from pure Deity, such as the Father is; though his Superior Nature, or God the Word, is comprehended in it. I answer: Not only God the Father, but God the Father and Son and Holy Ghost, must needs by your Friend be allowed to be Pure Deity: And consequently, if the Apostle had here mentioned Jesus Christ in the second place (as your Friend supposes) only upon Account of his being (as God-man) a Being distinct from pure Deity; he must have said, not, One God, the Father; but One God, the Father and Son and Holy Ghost; and one Lord (or God-man) Jesus Christ. Besides; When your Friend affirms the Son to be comprehended (in the Sense we are now speaking of) in the One God the Father, as He is the Word; What does This import, but

on I Cor. 8; 4, 5, 6.

but either that the word Father signifies both Father and Son, (which is to affirm that One Person is Two Persons ;) or else that he supposes the Son, because he is called The Word, to be only an Attribute or Power of the Father, and not any Perfon at all?

Authorities for his Interpretation of this Text, your Friend alleges None : Because it is notorious that All Antiquity understood this Text in My Sense, and not in His. One only passage he cites out of Irenæus, which in the Latin Translation (the original Greek being lost) has some Obscurity in it. There is One God the Father, (says Irenæus,) who is above all, and through

all, and in all : Above all, is the Father ; through all, is the Word ; and in all of us, is the Spirit : For there is One Father, who is above

all, and through all, and in us all. That this is the True Rendring of the words, (and not, as your Friend translates them above all, as Father; through all, as Word; and in all of us, as Spirit;) appears from the

words which follow in the very fame Chapter ; There is (fays he) One God the Father, who is Above All;

and One Word of God, which is Through E4 All :

## N° 6.

ACTS V; 3, 4. ——to Lie to the Holy Ghost.——Thou hast not lied unto Men, but unto God.

The plain and obvious meaning of these words, is; that attempting to deceive Inspired Persons, was not barely the Crime of attempting to deceive Men, but was in effect an attempting to deceive God himself, by whose Holy Spirit those Men were inspired. This I have shown at large in my

Scripture-doctrine; And Athanasius himself (as I there observed) interprets the Text in the same manner : He that lied (faith he) to the Holy Ghost, lied to GOD who dwelleth in Men by his Spirit : For where the Spirit of God is, there is God [himself.] For hereby, faith the

Apostle, we know that GOD dwelleth in us, because he bath given us of His Spirit. Neither can your learned Friend allege Any Author, from the time the Text was written, till after Athanafius's days, that understood

## A Commentary

understood this Text in any other Sense. Why then does he seek for a New Interpretation, unsupported either by Scripture or Antiquity? If the Term God, says he, can be applied to the Holy Ghost, [for Proof whereof he refers to five Texts, in not one of which the Term is so applied;] why should there be so much pains taken to prove it to belong to the Father, who is not mentioned in the Context? I answer: It requires no great Pains or Force, to understand the word, God, in this Text, as signifying the Father; because in All other Texts the same word always signifies the Father, and in No other Text ever signifies the Holy Ghost. The word occurs in the New Testament above One Thousand times, and in all those passages, (excepting three or four in which it is applied to the Son,) it always signifies the Father, (as I have shown demonstratively in my Scripture-doctrine,) and is in no place applied to the Holy Ghost. In what Sense, and with what Propriety of Speech, the Holy Spirit may, or may not, be stiled God, (which your Friend indeavours to intermix in his Argument,) is not here the Question: but whether in fact he is, or is not, so stiled in This or in any other Text of Scripture. The Holy Ghost, is the Holy Spirit of God; that is to say, the Holy Spirit of the Father: And if the word, God, placed absolutely

## on Acts 5;3,4.

lutely as in this Text, signified, not the Father, but either the Holy Spirit or the whole Three Persons; it would follow that the Holy Spirit of God, was the Holy Spirit of Himself; which is a manifest Absurdity. The only Argument your Friend urges, why the word, God, in this Text, should be meant of the Holy Ghost, and not of the Father; is This very obscure one: That if the word God here signified the Father, then it would follow that the Holy Ghost acted by the Authority of the Father; which since the Apostles themselves also did, it would follow that They might have been stiled the Objects of the Lie, as well as the Holy Ghost; the Person of the Spirit being as little affected with the Lie spoken, in comparison of the Authority affronted in Him, as the Persons of the Apostles were affected by it in comparison of the Authority affronted in Them; Neither could a Lie against the Holy Ghost, have been said comparatively to be none against Men, if the Holy Ghost were not He who is here stiled God. I am sure I have represented his Argument more clear and strong, than the Author himself represented it; and yet at the best it is a very obscure one. The Strength of it indeed, is no more than this: If the Holy Ghost be at all subordinate to the Father, (as the Scripture every where declares he is sent and given by Him.)

Him,) then He is nothing at all superiour to Men. The Reader, I trust, will not stand in need of any Help, to answer this Argument.

G A L. IV, 8. Te did Service unto them which by Nature are no Gods, [τοῦς μὲν φύσει δοσια θεοῖς, or, as it is in the Alexandrian MS, τοῦς φύσει μὲν δοσια θεοῖς.]

Of these words I proposed the Two following Expositions.

1. Unto Gods, which have no Being in Nature, or which in Nature [in reality] have no Being.

2. Unto Beings, which by their Nature are not capable of having any divine Power or Authority: Thus, Acts 19, 26, they be no Gods; that is, they have none of that Authority and Dominion over you, which you ascribe to them.

Of the latter of these Expositions, your Learned Friend takes not the least Notice. I suppose, he overlooked it, as not being in its proper place, but in the Addenda, pag. 1; Though he elsewhere takes Notice of something else mentioned in That Page.

Against

on Gal. 4, 8.

77

Against the former of them, He argues as follows :

1. That the Two most Antient Versions, the Vulgar and the Syriac, agree with our received Translation of the words.

2. That in St Paul's style of Writing, $\varphi v \sigma s$ is no where used for Nature in general, or the System of Natural Beings.

3. That, had φύσας been used by him in that general Notion, yet, according to the Analogy of his Style in other places, he would rather have said ἐν φύσας, than φύσαι alone, if he had meant in Nature and not by Nature.

4. That if φύσθ had signified in Nature, yet 'tis probable the Apostle would rather have said τοῖς μὲν ἐσπαι alone, than τοῖς φύσει μὲν ἐσπαι.

5. That $\varphi \iota \upsilon \sigma \epsilon$ is often used by the same Apostle in other places, to signify by Nature, not in Nature.

All these Criticisms of your Learned Friend, (which I have here fairly set before my Reader,) I acknowledge to be Right; And they are, by much, the most Scholarlike Observations in his Whole Book.

Though therefore the words (notwithstanding all this) may possibly signify, having no Being in Nature; yet, because By Nature is in itself the more natural Signification

78

## A Commentary

Addenda
to Script-
ture-doc-
trine, pag.

fication of the word, and also more agreeable to the Analogy of St Paul's style; I acknowledge it to be more probable, that That is the True Rendring.

The Question then is, what is the Meaning of the Phrase, By Nature. Them which by Nature are no Gods.

And This, in my Latter Exposition of the Words, (which your Friend takes no notice of at all,) is as much as to say, They have by their Nature none of that Divine Authority or Dominion over You, which you vainly ascribe to them.

Either This is their True Meaning ; or else the Words, no Gods by Nature, must signify Beings which in their metaphysical and essential Nature are not Supreme, Selfexistent and Independent.

Now that the Word [φινος] in Scripture, does not signify the Nature of things in the metaphysical or essential, but only in the vulgar and natural, the moral or political Sense of the Word; that is, the true State and Condition of things, their Capacities and Powers, their Circumstances and Qualifications, the Reason of things, and sometimes even Customs only; appears from the Use of the same Word, 1 Cor. 11, 14, Doth not even Nature itself teach you, that if a Man bath long Hair, it is a shame unto him? & Rom. 2, 14, do by Nature the things contained

on Gal. 4,8.

contained in the Law; & Rom. 1, 26, That which is against Nature; & Rom. 2, 27, Uncircumcision which is by Nature; & Rom. 11, 24, which is wild by Nature; & 2 Pet. 1, 4, you might be Partakers of the divine Nature; & Gal. 2, 15, We who are Jews by Nature; And Ephes. 2, 3, We were by Nature the Children of Wrath: Not by our original Nature or ESSENCE; God forbid: But, (as that Text itself expressly explains it.) by the Then present CIRCUMSTANCES of our Nature, the habitual Wickedness and Corruption of Mens Manners before their Conversion to Christianity. And thus likewise in the present Passage; Them which by their Nature, (that is, which in the Nature and Reason and Truth of Things,) are no Gods; have no divine, no invisible Authority or Dominion over you; have nothing of That Nature, which you vainly ascribe to them; nothing of That Dominion and Power, which the Worship you pay them supposes them to have. And This is the True Notion of Idolatry: viz. the ascribing to any Being, real or imaginary, such invisible Dominion or Power, and consequently such Worship and Honour, as does not belong to it. For, Worship being nothing else, but the Acknowledgment or Payment of due Honour, correspondent to the True Dominion and Dignity of the Person to whom it is paid; (for All

79

## A Commentary

All Worship or Honour is Personal, paid not to a Metaphysical Substance, but to an Intelligent Agent ;) it follows manifestly, that so far as Dominion and Power and Authority is ascribed to any Being, more than it really has; so far That Being is merely [εἰδω]ov,] an Idol, a Nothing in the World, i Cor. 8, 4, a mere Fiction of the Imagination. When therefore St Paul here charges the * Heathens, (or perhaps, as the Context seems to suppose, corrupt Jews and Worshippers of Angels,) with doing Service to Them which by Nature are no Gods; 'tis evident his Argument is not, that the Gods they worshipped were not absolutely Supreme. Self-existent, Independent Beings ;

- If the Apostle speaks here concerning Heathens, it must be observed, in order to the right understanding of the Context, that this Discourse is directed to such New Converts from among the Heathen, as were in danger of being corrupted by Judaizing Christians, to think Circumcision and other Jew with Observances still necessary to Salvation under the Gospel.

(for They did not pretend This, nor does the Word ever signifie any such thing;) but that, neglecting the Worship of the True God, they worshipped Gods who had really nothing of That Nature which was ascribed to them; none of that Authority or Dominion, either Supreme or Subordinate, which These Men (like the Church of Rome Now in the case of their Saint-Worship,) imagined them to have. For they were Gods, which either had no Being at all; Fictions, and mere imaginary Deities: Or

## on Heb. 3; 3, 4, 5, 6.

Or else, at best, they were [διαμύνα] Beings which in reality were not of That Nature which their Worshippers pretended them to be, induced or capable of being induced with divine invisible Dominion and Authority over Men. Thus fer. 5, 7, Them that are no Gods; and Acts 19, 26, They be no Gods; that is, have not and cannot have any of That divine Authority and Dominion over you, which you foolishly and wickedly ascribe to them.

But now, Christ is by Nature Truly God; (as Truly, as Man is by Nature Truly Man ;) that is, he is God, not Self-existent and Unoriginate; (for That is peculiar to the Father, and absolutely incommunicable;) but he is God, as having, by that Nature which he derives from the Father, True divine Power and Dominion over all things both in Heaven and Earth, in subordination to Him who alone is absolutely [οἱ καντοκραττω] of Himself Supreme over All.

## $\mathrm{N}^{\circ}$ 8.

HEB. III; 3, 4, 5, 6. For this Person [viz. Christ] was counted worthy of more Glory than Moses, inasmuch as He who bath builded the House, bath more Honour than the House. In the Scripture doctrine, N.264. In the Answer, page 23.

8r

For

82

## A Commentary

For every House is buikled by some one ; But He that built all things, is God.

And Moses verily was faithful in all his House as a Servant.

But Christ, as a Son over his own House, c.

The true Meaning of these words, I think, is This: Moses was faithful as a Servant, in another Man's House; Christ, as a Son in his own House, of his own Building; And the Supreme Householder [the Pater-familias] or Father over all, is GOD. It is a like Form of speaking, to that in 1 Cor. 11, 3, The Head of every Man, is Christ; and the Head of the Woman, is the Man; and the Head of Christ, is GOD. And Ver. 12, but All things, of GOD.

Your Friend is desirous to find another Interpretation of this passage; by which he would indeavour to understand the word, God, as spoken here of Christ. But what he says upon this occasion, is so obscure and dark; and so spoils the Elegancy of the Comparison the Apostle makes, between Moses's Faithfulness as a Servant, and Christ's as a Son; and seems so little satisfactory, even to Himself; that I need only desire the Reader to compare our Two Explications together, and follow That which shall seem to him most reasonable.

on 2 Pet. I, I.

# $\mathrm{N}^{0}$ 9.

2 P E T. I, 1. The Righteousness of God, and our Saviour Jesus Christ.

I do acknowledge (and I observed it before in my Scripture-doctrine,) that the words [ἐνεσιοι οὖπιν τῶν θεῶν μμώτων, χὴ σωτήρ Ιποσὺς χειτῶν,] may, in true grammatical Construction, equally be rendred, either, The Righteousness of our God, and of our Saviour Jesus Christ, or, The Righteousness of our God and Saviour Jesus Christ. So that, if one Man thinks the word, God, is here meant of the Father, and another thinks that it is rather applied in this place to the Son; Neither of their Opinions can be demonstrably disproved.

It seems to Me, to be meant of the Father; and your Friend thinks, on the contrary, it belongs to the Son. Only he did not do well, to set down his own Translation only, which determines the Sense according to his Explication; whereby an English Reader may be deceived, so as to think there is no Ambiguity in the Original; whereas indeed the Original leaves it wholly Ambiguous, and the English Translation in our Bibles determines it according to my Explication.

In the Scripture doctrine,

84

## A Commentary

My Reasons for understanding it of the Father, are: First, because the word, God, throughout the New Testament, generally signifies the Father, and therefore most probably does so in this place likewise. And Secondly, because in the very next Verse, the same words are repeated in such a Construction, as determines the Sense necessarily without Any Ambiguity; [ἐπὶν γνωστὸς τῆς θεωρὸς, ὑπ'ησθὸς τῆς κυείαν ἡμψώ] The Knowledge of God, and of Jesus our Lord. Now since, in the second Verse, these words, [The Knowledge of God, and of Jesus our Lord,] are in the Original placed in such a Construction, as of necessity to signify God the Father in Contradistinction to Jesus our Lord; 'tis probable that, in the first Verse likewise, those words, [The Righteousness of God, and our Saviour Jesus Christ,] though placed ambiguously in the Original, yet were intended in the same manner to signify God the Father, in Contradistinction to Jesus our Saviour.

Your Friend's Reasons for understanding this Text the other way, are : First, that the words [Kvεῖθ ἡμᾶν κοιὴ Κατῆρ] our Lord and Saviour, are often used conjunctly of Christ. But This is a very different Expression : For, our Lord and Saviour, is always meant of Christ, and never of the Father ; But on the contrary, God our Saviour, is always spoken of the Father, and never

```markdown

```

on 2 Pet. 1, 1.

never of Christ: See 1 Tim. 1, 1, The Commandment of God our Saviour, and the Lord Jesus Christ; and Tit. 3; 4, 6, The Love of God our Saviour,—which He shed on us abundantly through Jesus Christ our Saviour. 'Tis most probable therefore, that in the present Text likewise, both God and our Saviour Jesus Christ being mentioned, it was intended to be understood distinctly both of the Father and of Christ. Secondly, Your Friend alleges, that those other the like words, Tit. 2, 13, [τὸ μεγελίς Θεῖν χωρτήσεως ἡμου ἱποσὴ χειζῶν, The Great God and our Saviour Jesus Christ,] though he acknowledges them to seem, in our English Translation, against him; yet were understood by Clemens Alexandrinus and Gregory Nyssen, as if the Apostle had said, Our Great God and Saviour Jesus Christ: And This Sense he inclines to think the most probable, because the word [ὁπφάνεια, The Appearing of ὅc.] is never used elsewhere in the New Testament, but of Christ; the Father being never said to Appear. But This reason, I think, is by no means sufficient: For, Christ being the Image of the Invisible God; these words, the glorious Appearing (or, the Appearing of the Glory) of the Great God and our Saviour Jesus Christ, (as our English Translators rightly render the Text,) very naturally signify, the Appearing of the Great God By our Saviour

85

86

- For in Rom. 9, 5, it is uncertain whether the word, God, was originally in the Text; and, if it was, whether it be not there spoken of the Father. In 1 Tim. 3, 16, instead of the word, God; all the Old Versions have it [qui or quod] Which, or He which: And all the Antient Fathers, though the Copies of many of them have it now in the Text itself, [Štðs, God;]

## A Commentary

viour Jesus Christ; according to the Analogy of those other Scripture-expressions, that God shall judge the World by Jesus Christ, and that Christ shall come in the Glory of his Father, that is, the Glory of his Father shall appear invested in Him. Besides; the words, [τὰ μογαλαθ Θεῖν, The Great God,] being in the Old Testament the Character of the Father; Deut. 10, 17; 2 Sam. 7, 22; 2 Chr. 2, 5; Nehem. 9, 32; Job 36, 26: Ps. 86, 10; Fer. 32, 18; and in the New Testament never used of Christ, but of the Father only, Rev. 19, 17; 'tis therefore very reasonable that they should here also be so understood. Especially, considering the general Style of St Paul: Who, having laid it down as a Foundation, 1 Cor. 8, 6, that to Us there is but One GOD, the Father; and One LORD, Jesus Christ: and Epb. 4; 5, 6, One LORD, One GOD and Father of all: does so constantly and uniformly keep to this Rule of expression

through his whole Writings, that (in all * uncontested places) he never fails to confine the word, God, to the Father, and (except in Citations out of the Old Testament,) the word, Lord, to Christ; At least, whenever Both are mentioned together. Besides: This whole passage,

on 2 Pet. 1, 1.

passage, [The GRACE of GOD----bath APPEAR- ED to all Men;----looking for the glorious APPEAR- ING of the GREAT GOD, and our Saviour f E SUS CHRIST;] is exactly parallel to, and explained by, That other Passage in the next Chapter, Tit. 3; 4, 6, [The Kindness and LOVE of GOD our Saviour, (viz. of the Father,) toward Man APPEARED;----which HE shed on us----through f E SUS CHRIST our Saviour; that, being justified by his GRACE, &c.] But before I leave this Text, 'tis reasonable that I acquaint

the Reader with an Argument in favour of your Friend's Interpretation, much stronger than any that he has brought for himself. It may be alleged; if the Great God signifies here the Father, and not Christ; that then St Paul would have said, τὸ μεγαλὴ γεὺς ἐν Τῆ σωτὴρὶ μμών 'Iποὺ χειτὰ, and not simply τὸ μεγαλὴ γεὺς ἐν σωτὴρὶ μμών 'Iποὺ χειτὰ. But to This, the true Answer is; that in Nouns Characteristical and Equivalent (as it were) to Proper Names, the Article

87

yet from the Tenour of their Comments upon it, and from their never citing it in the Arian Controversy, it appears they read it, [qui or quod,] Which, or He which; till the time of Macedonius under the Emperour Anastasius, in the Beginning of the Sixth Century. Of the Two passages cited to the contrary by the learned Dr Mills in his Appendix, That from Justin Martyr does not prove he did, but rather that he did not, read it [Stds] God; and That from Athanasius, is out of a Book acknowledged to be spurious. And therefore these Two Texts, [1 Tim. 3, 16, and Rom. 9, 5,] are by your Friend himself omitted in his Collection. Lastly, Heb. 1, 8, is only a Citation out of the 45th Psalm, wherein the word is rendred Stds God, and is there spoken of a person, whom GOD, even His God, had anointed.

## A Commentary

ticle is very frequently left out. Thus ἐσὶς is often used to signify the same as οἱ ἐσὶς, μηεῖς as οἱ ημείς (ὔττήρ as οἱ ημείς; Though on the contrary οἱ ἐσὶς is never used for ἐσὶς, οἱ ημείς for μηεῖς, nor οἱ ημείς for ὔττήρ in general: The reason whereof, is, in the Nature of the Language, evident enough. Now in like manner as, supposing St Paul had said, τὰ μέγαλὴ ἐσάς χήμωι μηεῖς (that is, τὰ μηεῖς) ημείν 'Iποσὺ χειτᾶν, every Body would have understood θεᾶν to belong to the Father, and μηεῖς to Christ; so, when he says τὰ μέγαλὴ ἐσάς χήμωι ημείς (that is, τὰ σωτήρ) ημείν 'Iποσὺ χειτᾶν, his meaning is still evidently the same. Thus Luke 2, 11, Unto you is born this day (σωτήρ) a Saviour: It is put as signifying the same with οἱ ημείς, and should accordingly have been translated, The Saviour, (the expected or promised Saviour,) which is Christ the Lord. Again, Phil. 3, 20, From whence also we look for [σωτήρ, here our Translators rightly render it as if it had been τοῦ σωτήρ.] The Saviour, the Lord Jesus Christ. In like manner, I Tim. 1, 1, the words which we read, καὶ βοπιαταγυνν εἰδευ (ὔττήρ ημείν @ μηεῖς 'Iποσὺ χειτᾶν, by the commandment of God our Saviour and the Lord Jesus Christ, are in some Manuscripts, κατὰ βοπιαταγυνν εἰδευ (that is, τὰ γεῖς) πατερός, μηεῖς (that is, τὰ σωτήρ) ημείν 'Iποσὺ χειτᾶν,

# $\mathrm{N}^{\circ}$ 10.

MATT. XIX, 17. There is None Good, but In the One, that is God. Scripture doctrine.

Concerning This Text I have already No 1. In the Ani discoursed at large above, No 1. I shall here fwer, pag only take Notice of what your Friend has added New in this place.

I had cited a passage of Clemens Alexandrinus, where he Paraphrazes the words [One, that is God,] by these which follow, [οἱ καττήρ μικρὰ ὑπὸν τοῖς ἐφανώς, My Father which is in Heaven.] Your Friend alleges out of him Another passage, wherein he says, The One only Good God is the Son in the Father. I leave the Reader to compare the Two passages together, and draw from thence the best Inferences he can. Only let him consider, that One of them is a direct Exposition of the Text itself; the other, is only a Rhetorical Sentence of the Author.

In like manner, out of Athanasius, I cited a passage, wherein, by way of Exposition upon this very Text, he says, [The See Script Son, when he came into the World, glorified not himself, but his FATHER; saying to 52.

a cer-

## A Commentary

a certain Person that came to him, Why callest thou Me, Good? There is None Good, but One, that is God.] Your Friend refers to Other passages of the same Author, where he says the Son is not excluded, but is In the One and Only God. In what Sense This may well be true, and yet the same Author's Comment upon the Text still hold good and consistent, that the One God there mentioned by our Saviour, is the Father; an intelligent Reader will easily apprehend. If not; then What does the Authority alleged, amount to?

Novatian, speaking of the Father, has these words; Whom alone our Lord justly pronounces to be Good. Your Friend indeavours to find in the same Author some other passage, which, by a remote Metaphysical and Scholastick consequence, such as Novatian never thought of, may seem to cast some obscurity upon this plain passage. But Novatian spoke every where very uniformly; And his Whole Book was written professedly upon This Subject; And the Design of it throughout, is to maintain the very thing I am contending for: Which he has done with great Accuracy, against the Errours in Both Extremes: So that his Book is highly worth the perusal of all Learned Men.

And These are All the Authorities, your learned Friend here refers to.

As

on Matt. 19, 17.

As to the Text itself, the True Meaning of it seems to Me to be this: That in the like Sense as God the Father is in other places of Scripture declared to be the One and Only God, he is in this place declared to be also the One and Only Good. That is to say: As God the Father is, by way of Eminence, the One and Only God, because He Alone has [ἀντοθεόπτα] Divinity absolutely of Himself, Supreme, Unoriginate, and not derived from Any; (as Origen learnedly and at large argues in the place before-cited; See above, pag. 65.) and yet Christ is also True God, because he has True Divinity derived to him from the Father; whereas all False Gods, are no Gods at all: So God the Father is likewise, by way of Eminence, the One and Only Good, because He Alone is [ἀντομαγὴν] the original underived Fountain of Goodness; and yet Christ also is Truly Good, by having the Father's Goodness communicated to him without Measure; whereas False Gods, have neither Any Dominion, nor Any Goodness at all.

To This, your Friend answers : That Self-originated and Begotten, do not appear to be essential, but personal Characters ; inferring, not a difference of Substance, but a different manner of having their Subsistence ; the Son receiving it by Generation from the Father,

91

pag. 27.

92

## A Commentary

Father, which denominates him Begotten; the Father having it without receiving it from any other, which names him Self-originated.

I reply: His affirming Self-originated and Begotten, to be, not essential, but personal Characters; is affirming, in other Words, that they are, not essential, but essential Characters. For What is a personal Character, but a Character which distinguishes That particular Person from all other Persons? and What is This, but the Essence of That Person? If we know any thing of the Essence of God, the first and of all others the most essential Character of him, is his being Self-existent or Unoriginated. The Substance of the Son, whatever the Nature of it be in other respects, yet is certainly derived from the Father; otherwise, the Son being Self-existent as well as the Father, it would follow, that Not the Person of the Son, (that is, Not the Son himself,) but the Personality only of the Son was begotten of the Father: Which is unintelligible Metaphysicks, and without any Foundation in Scripture. However, your Friend, by allowing the Son to be Begotten, and the Father alone to be Self-originated or (to speak more properly) Unoriginated; clearly grants All that I contend for: Though, at the same time, by darkning it

on Rom. 11, 36.

93

with other more obscure and metaphysical Expressions, he would seem to deny it.

## N° 11.

ROM. XI, 36. Of him, and through him, and to him are all things.

The Meaning (I think) is: From Him [viz. from the Father] all things derive their Being, by Him all things are preserved and governed, to his Glory all things shall terminate.

Your Learned Friend paraphrases the Words thus : Of him, as HE is Father; through him, as HE is Son ; to him (or rather, in him, for so the Particle es is frequently used,) as HE is the Holy Ghost.

Had he here intended to express the Sabellian Notion, which in the Whole amounts to the very same with Socinianism; denying the Son and Holy Ghost to be any thing more than mere empty Names; I know not how it could possibly have been declared in more full and expressive Words.

The Passage he cites out of Novatian, is not for him, but directly against him. All things (fays Novatian, speaking of God the Father,) are OF Him, because they exist by his

In the Scripture-
doctrine,
N° 372.
In the Answer, page
2;

94

Discourse on 2 Pet.
2, 1.

## A Commentary

his Command, and are THROUGH Him, because they are [verbo ejus digesta] put in order by His Word; (Not, as your Friend would have it, by Him as HE is the Word.)

I observe one thing further upon This Head. Your Friend is very fond of confounding All Language. In the first Text he quotes, he will have [εις] to signify One Thing, instead of One Person. And here he would have [εις ἀστὴν] to signify, in Him, instead of, to Him: The Particle, he says, being Frequently so used. But there is no such thing in the World, as putting one Word, in this manner, for another: And indeed it is impossible there should; For, at that rate, no Language would have any Signification. Some Grammarians indeed give Instances of one Word's seeming to be put for another; But the Instances they allege, are always either Corruptions of the Copies, or else their own Mistakes. There are some places in the New Testament, where our Translators render [εις ἀστὴν] in him; But where-ever they do so, 'tis because they mistake the Sense. Thus 1 Cor. 8, 6, To us there is but One God, the Father; of whom are all things, and We In him: In the Greek it is [εις ἀστὴν] and we To him: That is, To or For his Glory; Or, (as the Learned Mr Mede paraphrases it,) to whom as Supreme we are

to

## on Ephef. 4, 6.

to direct all our Services. The like vulgar Error, with regard to the Particle [in] in the Latin Language, I have refuted in my Annotations upon Cafars Commentaries, ad lib. 4. de Bello Gallico, pag. 76, lin. 12. Sometimes indeed one and the same Word has indifferently two or more significations; and then it may (according as the Sense requires) be understood in Any of those Significations. Thus the Word [ἐπὶ] in the New Testament, according to the Genius of the Hebrew and Hellenistical Stile, signifies indifferently In or By. But there is no such thing in any Language, as putting one Word for another; And therefore εἰς ἀντον, (the Word εἰς having but one signification,) can be rendred no otherwise than To Him.

I JOHN V, 20, 21. This is the True God, and Eternal Life—Keep yourselves from Idols.

Your Learned Friend reads the whole Passage thus : The Son of God is come, and bath

## on 1 John 5, 20, 21.

bath given us an Understanding that we may know Him that is True, [viz. the Son of God ;] And we are in Him that is True, even in his Son Jesus Christ. This [viz. Jesus Christ] is the True God and Eternal Life : Little Children, keep yourselves from Idols.

I read and understand the Text thus : The Son of God is come, and hath given us an Understanding, [has enlightened the Eyes of our Understanding, as 'tis Ephe. 1, 18,] that we may know the True God, [viz. that we may know and acknowledge the Father revealed by the Son;] And we are in Him that is True, [viz. in That True God,] by (or, through) his Son Jesus Christ. This is the True God, and Eternal Life, [viz. This God, whom the Son has given us an Understanding to know, is the True God; and to know and acknowledge him, and to be In him in (or by) his Son, this is eternal Life: ἐπὶς εἰσυ οἱ αλληλινεύς Θεοδης, χή (αυπ) η ζωῶι αἴσιν: This is the True God, and This the Way that leads to him; This is the true Worship of God, by his Son, unto eternal Life:] Little Children, keep your selves from Idols, [from Idol-Worship.]

97

The Reasons for each of these Interpretations, are as follows.

## G

Your

98

A Commentary

Your Friend alleges in the first place, that the general Design of the Epistle, is not to teach the Truth and Existence of the One only God, in opposition to Idols; but to encourage Believers to continue in the Faith of the Son of God, by assuring them that they had eternal Life by virtue of That Faith. And from hence he supposes the Apostle to conclude, that the Son of God, Jesus Christ, is Himself the True God, and the only way to Eternal Life, or rather Eternal Life itself; and that therefore Men ought to keep themselves from Idols, or from All Means of approaching God, besides this Divine Person.

But now This Argument proves just the contrary to what your Friend intended. For if the Design of this Epistle be (as indeed it is,) to encourage Believers to continue in the Faith of the Son God, as that wherein their Eternal Life consists; and to warn them to keep from Idols, that is, from All other means of approaching God, but through this divine Person: Does it not from hence plainly appear, that the Apostle, in his Conclusion, means to declare Christ to be the Way, the Truth and the Life, the only Means of approaching God, that is, not of approaching himself, but of approaching his Father, who is here stiled The True God, whom the Son of God has given

on i John 5, 20, 21.

given us an Understanding to know, and, by giving us That Understanding, is our Eternal Life.

As the Design of the Gospel, is to show, that This is Life Eternal, to know the Only True God, and Jesus Christ whom He has sent, Joh. 17, 3; and again, John 20, 31, These things are written, that ye might believe that Jesus is the Christ the Son of God, and that believing ye might have Life through his Name: So the Design of this Epistle is to show, that our Eternal Life consists, in believing Jesus to be the SON of God, and in being brought by him to the knowledge and acknowledgment of the True God in opposition to all Idols; and not in believing him [the Son of the True God,] to be himself that very same individual True God [that very same Person] whose Son he is. Verse the 5th of this Chapter; Who is he that overcometh the World, but he that believeth that Jesus is the SON of God? And Ver. 11, 12, 13, This is the record, that God bath given to us Eternal Life; and this Life is in his SON: He that bath the Son, bath Life; and he that bath not the Son of God, bath not Life: These things have I written unto you that believe on the Name of the SON of God; that ye may know that ye have Eternal Life, and that ye may believe on the Name of the SON of God:

A Commentary

In the next place, your Friend alleges, that the word [αλληδίνος, and οὐ αλληδίνος,] He that is True, is in St John's Writings seldom applied to the Father; in this first Epistle, never applied to the Father; but, in all his Writings, frequently applied to the Son: As Rev. 3, 7, These things faith He that is True; Rev. 3, 14, These things faith the Faithful and True Witness; & Rev. 19, 11, He that sat upon the white Horse, was called Faithful and True; as if, True, were a Name or Attribute more particularly the Son's. And from hence he infers, that therefore probably in the present passage also, the words, Him that is True, and, in Him that is True, and, the True God, are all spoken of the Son: Especially since, in the Construction of the words, [We are in Him that is True, even in his Son Jesus Christ,] 'tis plain (he says) that the Terms, in Him that is True, are explained by, in his Son; so that no doubt (he says) can be made, but that the word True belongs here to Christ; and therefore 'tis a strong Probability, that they are meant of Him also in the foregoing words, that we may know Him that is True.

Now All this (which I have represented in its full force) is very plausible to an Unlearned Reader, and (I believe) very sincere

## on 1 John 5, 20, 21.

fincere (as the whole Book seems to be) in the Writer himself. But yet Nothing is more impossible, than This his Interpretation. For it all depends upon Two very great Errours: One, an Error in the reading of the Original; The Other, a Mistake in the English Translation. The First, is an Error in the reading of the Original: For if your Friend had (as it was reasonable he should have done,) consulted Dr Mills; he would have found that the Most and Best Manuscripts have it, [ἐναγνωστὸν μεν τῆς αλληδίνον Θεόν,] that we may know [not, him that is True, but] the True GOD. Now though your Friend's Observation is very right, that [αλληδίνος, or ο αλληδίνος, he that is True,] when used alone, as Rev. 19, 11, always signifies the Son, because it is of the same import with [ο αλληδίνος μορφιws] the Faithful and True Witness, Rev. 3, 14: yet, on the contrary, when it is joined with [Θεός] God, it always signifies the Father, and never the Son: Job. 17, 3, That they might know Thee the Only True God [αλληδίνος θεόν,] and Jesus Christ whom thou hast sent; And I Th. 1, 9, To serve the Living and True God, [Θεώ αλληδίνος,] and to wait for His Son from Heaven. The first part therefore of the Text before us, according to the true Reading, stands thus: The Son of God is come, and

## on 1 John 5,20,21.

is True, were meant of Christ; Whom then would the word, His, refer to, in the following Period, even in HIS Son Jesus Christ? Is not, HIS Son, as much as to say, the Son [τὸ αλληγήν] of That True One? and if then by That True One was meant Christ, would not This be to say that Jesus Christ was the Son of Himself? Or, does the word, His, refer to some very remote Antecedent? The True Rendring therefore plainly is; that we may know the True God, and we are In That True God BY his Son Jesus Christ: That is; Christ has not only given us an Understanding to know and acknowledge the True God, but has moreover brought us to a still nearer Union and Communion with him, even so as to be In Him: Thus ch. 4, 15, Who soever shall confess that Jesus is the Son of God, God dwelleth in Him, and He in God; And John. 17, 21, Thou, Father, in Me, and I in Thee, that They also may be one in Us. The Design of the Apostle is not to prove (as your Friend supposes,) that we Are in Christ; but to infer from thence, (That being the Premise, not the Conclusion of his Argument,) that in like manner as Those who are in the World, (ver. 19,) are thereby [ἐν τὴν ποντηρ] in the Evil One, and, because they have not the Son, therefore neither have they the Father, ch. 2, 23; so We, on the contrary, who Are in Christ, are

## A Commentary

thereby Consequently [ὐπὶ πλῆς ἀλληνῶν] in the True God, and Know ourselves to be of God (ch. 5, 19.) because by Christ we have the Knowledge of the True God (ver. 20.) and are in the True God by his Son Jesus Christ. We have the Father (ch. 2, 23.) or are in the Father, ver. 24. Thus [εἰναίον τῶν νυνιμαπ.] to be In the Spirit: And [εἰναίον τῶν νυνιμαπ αὐτῶν πλῆς] to be in an unclean Spirit, signifies the same as [ὑχυν] to have an unclean Spirit. We are in the True God then, BY his Son Jesus Christ: So the Præposition [ὐπὶ] signifies in the Hebrew Idiom. Thus ver. 11th of this very Chapter; This is the Record, that God bath given to us Eternal Life, and This Life is IN (that is, By or Through) his Son. Again, ver. 5, This is He that came by Water [δὲ υδαπες] and Blood; not by Water [δὲ ΕΝ τῶν υδαπες] only, but by Water and Blood. Or, the words may be rendred thus; We are In That True God, by being IN his Son Jesus Christ: Taking the Præposition in its usual Signification; As ch. 2, 24, Te shall continue In the Son and In the Father. But the more natural rendring of the words before us, is (as I said,) We are in That True God, BY his Son Jesus Christ. For suppose the Apostle had said in this place, according to his usual Style, εὔμεν τῶν τῶν ἀλληνῶν, [ὐπὶ αὐτῶν τῶν μμιν.] τῶν τῶν αὐτῶν Ιποσύ χριζω, it would then have been manifestly

## on 1 John 5,20,21.

manifestly impossible to render the latter Præposition [ἐν,] otherwise than, BY. We are in the True God, [and He in Us,] By his Son Jesus Christ. Lastly, if any one shall allege, that the Præposition [ἐν,] repeated (as in this place) twice together in the same Sentence, cannot so vary, as in the one part to be rendred, in, and in the other, by; the following undeniable Instances will convince him of the contrary: Acts 7, 44, ἐν τοῖς παντεάσυν ἡμᾶν ἐν τὴν ερήνω, was with (or among) our Fathers in the Wilderness: Rom. 1, 9, ἐν τῆς συνεύματι μυεῦ τῆς ἐναγγελίω, with my Spirit, in the Gospel: Gal. 2, 20, ἐν σαρμι, ἐν πίστεα, in the Flesh, by the Faith: Ephes. 1, 3, ἐν παιση ευλογία ἐν τῆς επικεανιοις ἐν χειτώ, with all Blessings in Heavenly places in Christ: Ephes. 2, 6, ἐν τοῖς επικεανιος ἐν χειτώ, in Heavenly places in [with or by] Christ: Ephes. 3, 21, ἐν τῆς επικλεσις ἐν χειτώ, in the Church by Christ: 2 Th. 1, 4, ἐν υμιν ἐν ταῖς επικλεσις, in [concerning] you in the Churches: 1 Joh, 4, 9, ἐν τέτω—ἐν μμίν, in This—towards us. And thus likewise in the present Passage, ἐν τῆς αλληλινᾶν ἐν τῆς γψᾶν αυτὸ, in the True One by his Son.

In the third place, your Friend alleges that Irenaeus , and some other of the Ancients, stiled Christ [Verus Deus] True God, or, as the better Copies of Irenaeus generally have

105

106

## A Commentary

have it, [Verre Deus,] Truly God ; And that therefore Christ is most probably meant in this Text by the Words, This is the True God.

True God and Truly God, meaning thereby,

(as I have above shown at large, pag. 11,)

not Self-existent, Independent, Underived Divinity; but (as he expressly explains himself in the * Book your Friend so often refers to,) that Christ is Truly God, by having RECEIVED from his Father True Divinity and Dominion over the whole Creation; whereas False Gods have no Divinity or Dominion at all. But in what Sense soever Christ is by Irenaeus affirmed to be, and in reality is, True God; yet in This Text 'tis not He, that is spoken of by that Name. For the preceding Word, He that is True, (to which your Friend thinks nothing but Apparent Prejudice can hinder a Man from referring the following Words, This is the True God;) I say, the preceding Word, He that is True, being spoken (as I have now shown) not of the Son, (as your Friend supposes,) but of the Father; it follows plainly that the next Words, This is the True God, are meant also, not of the Son, but of the Father. For it cannot be, but the same Person must be spoken of throughout; that we may know the TRUE GOD, and we are in Him that is TRUE

[in

## on 1 John 5, 20, 21.

[in That TRUE GOD] by (or through) His Son Jesus Christ, This is the TRUE GOD ec. The word, [ἐπιος, This,] refers back, as is most natural and usual, not to the last word in Order, but to the last and principal in Sense, that which was uppermost in the Writers Thoughts, and chiefly aimed at in the Whole Discourse. Had St John intended to affirm, what your Friend supposes; he would not have said, ἐπιος εὐν, but 'Εὐσί ἐπιος, or ἐπισί μεχι ἐπιος &c. Neither would he have said, ἐπιος εὐν [ο] αλληλινος ἐπιος; for That would have been affirming the Person of the Son to be the very Person of the Father before mentioned; but he would have said, ἐπιος εὐν, or ἐπισί μεχι ἐπιος, 'Αλληλινος ἐπιος. For, as I before observed, though Θεος is often the same as ο θεος, yet, on the contrary, ο θεος is never by Any Writers put for Θεος: As the Learned well understand. It ought also to weigh somewhat with your Friend, that None of the Writers of the Three First Centuries allege these words, This is the True God, as spoken of Christ.

Lastly, your Friend urges in the last place, that "the following part of the Context, Eternal Life, is another Argument that the Person of Jesus Christ is understood by the True God: For it does not

107

" appear;

108

" alfo ".

## A Commentary

"appear, (fays he,) that those Terms [viz. eternal Life] are spoken of any other Perfon in the whole Epistle, but of the Son only; as ch. I, 2; ch. V, 11, 12, 13. If then the Son be the eternal Life; the True God, WHO IS this eternal Life must be the Son

" IS this eternal Life, must be the Son

That the Terms, eternal Life, are indeed in this whole Epistle applied only to the Son, is very true; but This proves, not what your Friend supposes, but the contrary. For St John does not say, that The True God IS eternal Life; but he Therefore calls the Son eternal Life, because it is He that bringeth us to the True God. He is the Way, the Truth, and the Life; and is so, by giving us an Understanding (as this very Text expresses it) that we may know the True God. The Meaning therefore of these last Words [This is the True God and eternal Life,] is, as I before said; This God, whom the Son has given us an Understanding to know, is the True God; and to know and acknowledge him, and to be In him in (or by) his Son, This is eternal Life: ἐπὶς, ἐξεῖν οὐ αλληγονός θεος πὸ (ἀντῶν) ἐφὶς πὸ πάνωι: This is the True God, and This the Way that leads to him: This is, having the Father and the Son, 1 John, 2; 22, 23; and 2 John, 9. This is the True Religion, the Worship of this True God by

## on Jude 4.

and through his Son Jesus Christ : Little Children, keep your selves from Idols ; Beware of Idol-worship.

Your Friend wishes some Instances had been given, in which the True God is used for the True Religion. I answer: By the same Figure that Idols or False Gods, in the very next Words, signifie the Worship of False Gods, which is False Religion; by the very same figure of speaking here, the True God signifies the Worship of the True God, which is True Religion. 'Tis in Neither Case, a strict Translation; but in Both, a true Paraphrase.

Some Copiers of St Judes Epistle, not understanding This Sense of the Words, which St John so clearly explains; and not apprehending how all Deniers of the Son, are Consequently charged as being Deniers of the Father also; have read St Judes Words thus: μὲν νου δεσθότην χή πιελον ἡμῶν 'ιποδύχχεισον or, μὲν θεόν γὰρ δεσπότεν τὴν πιελον ἡμῶν 'ιποδύχχεισον denying our Only Master and Lord, Jesus Christ; or, our Only God and Master, the Lord Jesus Christ. But 'tis plain that Neither of these Readings can be True: First, because the Word Mòr Only, can by no means be ascribed to Christ, as it frequently is (by way of Eminence) to the Father: And Secondly, because the Word [Δεσθότης Supreme Lord] is Never applied to Christ in the New Testament, though Grotius (by Mistake) affirms that it is, in his Note on those Words, 2 Pet. 2, 1, Denying the Lord [Δεσθότητα] that bought them. The Comment of the Learned Dr Whitby upon which place, is very remarkable: "Christ (faith he) being never stiled Δεσπίστης in the New

" Tettament ; and St Jude (ver. 4) diftin-

32, 6. Is he not thy Father who has bought thee? And the Christians, 1 Cor. 6, 20. Ye are bought with a Price; therefore glorify God in your Body, and in your Spirit, which are God's": To which may be added, 1 Pet. 1; 17, 18, 19. If ye call on the Father, —ye know that ye were not redeemed with corruptible things, but with the precious Blood of Christ, as of a Lamb without blemish. Now as Christ is no where in the New Testament stiled Δεσπίτης (but God the Father only, as Luke 2, 29; Acts 4, 24; 2 Tim. 2, 21; fude, 4; and Rev. 6, 10;) so in other the most Antient Writers, and particularly in Clemens's Epistle, the nearest to the Apostolical Stile; the Word δεσπίτης, and οἱ δεσπίτης τὴν ἀπαντῶν. [the Supreme Lord of all,] is with the greatest Care continually appropriated to God the Father. What Grotius (on 2 Pet. 2, 1.) alleges to the contrary, out of Responf. ad Orthodoxos, is out of a late and spurious Book.

Against all this, your Friend has nothing to allege; and therefore he lets pass my Translation of the Word δεσπίτης, (which I just mentioned in my Scripture-doctrine, and have here more largely explained,) without making any Remark upon it at all. The only thing he has here to observe, is; that whereas I cited a Passage out of Bishop Pearson,

## 112

## A Commentary

Pearson, wherein That learned Writer says, (speaking of God the Father,) that He is the Only Potentate, because He alone has all Power of Himself, and whosoever else has Any, bath it from Him, either by Donation or Permission; "we must not", fays your Friend, "understand this Citation out of Pearson, as if That learned Author meant

" that the Son—received his Power from the

"Father either by Donation or Permission". And Why must we not understand That learned Author to mean, what he so expressly affirms? Why, "Because he says in another place, that God was always

"Father, as always God : Which SEEMS

"to import, that the Character of Father

“depends----not upon the Will :---”

" that the Son is as Neceffarily Son, as the

" Father is Father ; and that therefore his

" Power is no more by bare Donation from

" the Father, than his very Being ". Suppose now the latter words of Bishop Pearson, had not only SEEMED to import, but had really imported what your Friend thinks they import. Would it from thence have followed, that his former words did not mean what they plainly express? No: it would only have followed, that in the latter words he had affirmed something, which, by a consequence, whereof perhaps he might not be aware, destroyed his former words. For if the Will of the Father was not con-

## on Jude 4.

cerned in the Generation of the Son, it will follow unavoidably that the Son was not generated at all from the Father, but was as much Self-existent as the Father himself. For whatever is caused by an Intelligent Being, is caused by the Will of That Being. Otherwise it is not (in Truth and Reality) caused by That Being at all, but by some Superior Cause, (be it Necessity, or Fate, or Whatever it be;) under which, the Intelligent Being is in such Case as much an Instrument only, as if it was wholly Unintelligent. For which Reason, All the Learnedest of the Orthodox Fathers, who understood how to argue closely and strongly; Justin Martyr, Origen, Novatian, Eusebius, the Council of Sirmium, Marius Victorinus, Basil, Gregory Nyssen, and indeed almost all Antient Writers excepting only Athanasius; professedly argue (as I have shown at large in my Scripture-doctrine, Part II, §. 17,) that the Son was generated of the Father, not by absolute Necessity of Nature, but by an Act of his eternal Power and Will. Which is the only intelligible Foundation of that Authority the Scripture every where ascribes to the Father in the Mission of the Son. And though the Scripture has no where indeed in express Terms, declared the Generation of the Son to be by the Will of the Father; yet it does so by plain Consequence, when

## $\mathrm{N}^{\circ}$ 16.

MATT. VI, 9. Our Father, which art in Heaven.

Upon this Text, your Learned Friend was obliged by his Hypothesis to say so absurd a thing, that a Man of his Sincerity and Goodness could not speak it in his own Words; and therefore he only repeats the words of Tertullian, who says, that The Son also is invoked in the Father; and (speaking of Christ's delivering this Prayer,) that God only could teach how he would have HIMSELF be prayed to.

Now if This were true, it would follow, that when Christ prayed to his Father, he prayed to Himself; and when any Man prays to the Son to intercede for him, he prays to the Father to intercede for him; and when St John says that Christ is our Advocate with the Father, he means that the Son is an Advocate to Himself. With many other such Absurdities, as not only Tertullian's Authority, but even that of an

Angel

## on 1 Tim. 2, 5.

Angel from Heaven, would be very insufficient to establish in the Opinion of any reasonable Man, who had ever read the New Testament. Your Friend (I presume) sees not these Consequences, because he writes every where like a very sincere and good Man: But yet, for all that, they are Consequences, and as evident ones too, as any in Euclid.

## $\mathrm{N}^{\circ}$ 17.

1 T I M. II, 5. For there is One God; and One Mediator between God and Men, the Man Christ Jesus.

That the words, One God, are here meant of the Father only, is evident from hence, that they are used as a Description of the Relative Person, to whom the One Mediatour makes Intercession. For, to whom is the Mediation made? Is it not to the Father? 1 Job. 2, 1, We have an Advocate with the FATHER, Jesus Christ the Righteous. Not an Advocate with the Father and the Son; For then Christ, who mediates as God-man, would mediate to Himself: Which is abfurd.

What does your learned Friend answer to This? Why, the One God (he says) is H2 spoken

115

In the Scripture doctrine,
No 501
& 244.

37.

116

A Commentary

spoken in opposition to False Gods. Not so in this place: For there is no mention of False Gods in the Context: But One God is put directly as the Person mediated to, (which all Men allow to be the Father only,) in contradistinction to the Person mediating to him, which is the Whole Person of the Son incarnate. Wherefore though the mentioning the Son here (as your Friend goes on) After the One God, had not in it self been a sufficient Argument that he is excluded out of the One God; yet the mentioning him with the Character of Mediatour, in contradistinction to the One God mentioned under the Character of the Person mediated to, evidently shows him to be not included here by the Apostle in Him whom He thus stiles by way of Eminence the One God.

All the Passages that follow, cited out of the Antients, to prove that they stiled Christ, God; (together with your Friends very obscure Comments upon some of them;) are entirely besides the Purpose. For I have already often shown, in what Sense they taught (without the Confusion your Friend introduces,) that Christ, both God and Man, became our Mediatour and Intercessor, in order to bring Men back to His and Their God and Father Supreme absolutely over All. " And in the Revelation, when John would

"have worfshipped the Angel, be forbad him,

"Fellow-Servant, and of thy Brethren;

"Worship the Lord Jesus". Thus Cyprian.

Now from this whole passage, what any Man can infer contrary to any thing I have anywhere affirmed, I cannot imagine. Only instead of the words which We now read, Worship God, (that is, according to the general Use of the word God in the New Testament, worship the Father ;) Cyprian either read in his Copies, or perhaps cited by his Memory, the following words, Worship the Lord Jesus. Which, though not the True Reading of the Text, yet contains nothing in it inconsistent with what I said in my Scripture-doctrine concerning absolutely Supreme Worship being peculiar to the Person of the Father. For so we find in Scripture a Worship given to Christ, which cannot be given to God the Father: Rev. 1, 5, Unto Him that loved us, and washed us from our Sins in his own Blood, and bath made us Kings and Priests unto God and his Father, [πὶ Θεῖν ἐ πανέι αυτὸν, unto His God and Father ;] to Him be Glory and Dominion for ever and ever, Amen.

on Luke 1; 16, 17.

# $\mathrm{N}^{\circ}$ 19.

LUKE I; 16, 17. Many shall He [John In the the Baptist] turn to the Lord their God; doctrine, and he shall go before Him, &c. N° 534. In the An- fwer, pag.

The Title, Lord God, in the common and natural Apprehension of all Mankind, and in the constant Usage of Scripture, is a PERSONAL Character; expressing, not speculative and metaphysical Qualities, but Personal and Relative Perfections, namely Dominion and Government over the World. As appears plainly from the Relative Terms so frequently joined with it, OUR God, OUR Lord God, the God OF HEAVEN, and the like: None of which Terms can be joined with metaphysical Appellations, which are Absolute and not Relative; such as are, The Infinite Being, The Eternal Being, and the like.

By this Term therefore, Lord God, in the present Text, must of necessity be meant either God the Father, considered as Supreme and absolute Lord of all; or else the Son, who, by communication of the Father's Power and Dominion, is also, in Subordination to Him, truly Lord of all.

In which foever of these Senses it be underfstood, nothing can from this Text be H 4 inferred

120

A Commentary

inferred contrary to any thing I have affirmed. For if the Title, Lord God, be here applied to the Son; it is still applied to him in the Sense now explained: And if it be meant of the Father, (as I think is more probable;) then the latter part of the Text is, by an easy Figure, a Transition from the Person of Him who is the Invisible God, to the Person of Him who is the Image of the Invisible God, and who always acts and speaks in His Name and Authority. A Figure not unusual, either in Scripture or other Authors. See my Scripture-doctrine, No 538, 597, 616 & 1056: Also 1 Job. 3, 1, compared with ver. 5.

Your Friend contends that the Title, Lord God, is here given to the Son. And if it was so, in the Sense wherein his own Quotations out of the Fathers (as I shall show presently) give that Title to Christ; it would not prove any thing at all against Me. For Most of those Fathers, in those very Passages he cites, explain themselves (as will appear immediately) in the very same manner as I have done. But your Friend, not content to understand these words in the Text, in the same Sense as the like words are found in his Quotations; labours to suggest another Sense, which yet he cares not to express distinctly, but leaves it darkly to his Reader to infer as well as he can. If his words infer any thing; it must

## on Luke 1; 16, 17.

must be, that the Son is so stiled Lord God, as not to be distinguished from the Father: Which, when expressed intelligibly, can end in nothing else, (though your Friend seems not to intend it.) but in denying the Son to have Any Being at all: Which is the Socinian Notion. And What Need is there of interpreting Scripture after so unintelligible a manner, when All the Texts in their obvious and natural Sense carry with them another Signification? That which seems to have led your Friend into This, is a Notion he has entertained of certain Scripture-Interpretations justly Conceived to be founded upon TRADITION, and of certain Doctrines and Expositions of the Sacred Writings, handed down to the Fathers by TRADITION from Their Fathers. What and where this Tradition is, it does not appear: For the Greater Part of the Fathers plainly enough expresses the very Notion I am pleading for; (as the Reader will see presently in those very Passages, which your Friend has cited to prove the contrary:) But What that Doctrine is, which your Friend alludes to as Traditionary, remains very obscure; and he himself leaves it to his Reader to guess at, rather than any where explicitly expresses it. What he drops in different places, amounts (if any thing) to a Total Confusion of Persons: Which Persons he sometimes speaks of.

121

pag. 51,
49.

of,

122
pag. 58,
or 28.

## A Commentary

of, as being One subordinate to Another ; and at other times, as if he thought them All but One Person. Your Friend being a sincere and sober-minded Man, has entred only a little way into these Traditionary Explications of Scripture : But those who have gone far into them, (as some late Writers have done,) have given such Vifionary and Cakbalistical Interpretations, especially of the Old Testament; as give too sad an Occasion for Infidels to look upon all Religion as Enthusiasm; and particularly have caused the Study of the Hebrew Language, (which in itself is a plain, easy, inartificial Language,) to be brought (by Men of weak Judgments abusing it) into the utmost Contempt.

But to proceed to particulars.

It seems to Me that the Term, Lord God, in the Text before us, signifies the Father. My Reason is, because this Title, the Lord God, when used absolutely, and without any antecedent Mention of the Person it refers to, does in Scripture-Language, by way of Eminence, Always signify the Father. Thus in this very Chapter, Blessed be the Lord God of Israel, (says Zacharias, ver. 68,) for that he hath raised up an horn of Salvation for us in the House of his Servant David; viz. for that he hath raised up unto us That Lord, before whom John the Baptist was to go, ver. 76. To This,

on Luke 1; 16, 17.

This, your Friend replies, that elsewhere $^S$ Thomas calls Christ, My Lord and my God. But I answer, This is plainly meant in That Sense, in which $^S$ Paul applies to him the Title God in that Citation out of the Psalmist; Thy Throne, O God, is for ever and ever; thou hast loved Righteousness; therefore GOD, even THY GOD, [or, therefore, O God, THY GOD] hath anointed thee, &c. Your Friend alleges but one Text more, Rev. 22, 6, The Lord God of the Holy Prophets sent his Angel: And this he thinks is meant of the Son, because it is said, Rev. 1, 1, that Jesus sent his Angel. As if it might not very properly be said, that God the Father sent That Angel, whom Christ sent forth with that Revelation which God the Father gave him to send by the Angel. There are in Scripture innumerable Instances of the like manner of speaking. And that the Lord God of the Holy Prophets does indeed here signify the Father, is evident from hence, that, in the five fore-going Verses of the same Chapter, the word, God, and Lord God, are used two or three times in express contradiction to the Lamb.

As to the Passages your Friend cites out of the Old Testament; if they be understood literally, (in the manner he cites and applies them,) they prove too much for him, viz. that Christ is God the Father himself;

123

PAG. 44.

pag. 43.

124

## A Commentary

himself; that is to say, in other words, that Christ is a mere Man only, in whom God the Father dwelt. But if they must not be thus understood, (as he will grant they must not, though he takes no Care to avoid it;) then they can no otherwise be understood, than according to St Stephen's Explication, Acts 7, 30, where he says, The Angel of the Lord appeared to Moses in a Flame of Fire in the Bush. Saying, I am the God of thy Fathers; Which is plainly the Angel of God's presence speaking in the Name and Person and Authority of the Father.

And in This Sense, if the Title, Lord God, in the Text before us, be understood of the Son, it will not prove any thing against Me. Any more than your Friend's Citations out of the Fathers do: Which when the Reader compares, he will be surprised to find almost all of them most fully and plainly expressing that Sense I am now speaking of, and not at all That which your Friend cites them for.

The place he cites out of Justin, is: that Christ is Lord and God; yet so, as that the Father is the Cause [ἀμὴός πὸ αὐπὶ τῆς εἰναι χὴ μωεῖσθ ὑθεῖ] of his being both Lord and God. Directly contrary to what he cited it for.

Honour even too far, have dared to speak of him not as of the Son, but as of God the Father himself.

## $\mathrm{N}^{0}$ 2c.

JOHN I, 1. And the Word was God.

Of these words, I said, there are only Three Possible Interpretations. The first is that the word was That same Person, whom he was with: And This is both a Contradiction in Terms, and also the antient Heresy of Sabellius. The second is; that the Word was Another Self-existent, Underived, Independent Person, co-ordinate to Him with whom he was: And This is the Impiety of Polytheism; subverting that First and Great Foundation of All Religion both Natural and Revealed, the Unity of GOD. The third is; that the Word is a Person, (not λόγος συνδιαθετος the internal Reason or Wisdom of God, which is merely an Attribute of the Father; but a Person, whose Name is called The Word of God, Rev. 19, 13: The Interpreter and Messenger of his Father, λόγος — τὸ ἐπιστὰ πάλειος ἔφυμενεύς "Αγγελῶν, Athanasf. contra Gentes: God by communication of Divinity from Him who is of Himself God, μετοχή τὴν 'Auθοδὲ θυσποίη-

## on A&ts 20, 28.

mer, Origen. in Job. pag. 46:) a Person, deriving from the Father (with whom he existed before the World was,) both his Being itself, and incomprehensible Power and Knowledge, and other divine Attributes and Authority, in a Manner not revealed, and which Humane Wifdom ought not to presume to be able more particularly to explain.

Against this Exposition of mine, your Friend alleges nothing but a metaphysical Inquiry, concerning Substances and Essences ; which the Scripture never at all meddles with, either one way or other : And a figurative Passage out of Clemens Alexandrinus, and another of Irenaus ; which Passages the Reader may understand, by comparing them with other Passages cited above out of the fame Writers.

## $\mathrm{N}^{\circ}$ 21.

ACTS XX, 28. To feed the Church of God, which He bath purchased with his own Blood.

Upon this Text I observed, First; that the Best and most Antient Copies read it, and the most Antient Fathers cite it, The Church of the LORD. This, your Learned Friend conceals entirely from his Reader.

127

In the Scripture doctrine,
N° 538.
In the Answer, pag.
54.

128

A Commentary

er. Which he should by no means have done ; because it removes at once all the difficulty that appears in the construction of the Words.

Secondly, I observed that the Word, God, (if that Reading be True,) may possibly be understood of Christ, in like manner as in John, I, 1. But if, (which is much more natural, supposing That Reading to be genuine,) it be understood to mean the Father; then either, his own Blood, must signify, the Blood of his own Son; or else the Words, He bath purchased with his own Blood, may still be understood of Christ, in the same manner of speaking that we find used by St Luke, ch. I; 16, 17: Not much unlike to which, is that Transition from one Person to another, which St John uses in his first Epistle, ch. 3, ver. 1, compared with ver. 5.

p1g

But your Friend contends for another Interpretation, viz. that the Word, God, signifies in this place Both Father and Son. Of which Confusion, I am very sure there is no Instance in the whole Bible; that one and the same personal and relative Title, such as God or Lord or King or Father or Son or the like; should at one and the same time signify

Two Persons. Yes, he says, St John comprehends the Father and Son under the Name God, in the first verse of his Gospel; and therefore may be supposed to use the

on Acts. 20, 28.

fame Name sometimes for the Father, and sometimes for the Son, or for Both together. But does St John indeed use the Word, God (not only sometimes for the Father, and sometimes for the Son, but also) for Both Together? Verily, had your Friend considered here what he said, I am perswaded he would not have stood to it. For observe what a Paraphrase his Criticism makes, when applied to the Text he directs us to apply it to. John, I, 1. The Word was with God, and the Word was God; that is, The Word was with the Father and the Son, and the Word was the Father and the Son. This is no misrepresentation of his Sense; but is his True, real, and professed Meaning. For his Design is to show, not that the Word, God, means in one part of the Sentence the Son, and in the other the Father; but that the same individual Word singly in one part of the Sentence alone, signifies Both Father and Son. I wonder much, he should not see the Absurdity of it. Well: But in that Passage I referred to, John, 3, 1, he thinks the Case is plain: Behold what manner of Love the Father hath bestowed upon us, that we should be called the Sons of God: In this place, he says, the Term God may very well be judged to stand for the Father and the Son. Let us see how that can be. Why, the Washing of Regeneration, says he, through the Virtue

129

130

1ag. 56.

## A Commentary

tue of their Names invoked in Baptism, begets us into Children, so that we are the Sons of Both Persons. By This Argument he should have said, we are the Sons of the Three Persons. But the Scripture gives quite a different Account of this Matter. We are There called The Sons of God, because we are made, in our measure and degree, by Adoption and Grace, what Christ was, in a complete and perfect manner, by Nature: We are represented as Heirs of God, and Joint-Heirs with Christ, that is, Brethren of Christ and Sons of God. If then our being Sons of God, means that we are Sons of the Father and the Son; then also Christ's being the Son of God, means that he is the Son of the Father and the Son. When once Confusion is introduced, there is no End of Absurdities. But your Friend draws one Argument more, from the Word [φανεωρὸν he shall appear,] used in the next verse, 1 John, 3, 2. Now are we the Sons of God, (that is, faith be, of the One God, Father and Son:) But when He shall appear, (that is, faith be, when the One God shall appear in the Person of the Son.) Put now these two Explications together, and then the whole verse stands thus: Now are we the Sons of the Father and the Son; But when the Father and the Son shall appear in the Person of the Son, we shall be like HIM.

on John 1, 3.

c. I trust your Friend will be ashamed of This, when he considers it again.

## $\mathrm{N}^{\circ}$ 22.

JOH. I, 3. All things were made by Him, [Gr. δι' αὐτῶν, Through him.]

These Words, I said, were fully and clearly explained by the parallel Texts, Eph. 3, 9, God created all things By Jesus Christ; and Heb. 1, 2, By whom also He made the Worlds. And in This Explication, all the Antient Fathers, how much soever they differ from each other in Other things, unanimously agree. Even Tertullian, in his Book against Praxeas: [Alium, per quem omnia; alium, a quo omnia:] One, faith he, O F whom are all things; Another, BY (or Through) whom are all things: Commenting this very Text of St John.

Here therefore your Learned Friend attempts not to allege against Me, either Scripture or Antiquity. Only, after having expressly granted All that I contend for, viz. that the Son works every thing in Obedience to his Father; the Father working primarily, the Son subordinately: he adds, YET the Son works Freely. Which I very readily

pag. 57

## A Commentary

ly grant him, in return for what he has granted to Me. For I never thought, (and I believe no body else ever did,) that the Father uses the Son as an Unintelligent, but as an Intelligent Instrument. Whatever the Father does by the Son, he does by the Free Will of the Son, concurring with and accomplishing the good pleasure of his Father. The Son quickneth whom he will, Joh. 5, 21; Freely, and by his own Will: Because, (ver. 22,) the Father hath committed All judgment unto the Son. So again, Job. 10, 18, No Man taketh my Life from me, but I lay it down of myself, [Freely and Willingly:] I have Power to lay it down, and I have Power to take it again; [Because] This Commandment have I received of my Father.
