# $\mathrm{N}^{\circ}$ 23.

JOHN V, 18. But said also that God was his Father [παιτεία]ov, his own Father,] making himself equal with God.

Your learned Friend, in his Explication of this Text, begins to indulge his Imagination, more than is usual for so calm and reasonable a Writer. The Jews, he says, who drew the Inference, MUST have had

on Joh. 5, 18.

133

some reason for understanding the Premise in so exalted a Sense, as would infer their Conclusion: that is, they MUST have had a Notion that there was a certain Person so closely united to the Great God——that the Great God was his Proper Father, and that on This account he was Equal with the Father：——For except all this be supposed, it is difficult to account how so extraordinary a Conclusion COULD be drawn from a Premise, that was otherwise capable of a lower Interpretation. Now it is CERTAIN that the Jews, if they understood the Meaning of their own Scriptures, MUST have known, that there was a Divine Person subsisting with the Father——from the Beginning of the World, which is called Wisdom; as is EVIDENT from Prov. 9, 22, The Lord possessed me in the Beginning，and that this Divine Person was brought forth, or begotten, when there were no Depths, Prov. 8, 24; And BY CONSEQUENCE that this Divine Person MUST be the Only begotten。They MUST have also known that God had a Son, Prov. 30, 4; and that This Son is the same with Wisdom or the Only-begotten；otherwise Wisdom could not be the Only begotten, if there be a Son begotten different from Wisdom。The Jews, I say, could not have been ignorant of these great Truths, upon the SUPPOSITION of

1 3

134

A Commentary

their Knowledge of the Scriptures ; and this Knowledge OUGHT to be SUPPOSED, 'till the Contrary be made to APPEAR.

Is not This now a wonderful Flight of Imagination; to conceive that the Affirmative ought always to be Supposed, 'till the Negative appears? that every Man ought to be supposed to know every thing, 'till the contrary appears? and that every Accusation brought against our Saviour by his malitious Enemies, must needs be in Them a True Deduction from True Principles Truly understood? That the Jews, not only understood our Saviours calling God his Father, to be a claiming to himself more than any other Man or Prophet could claim; but that they understood also clearly every thing that was hinted in the Old Testament, even in the obscurest Prophecies, concerning the Greatness and Dignity of his Person? The Apostles, after they had conversed with him for several Years, and, besides their own Knowledge of the Old Testament, had heard Him also frequently explain it to them; and had heard him moreover expressly declare that his Kingdom was not of this World, but that at the End He should come in the Glory of his Father with his Angels to judge the whole World; were yet so ignorant, even after his Resurrection, as to ask him, Lord, wilt thou

## on John 5,18.

thou at This time restore again the [temporal] Kingdom to Israel : And had the Jews, his Accufers, so perfect a Knowledge of his spiritual Kingdom, as to understand all the literal, and all the mystical Prophecies concerning him, as perfectly as any Christian has ever done to This day? Surely, there never was a more extraordinary Imagination.

The True State of the whole Matter, is plainly no more than This. Our Saviour having healed a Lame Man upon the Sabbath-day, was accused and persecuted by the Jews as a Sabbath-breaker. To This, our Saviour replies, ver. 17, My Father worketh hitherto, and I work: That is to say; Though the works of God are finished from the Foundation of the World, and my Father ceased indeed on the Seventh day from his works of Creation; yet his works of Providence and Goodness go on every day for ever without Intermission; and I likewise do works of Goodness and Charity, even on the Sabbath-day. The Jews, willing to take any handle (though never so unreasonable) of Accusing him, infer, ver. 18, (by way of Calumny, not by way of strict Reasoning,) that his calling God his Father [o πατρὰ μυ] was as much as assuming to Himself, that God who was the common Father of them All, was in a higher and more peculiar manner [πατρὰ ιδιον]

135

Heb. 4, 3.

136

## A Commentary

His own proper Father: And from This, and from his joyning and comparing his own Works with his Fathers Works in one and the same Sentence, they infer further, in the next step of Calumny, that he made himself equal with God: Meaning thereby, not that he claimed to Himself to be God indeed in Any Sense; (for neither They nor his own Disciples had as yet any the least Thought of That;) but that by Consequence, (which angry Accusers draw very hastily,) he assumed to himself a Power and Authority like That of God. The Expression is the same, and meant in the same Sense, as That other Accusation, Job. 10, 33, Thou, being a Man, makest thy self God: Which was spoken after the same manner, as Men say to an assuming Person, You make your self King; when they intend to charge him with taking upon himself, not the Person, but the State of a Prince. Accordingly, to this Accusation of making himself equal with God, our Saviour replies, ver. 19, not by confounding himself with the Father, (as was to have been expected in your Friend's Scheme,) but by referring all his works To the Father, and showing that he really was (what he pretended to be,) the promised Messiah, the Son of God, sent forth from God, invested with the Power and Authority of the Father: That the Son can do nothing of himself, but what be

on Joh. 5, 18.

137

he seeth the Father do, ver. 19: That the Father loveth the Son, and showeth him all things that himself doeth, ver. 20: That the Father bath committed all judgment to the Son, ver. 22: That therefore, as all Men honour the Father, so they ought also to honour the Son, to the Honour of the Father which sent him, ver. 23: That, as the Father bath Life in himself, so bath he given to the Son to have Life in himself, ver. 26: That he can of Himself do nothing, and that he seeketh not his own Will, but the Will of the Father which sent him, ver. 30: That the works which he doth, bear witness of him, that the Father bath sent him, ver. 36. This is the Answer he gives to the Jews Accusation; And it shows very plainly what he meant by calling God his Father, (which was the Ground of their Accusation,) and by joining himself with the Father in those words, My Father worketh hitherto, and I work.

See this whole matter further explained beneath, No 25 or 26.

The Passage your Friend cites out of Novatian under this Head, is very extraordinary. Novatian says, [cap. 31,] The Son's Godhead is taught us in such a manner, as that None may think that Two Gods are introduced [aut diffonantia aut Inequalitate Divinitatis,] either by a Difference or Inequality

138

"Son be distinct Gods".

Equatione in utroq; oftea-sa, duos faceret innatos; & ideo duos faceret Deos. — Equales inventi duos Deos reddidissent. — Par expressus, duos invisibles ostendisse, & ideo duos comprobâtser & Deos. — Nunc autem, dum se Patri in omnibus obtemperantem reddit, quamvis sit & Deus, Unum tamen Deum Patrem de obedientia sua ostendit, ex quo & originem traxit: Er ideo duos facere non potuit, quia nec duos Origines secit. — Subjectis enim ei quasi filio omnibus rebus a Patre; dum ipse, cum his qua illi subjecta sunt, Patri suo subjicitur; patris quidem fui Filius probatur, caterorum au-cem & Dominus & Deus.

## A Commentary

quality of Divinity. "That is", Says your Friend, "The Divinity of the Son was taught to be like to, and Equal to that of the Father; because otherwise, if they were unlike and unequal, their Natures must be different, and Father and

But now it is impossible for any Man who reads this very Chapter of Novatian, [cap. 31,] not to see that the words, which your Friend cites, are Corrupt; The express design of the Author in every word of that Chapter, being to show directly on the contrary, that, if they were absolutely Equal, then and for that very reason they would of necessity be Two Gods. If (faith

he) they were Both Equal, they would Both be Unoriginate, and consequently Two Gods. Being found Equal, they would be Two Gods. Being Equal, they would be Two Invisibles, and consequently Two Gods. But Now, whilst the Son obeys his Father in all things; though He himself also be God, yet by his Obedience he declares his Father to be The One God, from whom also he derives his Original: And therefore he could not make

on John 5, 18.

make Two Gods, because he did not make Two original Principles. [Here come in the corrupt Words cited by your Friend.] For all things being made subject to him as Son, by his Father; be himself, together with all things under him, being subject to his Father, is proved to be the Son indeed of the Father, but of all other things the Lord and God. The Son indeed is shown to be God, because Divinity is communicated and derived to him; and yet the Father is nevertheless proved to be The One God, whilst That Majesty and Divinity, which the Father communicates to the Son, is by the Son in acknowledgment continually returned back to the Father who gave it. So that God the Father, is justly stiled The God over All; and the Original even of the Son himself, whom he begat Lord of All: And at the same time the Son is the God of all Other things, because God the Father made all things subject to Him whom he begat. Thus Jesus Christ the Mediatour between God and Men, having from his Father All Creatures subjected to him as their God; himself with the

139

Deus quidem ostenditur Filius, cui divinitas tradita & porrecta consipeditur; & tamen nihilo minus Unus Deus Pater probatur, dum gradatim reciproco medu illa majestas atq. divinitas ad Patrem, qui dederat eam, rursum ab illo ipso Filio missa revertitur & retorquerur. Ut merito Deus Pater, Omnium Deus sit, & Principium ipsius quoq; Filii fui quem Dominum genuit; Filius autem, ceterorum omnium Deus sit, quoniam omnibus illum Deus Pater praposuit quem genuit. Ità Mediator Dei & hominum Christus Jesus, omnis creature subjectam fibi habens a Patre proprio potestatem, qua Deus est; cum totà creatura subdita fibi, concors Patri suo Deo inventus, Unum & Solum & Verum DEVM Patrem suum breviter approbavit. cap. 31.

## A Commentary

the whole Creation under his Dominion, being in perfect Agreement with God his Father, has briefly shown his Father to be The One and Only and True GOD.

Hic ergo, QUAMVIS effer in forma Dei, non est rapinam arbitratus Equalem se Deo esse. Quamvis enim se ex Deo Patre Deum esse meminisfer, nunquam se Deo Patri aut comparavit aur contulit; memor te esse ex suo Patre, & hoc ipsum quod est, habere se qua Pater Dedisset. Inde deniq; & ante carnis assumpitionem, sed & post assumpitionem corporis, post iplam præterea resurretti nem, omnem Patri in omnibus rebus obedientiam præstitit pariter ac praestat. Ex quo probatur, nun quam arbitratum illum esse rapinam quandam divinitatem, ut aquaret se Patri Deo: quinimo contra, omni ipsius imperio & voluntati obediens atq: subjectus, eriam ut formam Servi susciperet contentus fuit, hoc est, hominem illum fieri, &c. cap. 17.

And again, paraphrasing That Text, Phil. 2, 6; Christ (faith he.) THOUGH he was in the Form of God, yet did not catch at being Equal with God. For though he knew that he was God, as having God for his Father ; yet he never compared himself with God his Father ; remembring that he was from his Father, and that he received from his Father That very Thing, (viz. his being God.) Wherefore both before and after his taking upon him human Flesh, and also after his Resurrection, he always did and does pay all Obedience to his Father. From

whence it appears, that he never thought fit so to claim to himself Divinity, as to Equal himself with God the Father : Nay on the contrary, he was always obedient to His whole Will and Pleasure, even so as to be content to take upon him the Form of a Servant, that is, to become a Man.

Let

## on Joh. 8, 58.

Let any sensible Person Now judge, whether the Words your Friend cited be not manifestly so corrupt, as to express just the very contrary to what the Author in that very place intended to express. How the Words ought to be read, is not easy to conjecture without Manuscripts. Possibly instead of [aut disfonantia aut Inæqualitate Divinitatis,] it should be [aut disfonantia aut——(some Word here being dropt out) in Equalitate Divinitatis.] But of this, the learned Reader must judge. Something to That Purpose, the Author manifestly meant.

# $\mathrm{N}^{\circ}$ 25 & 26.

JOH. X, 30. I and my Father are One. And

JOH. X, 33. That Thou, being a Man, makest thy self God.

Your learned Friend understands these Texts, of an Unity of Nature; But what he means By Unity of Nature, he declares not. Most usually, what he says about it, amounts to an Unity of Person: As when he says, Hebovah our God, Hebovah is One; Which, in the Scripture, is expressly spoken of One Person; as has been shown above, No 1. So likewise, when he says

pag. 66. The Father and Son IS the One True God : and that Of him are all things, as HE is

Father; Through him, as HE is Son; and To him, as HE is the Holy Ghost; This is undeniably reducing them to One Person only. Nor does he any where show, what he means by That Unity of Nature; in which, Two Persons, Two Intelligent Agents, Equally Supreme, shall not be TWO Gods, that is, Two absolutely Supreme Governours. However, without knowing distinctly what he intends by Unity of Nature, he proceeds to prove that these Two Texts are meant

of

## on John 10; 30, 33.

145

of an Unity of Nature : Not by considering the Context, and the Connexion of the words ; but by Two foreign Arguments. The first, is ; that the Name of God, [Jehovah,] is in several places of Scripture given to the Son: And in what manner This is done, I have just now explained, in the fore-going No 24. The second Argument is, a Collection of Sentences out of the Fathers: Some of which, do not at all mean what your Friend thinks of; (especially those of Novatian; As the Reader will find by comparing the Passages themselves with what immediately goes before and follows after, and with the other Passages which I have above cited out of the same Author :) And the Rest are no Proofs at all, what is the True Meaning of the Texts now before us.

That there is a reasonable Sense, in which the Father and the Son, though One be Self-existent and the Other not, may yet truly be affirmed to be of the same Nature, (though the Scripture never enters into such metaphysical Speculations,) I deny not. But the Question Now is, not what may or may not Truly be affirmed in general, but what is the True Meaning of the particular Texts at present before us. And This is to be gathered, not from our own or other Mens preconceived Hypotheses, but from the Scope and Connexion

K

146

## A Commentary

of our Saviours whole Discourse in the Texts referred to.

Ver. 24, The Jews ask our Saviour, How long doft thou make us to doubt? If thou be the Christ, tell us plainly. Our Saviour replies, ver. 25, The works that I do in my Fathers Name, they bear witness of Me; That is, The Miracles which he worked by the Power and Authority of the Father, proved him to be in reality, what he pretended to be, the Messiah, sent forth from God.

Ver. 26, But ye (faith he) believe not because ye are not of my Sheep; That is they were prejudiced, vitious, and unreclaimable Perfons.

Ver. 27 & 28, My Sheep [well-difposed and unprejudiced Persons] hear my Voice and—follow me; And I give unto them eternal Life, and they shall never perish, neither shall any pluck them out of my hand.

Ver. 29 O 30, For My Father, which Gave them me, is Greater than All; and none is able to pluck them out of my Father's hands : and I and my Father are One. That is : Since None can pluck them out of the Father's hands, and the Father has communicated His Power to the Son; therefore None can pluck them out of the Son's hands: So that, being in the Father's hands, or being in the Son's hands, is One and the

## on John 10; 30, 33.

same thing. [This is the natural Sense of the words, to which the Construction and Connexion of the whole Discourse leads us. And since the same words are used also in other places of Scripture in the very same Sense; As, Job. 17, 22, That They (my Disciples) may be One, even as We (I and the Father) are One; I in Them, and Thou in Me, that they may be made perfect in One; And 1 Cor. 3, 8, He that planteth, and he that watereth, (Paul and Apollos,) are One; 'Tis evident that the Sense, in which your Friend desires to take This Text, cannot be gathered from the Words themselves, nor from the Connexion of our Lord's Discourse, but must be proved (if it be proved at all,) by some other ways.]

47

Ver. 31 & 33. Upon this, the Jews took up Stones to stone him,—saying; we stone thee—for Blasphemy, and because that Thou, being a Man, makes thy self God. Meaning to accuse him, (as I have shown above, $N^{o}23,$ ) not of affirming himself to be the Supreme, Self-existent Deity; nay, nor so much as of taking upon himself to be a Divine Person at all; but only of assuming to himself the Power and Authority of God. For, their Accusation, thou makes thy self God, was not founded (as your Friend imagines, pag. 70,) upon his affirming himself to be One with the Father,

148

## A Commentary

ther, (which Phrase it does not appear they thought at all difficult to be understood;) but the Accusation was founded upon his stiling God his Father, [ver. 25, 29 and 30,] and consequently making himself the Son of God. This appears plainly, from the Answer our Lord gave them in the words immediately following, ver. 34, 35, 36, Is it not written in your Law, I said, ye [Rulers and Magistrates] are Gods, [and Children of the most High?] If he called Them Gods, unto whom the word of God came, and the Scripture cannot be broken; Say ye of Him whom the Father bath sanctified and sent into the World, Thou blasphemeest, because I said, I AM THE SON OF GOD? From these words 'tis evident, that their Charge against him of Blasphemy, for which they went about to Stone him, was founded upon his calling God his Father, (see above, in No. 23,) or declaring himself to be the Son of God; which they, in their Anger, represented, by way of Aggravation, as making himself God.

Now in order to understand clearly what their Notion was of That Blasphemy, wherewith they charged him for calling himself the Son of God; it will be necessary to consider the parallel places, wherein the like Accusation is brought against him. Mark 2, 7; Upon his saying to a sick Man; Thy

on John 10; 30, 33.

Thy Sins be forgiven thee; they answer, Why doth this Man thus speak Blasphemies? Who can forgive Sins, but God only? The Blasphemy they accuse him of here, was his taking upon himself, not the Person, but the Power and Authority of God, to forgive Sins. Again; when he was brought before Pilate, the Accusation against him was, Job. 19, 7, We have a Law, and by our Law he ought to die, because he made himself the Son of God. What they understood here by his making himself the Son of God, and thereby being guilty of Blasphemy; appears from the Account given in the other Three Gospels, of his Examination before the High-Priest in the Council. St Matt. 26, 63, Tell us (said the High-Priest) whether thou be the Christ the Son of God: Jesus faith unto him, Thou hast said: [ἐν λέγειω] not, Nevertheless; but, Moreover] I say unto you, Hereafter shall ye see the Son of Man sitting on the right-hand of Power, and coming in the Clouds of Heaven: Then the High-Priest rent his Clothes, saying, He bath spoken Blasphemy,——ye have heard his Blasphemy. St Mark relates it thus; ch. 14, 61, Art thou the Christ, the Son of the Blessed? And Jesus said, I am; And ye shall see the Son of Man sitting on the right-hand. &c. St Luke thus; ch. 22, 67, They asked him, saying, Art thou the Christ? He answered,

K 3 Hereafter

149

150

## A Commentary

Hereafter shall the Son of Man sit on the right-hand of the Power of God: Then said they all, Art thou Then the Son of God? And he said unto them, ye say that I am. From these places compared together, it is evident, that That which they meant by asking him whether he was the Son of God, was to charge him with taking upon himself to be That Son of Man, of whom Daniel had prophesied, ch. 7, 13, I saw in the Night-visions, and behold, one like The Son of Man, came with the Clouds of Heaven, and came to the Antient of days, [to God the Father,] and they brought him near before him; And there was given him Dominion and Glory and a Kingdom, that all People, Nations, and Languages should serve him; His Dominion is an everlasting Dominion, which shall not pass away; and his Kingdom, that which shall not be destroyed. Our Lord's declaring Himself to be This Son of Man, the Christ, the Son of God, prophecied of by Daniel; was the Blasphemy, whereof the Jews accused him. And therefore 'tis probable, when they said they had a Law by which he ought to die, Joh. 19, 7; they did not mean That Law referred to in the Margin of our Bibles, Levit. 24; 15, 16; which threatens Death to him that curseth God, or blasphemeth the Name of the Lord; but rather That Law, Dent. 18, 20, which threatens

## on John 10; 30, 33.

threatens Death to Him that shall presume to speak a word in the Name of God, without being really sent by him. And accordingly, to This very same charge of Blasphemy. (in the place at present under our consideration, John 10, 33.) we find our Saviour replying, (not what your Learned Friend would have expected in His Hypothesis, but) that he was really sent from the Father, spake in his Name, worked by his Power, and was no Deceiver: Ver. 32, Many good Works have I showed you from my Father. Ver. 36, Say ye of Him whom the Father hath sanctified and sent into the World, Thou Blasphemest, because I said, I am the Son of God? And ver. 37, 38, If I do not the Works of my Father, [if my Works are not really such, as prove me to be sent of Cod, and to act by his Power,] believe me not: But if I do, though ye believe not Me, believe the Works; that ye may know and believe, that the Father is in Me, and I in Him.

These last words, [the Father is in Me, and I in Him.] are of the same import with those fore-going, ver. 30, I and my Father are One. And the Meaning of Both is fully explained, as by the Whole Scope of our Saviour's Discourse in this place, so by those other parallel Expressions, ch. 6, 56, He that eateth my Flesh and drinketh my Blood, dwelleth in Me, and K4 I in

151

152

A Commentary

I in Him, [even as the Father dwelleth in me, and I in the Father; So some MSS have it.] Ch. 14 10, Believe thou not, that I am in the Father, and the Father in Me? The words that I speak unto you, I speak not of my self; but the Father, that dwelleth in me, He doth the Works: Believe me, that I am in the Father, and the Father in Me; or else believe Me for the very Works sake. Ver. 20, At that day ye shall know, that I am in my Father, and You in Me, and I in you. Ch. 15, 4, Abide in Me, and I in you. Ch. 17, 11, Holy Father, keep through thine own Name, those whom thou hast given me; that They may be One, as We are. Ver. 21, That they All may be One, as Thou, Father, art in Me, and I in Thee; that They also may be One in Us; that the World may believe that Thou hast sent me. Ver. 22, And the Glory which Thou gavest Me, I have given Them; that They may be One, even as We are One. Ver. 23, I in Them, and Thou in Me, that They may be made perfect in One, and that the World may know that Thou hast sent Me, and hast loved Them as Thou hast loved Me. Ver. 26, That the Love wherewith thou hast loved Me, may be in Them, and I in Them. 1 Joh. 3, 24, He that keepeth his Commandments, dwelleth in Him, and He in Him. 1 Joh. 4, 15, Whosoever shall confess that Jesus is the Son of God, God dwelleth

cn John 10; 30, 33. leth in Him, and He in God. And ver. 16, God is Love; And He that dwelleth in Love, dwelleth in God, and God in Him.

I conclude This Head with a Passage out of Tertullian; who, though a Montanist, and One, who in this very Book against Praxeas affords your Friend more Citations to his Purpose, than are to be found in all the other Writers of the Three First Centuries put together, yet Thus commenteth

the Text we are Now upon. Concerning his Sheep also our Lord says, that none could take them out of his hand. For my Father, who gave them me, is Greater than All; and I and the Father are One. One Thing, he says; not One Person. For, One Thing, in the neuter Gender, does not express Identity, but Union, Likeness, Conjunction, the Love of the Father towards the Son, and the Obedience of the Son to the Will of his Father. When he says, I and the Father are One; he shows that they are Two Persons, whom he so joys equally together. For the same reason,

153

De ovibus etiam suis, quod nemo illas de manu ejus eriperet. Pater enim quod mihi dedit, majus est omnibus ; &, Ego for Pater Unum sumus. — Unum sumus, non, Unus sumus. — Unum dicit, neutrali verbo; qued non pertinet ad Singularitatem, sed ad Unitatem, ad Similitudinem, ad Conjunctionem; ad Dilectionem Patris, qui Filium diligit; & ad obsequium Filii, qui voluntati Patris obsequitur. Unum sumus, dicens, Ego for Pater; ostendit duos esse, quos aquat & jungit. Adeo addit etiam mulra se opera a Patre ostendisse, quorum nihil lapidari mereretur. Et ne putarent ideo se illum lapidare debere, quasi se Deum ipsum, id est Patrem, voluisset intelligi; quia djxerat, Ego for Pater Unum sumus; qua Filium Dei Deum ostendens, non qua iplum Deum : Si in lege, inquit, scriptum est, Ego dixi, vos diii estis; & non potest be.

154

solvi Scriptura; quem Pater sanctificavit, & misit in mundum, vos eum blasphemare dicitis, quia dixi, Filius Dei sum? Si non facio opera Patris mei, nolite credere; si vero facio, & Mihi credere non vultis, vel propter opera credite: Et scirote qudd ego in Patre sim, & Pater in Me. Per opera ergo erat pater in filio, & filius in Patre; & ita per opera intelligimus Unum esse Patrem & Filium. Adeo totum hoc perseverabat inducere, ut duo tamen crederentur in una virtute; quia aliter Filius credi non posset, nifi duo crederentur. Adverf.Praxeam. c. 22.

## A Commentary

be adds also that he had shown them many Works from the Father, for none of which be deserved to be Stoned. And least they should think he deserved to be Stoned, as making himself to be God Himself, that is, the Father; by saying, that He and the Father were One; (by which he meant that He was God as being The Son of God, not as being GOD HIMSELF;) he therefore adds further, saying; If it is

written in the Law, I said ye are Gods, and the Scripture cannot be broken; say ye of Him whom the Father hath sanctified and sent into the World, Thou blasphemeest, because I said I am the Son of God? If I do not the Works of my Father, believe me not : But if I do, and ye believe not Me, believe me for the very Works sake: And know, that I am in the Father, and the Father in Me. By the Works therefore was the Father in the Son, and the Son in the Father; and so we understand the Father and the Son to be One, by the Works. Thus the whole of our Lords Discourse leads us to believe, that though they are One in Power, they are nevertheless Two Persons: Because otherwise it could not

b.2

on John 12; 41.

be believed there was a Son, if it was not believed there were Two Persons.

Thus also Novatian: If Christ (faith he) had been the Father, as the Hereticks imagine; he would have said, I and my Father Am One, [Unus, One Person.] But One, in the Neuter Gender, [Unum, One Thing,] signifies Agreement of Fellowship, not Unity of Person. So that the Father and Son are One Thing, by Agreement and Love. The Apostle Paul also takes notice of this Unity of

Agreement, with a Difference of Persons. He that planteth, faith be, and he that watereth, are One, [One Thing.] Now every body knows, that yet Apollos was One Man, and Paul another, and not Paul and Apollos One and the Same Man.

Si erat, ut heretiet putant, pater Christus, oportuit dicere Ego & Pater Unus Sum.—Unum neutraliter positum, societatis concordiam, non unitatem personæ sonat. ut merito Unum sit pater & filius, per concordiam & per amorem. Novit hanc concordiae unitatem & Apostolus Paulus, cum personarum tamen distinctione. qui plantat, & qui rigat unum sunt. 156

## A Commentary

When Isaiah in his Vifion saw the Glory of God, he foresaw the Glory wherein Christ was to be revealed: And the Glory it self which he Then saw, being the Visible Image of the Invisible God, was (in the unanimous Opinion of All the Ancient Fathers) the Son representing the Person of the Invisible Father. See my Scripture-doctrine, Part I. No 597, 616.

Against This, (which is very plain and intelligible, as well as the constant Doctrine of the Ancients,) your learned Friend alleges neither Arguments of Reason, nor the Authority of Antiquity. He adds only a remote and very obscure Discourse, concerning Christ's having a Name and Power and Glory of his own, as well as his Fathers; which is very true, but not to the purpose: Concerning a personal, but not real, difference of Glory; which is a Distinction very dark and obscure: Concerning a Name derived from the Father with the Person of the Son, referred to in the Form of Baptism; as if, being baptised into the Name of Christ, was not being baptised into the Profession of his Religion, but into the Name itself taken (as they speak) technically, materially, or cabbalistically: Concerning an Unity of Essence inferred from an Unity of Essential Glory, and an Unity of Glory from an Unity of Essence; which

on John 12, 41.

which is a part of Metaphysicks very hard to be understood, and which the Scripture never meddles with: Lastly, concerning Two Persons represented by One Glory or Appearance, (which yet is not the Cafe, but, on the contrary, One Person's being the Glory or Representation of Another, the Visible representing the Invisible;) which, he says, 'tis natural to conclude is with this Design, that we should believe the Two Persons to be One Being; Which, I think, is a manifest Contradiction in Terms.

And here therefore at length your Friend joyns issue with me. The most remarkable thing (he says) in the Learned Doctor's Note upon the Text, and which I take to be the Key of his whole Book, is his confounding Individual Being and Person, as if they were Terms of the same Import; and then ranking those among the Followers of Sabellius, who hold the Father and Son to be One and the same Individual Being. Well then: If a Person be not an Intelligent Agent, and an Intelligent Agent an $^{*}$ Individual Intelligent Being, (which is the common and natural Notion

* Res singularis per se subfiftens, in rebus intelleftu praditis, idem est quod persona : An individual Thing [or Being,] subfifting by itself. (says the learned Bishop Bull.) is, in things induced with Understanding, the same as Person. Defenf. Self. 2, cap. 9, §. 11. See also a large Passage of Justin

157

p. 16. 74.

158

## A Commentary

Notion All Mankind have of a Person ;) will your Friend tell us what a Person, in His Sense, is; and what the Scripture means, when it continually represents the Son to us as an Intelligent Agent? Will he tell us what Such a Person is, which is no Being; or how Modes of Substistence, mere abstract Notions, can be Intelligent Agents, without having so much as any real Existence at all? Or, of what Benefit can it possibly be, to make Use of words which have no Signification? Your Friend cannot here reply, that these things are a Mystery. For we are not now speaking about the

Justin Martyr in the latter part of his Dialogue with Trypho; where, speaking against those, who taught that the Son [ἀτμιστον χὰ ἄχωεισον τῶ πάτερος τυπιν τὴν δυναμνὸν πελεχεν. οὐπερ τεγπον τοῦ ἡλιὴ φασι φώς εἰπὸ γὰς είναι ἀτμιστον χὰ ἄχωεisinον σύνταῖν συναφοφεηται τοῦ φώς] was only a Power emitted from the Father, so as not to be really distinct from him, in like manner as Men say the Light of the Sun is upon Earth, yet so as not to be a real Distinct thing from the Sun in the Heavens, but, when the Sun sets, the Light also goes away with it; he, on the contrary, explains his Own Opinion to be, that as Angels are permanent Beings, and not mere Powers, so the Son, whom the Scripture calls [Θεοὺν χὰ Αγγελον] both God and an Angel, [ὃχώς τοῦ ἡλιὴ φασι φώσμα μόνου αειμίται, αλλά η αειμίτω έπειν πέσαι,] is not, like the Light of the Sun, a mere Name (or Power,] but a really distinct Being, begotten from the Father by his Power and Will; not by Division, as if the Father's Substance could be parted, as all corporeal things are divided and parted, and thereby become different from what they were before Part was taken from them; but as One Fire is lighted from another, [so as to be really distinct from it,] and yet the former suffers thereby no Diminution.

Words

on John 12; 41.

Words of God, which are the Expressions of Scripture; but about the words of Men, who can never be commended for speaking mysteriously. Not to say, that plain Contradictions are in reality no more mysterious than the plainest Truths.

We must then be content to hear what your Friend, without having any distinct Notion of his own, what a Person is; has to object against My Notion of it, which is the common and natural Notion of All Mankind who never learnt the Jargon of the Schools. Why; "if God (faith be) be [οἱ ωὴν] Being, and God be individually One, and Being individually One; and the Son, no less than the Father, be True God, as has been already proved; then must the Father and the Son be One and the same Individual Being, that is, there must be Two Persons in One and the same Individual Being". The Conclusion, [Two Persons in One and the same Individual Uncompounded Being,] is an express Contradiction; and therefore the Premises must of necessity be Faulty somewhere. And indeed the Fault is very evident. God is not only [οἱ ωὴν] individually One Being, but also [οἱ ωὴν] individually One Intelligent Agent or Person, as has been shown above, No 1. And therefore if the Son's being True God, proves him

159

pag. 74.

160

## A Commentary

him to be o' œv, it will prove him to be, not only the same individual Being, but also the same individual Person with the Father. Which is more than your Friend intended to infer. And therefore the Son's being True God, must not be understood to signify that he is o' œv, but that He has True Divinity (which False Gods have not,) communicated to him from Him who is Alone o' œv. But your Friend proceeds:

"Since (faith be) the One True God is but One individual Being; and the Father is This One True God; the Son, if he is a distinct individual Being from the Father, must be distinct from the One True God, that is, be no True God himself at all; Which is contrary to 1 John. 5, 20". (And This also is Dr Wells's chief Argument: Letter to Dr Clarke, pag. 59.) I answer: That passage of St John, is not spoken of the Son; as I have shown above, No 14. But if it was, still it would follow, that the Son was True God, not by being himself the Father, the Self-existent Being; but by having True Divinity and Dominion communicated to him from Him, as I before said.

on Acts 7; 30, 31, 32.

# N° 28.

ACTS VII; 30, 31, 32. There appeared to him—an Angel of the Lord, in a Flame of Fire in the Bush. And the Voice of the Lord came unto him, saying, I am the God of thy Fathers, the God of Abraham, and the God of Isaac, and the God of Jacob.

Justin Martyr and all the Antient Fathers observe upon this place, that the same Person is here stiled both an Angel and God. From whence they infer, that the Person here appearing was Christ, (the Angel of the Covenant, Mal. 3, 1; the Angel of Gods presence, II. 63, 9; and in whom the Name of God [the Authority and Power of the Father] was, Exod. 23, 21;) speaking in the Person of the Invisible Father. Thus Gen. 16, 10, The ANGEL of the Lord said unto her, I will multiply thy Seed exceedingly. Again, Gen. 31; 11, 13, The ANGEL of God spake unto me in a Dream, saying,——I am the GOD of Bethel, where——thou vowedst a Vow unto me. And ch. 48, 15, Jacob blessed Joseph, and said; GOD, before whom my Fathers Abraham and Isaac did walk, the GOD which

162

A Commentary

which fed me all my Life long unto this day, the ANGEL which Redeemed me from all Evil, bless the Lads. [And Rev. 11; 1, 3, even an inferior Angel is introduced speaking in the Name or Perfon of God ; The Angel stood, saying ;——I will give Power unto MY two witnesses, c. ]

What your Friend alleges upon this Head; viz. that Christ is not excluded from being the God of the Patriarchs, (pag. 76;) that Christ is the God of Abraham, and the God of Isaac, and the God of Jacob, (pag. 76;) that Christ is the Person who spake to Moses, and called himself the God of the Patriarchs, (pag. 77;) that Christ is stiled Hebovah, in distinction from the Person of the Father, [which yet may be controverted,] Gen. 19, 24, (pag. 78;) All This is in no wise contrary to any thing I have asserted. But then the Inference he draws, viz. that therefore the Son with the Father is the Necessary Being it self; This, as it is an express Contradiction, so it is also directly contrary both to the Text, and to the Opinion of all the Antient Fathers: See my Scripture-doctrine, Part I, No. 597 & 616. I shall mention here but one place, which expresses the Sense of all the rest: The Son, (says the Synod

on Acts. 7; 30, 31, 32.

Synod of Antioch,) is sometimes called an Angel, sometimes the Lord, sometimes God. For it is Impious to imagine that the Supreme God of the Universe, is any where called an Angel. But the Angel [or Messenger] of

the Father, is the Son; who Himself is Lord and God. For it is written; The Angel of his Great Counsel [or Covenant.]

The only Argument your Friend alleges for his Opinion in this place, is, that the Name Jehovah is incommunicable to any Being but the True God, (pag. 79 & 83.) Which is very True, if thereby be meant that it cannot be given to any False God. But that it may be and is given to Him who is the Messenger and Representative of the Only True God, (who consequently is Himself True God, and yet not He who upon account of his Self-existence and Supreme underived Dominion is stiled by way of Eminence The One True God;) is expressly affirmed in the Text, Exod. 23, 21, My Name (or Authority) is in Him. [Yet it may perhaps be worth taking Notice of in this place, that though the Name Jehovah is in the Old Testament given to That visible Person, who appeared as representing the Person of the invisible God; yet in the New Testament, where Christ appears in L 2 his

## A Commentary

his own Person, That Name [the True Rendring of which, feels to be, οὐν χή οὐν χή οὐν εργμένου, He which is and was and is to come,] is never given to him, any more than the Title [παντοκεύπτωρ] Supreme over All; but he is stiled only κυμεῖς and θεώς, which are the words by which the Septuagint render ישראל and ישראל, which they always read and translate instead of the Proper Name יהודע.

What he further offers upon this Head, (pag. 79;) that the Name Jehovah, or the Divine Virtue of the Name, did not lodge in the Word, as in a distinct Subject, but was the very Word itself; That (pag. 81,) the Son revealing the Father, is the very Name, and not the bare Subject in which it dwells; That (pag. 82,) since the Apostle tells us there is none other Name under Heaven (but the Name of Jesus Christ) given among Men, whereby we must be saved; and it is certain the Name by which the Church is to be saved according to the Prophecies of the Old Testament, is the Name Jehovah; it follows therefore that the Name Jesus, and the Name Jehovah, are One: All This, is to Me utterly unintelligible.

Page 80. The Father and the Word (he says) are the One Object of Jewish Worship, or the Lord God who is but One, Deut. 6,4. But I think the contrary is plain in the Texts now before us; viz. that the Father

on Acts 7; 30, 31, 32.

only was the Object of the Jews Worship, manifesting himself unto them by his Son, the Angel of his Covenant.

Page 81 & 82. The Son manifesting the Father, is (he says) the same Object of Divine Worship to the Christians, as God the Creator was to the Gentiles: —Otherwise the Christians in their New Dispensation, which is a plain Improvement of the State of Nature, would have a Name to trust in, of lesser Efficacy, than the very Gentiles. As if Christians did not trust in God the Father, the Creator of all things; as well as the Gentiles, who knew him much more imperfectly; Besides that Christians have moreover, (whom the Gentiles knew not,) an Advocate with the Father, Jesus Christ the righteous, who is their Saviour, Redeemer, and Intercessor.

Lastly, he sums up his Argument, (pag. 82;) Therefore the Son manifesting the Father, is One with God the Creator, since the True God is but One: And (pag. 83,) If then Jehovah be incommunicable to any but the True God, and the True God is but One, and Jesus Christ is Jehovah; Jesus Christ is the True God. To which I answer, as before: Christ is, and is rightly called, True God, by having True Divinity and Dominion communicated to him from the Father; not by being (what your Friend's Argument would prove, if it proved any thing.)

That He [Chrift] might be the Lord both of the Dead and Living.——We shall all stand before the Judgment-Seat of Christ : For it is written ; As I live, faith the Lord, every knee shall bow to me, and every tongue shall confess to God : So then every one of us, shall give account of himself to God.

How the Scripture is to be understood, when it declares in some places that God will judge the World, and in others that Christ shall judge the World; appears (I said) from such Passages, wherein Both are mentioned together. As Acts 17,31, God bath appointed a day, wherein HE will judge the World in Righteousness, BY that Man whom he bath ordained : And Rom.2, 16, God shall judge the Secrets of Men by Jesus Christ.

Your

on Rom. 14; 9, 10, 11, 12.

Your learned Friend alleges, that, not only God will judge the World by Jesus Christ, but Jesus Christ himself also is both Lord and God. I acknowledge, and have shown at large, that he is so. But your Friend, not content with This, laboureth further to introduce an unaccountable Confusion of Persons; by arguing, that when St Paul says, ver. 6, He that eateth, eateth to the Lord, and giveth God thanks; and He that eateth not, to the Lord he eateth not, and giveth God thanks; it Seems, and it is Reasonable (he thinks) to Suppose, that the word, God, in each of these Sentences, signifies Christ. But This is so directly contrary to the whole Style of the New Testament, that he is not very Consident of his Assertion: And therefore he tells us it is yet more probable, if we consider, that when St Paul faith of a weak Brother, ver. $^{3}$ of this Chapter, God has received him; he afterwards expresses the same thing thus, ch. 15, 7, Christ also has received us to the Glory of God; "putting Christ (says your Friend) in the place of God, as if He were comprehended in that Name". Whereas indeed, had St Paul written on purpose to prevent that Confusion of Persons which your Friend here labours to introduce, he could not possibly have expresst himself more distinctly, than in these very

L 4 words:

Your learned Friend alleges, that, not only God will judge the World by Jesus Christ, but Jesus Christ himself also is both Lord and God. I acknowledge, and have shown at large, that he is so. But your Friend, not content with This, laboureth further to introduce an unaccountable Confusion of Persons; by arguing, that when St Paul says, ver. 6, He that eateth, eateth to the Lord, and giveth God thanks; and He that eateth not, to the Lord he eateth not, and giveth God thanks; it Seems, and it is Reasonable (he thinks) to Suppose, that the word, God, in each of these Sentences, signifies Christ. But This is so directly contrary to the whole Style of the New Testament, that he is not very Confident of his Assertion: And therefore he tells us it is yet more probable, if we consider, that when St Paul faith of a weak Brother, ver. $^{3}$ of this Chapter, God has received him; he afterwards expresses the same thing thus, ch. 15, 7, Christ also has received us to the Glory of God; "putting Christ (says your "Friend) in the place of God, as if He "were comprehended in that Name". Whereas indeed, had St Paul written on purpose to prevent that Confusion of Persons which your Friend here labours to introduce, he could not possibly have expret himself more distinctly, than in these very

L 4 words:

168

A Commentary

words; CHRIST also has received us, to the Glory of GOD. But he proceeds : What St Paul says, We shall all stand before the Judgment-Seat of CHRIST; is expressed by Isaiah thus, every Tongue shall confess to GOD; and immediately after by St Paul also himself, Every one of us shall give account of himself to GOD: And what follows from hence? why, either that CHRIST and GOD are one and the same Person; (which though your Friend seems not to mean, yet he plainly infers it, pag. 87;) or else, (which one would think should not be hard to understand,) that giving Account to Christ, by whom God judges the World, is the same thing as giving Account to God, who judges the World by Christ.

The Pallage your Friend here cites out of Irenaus, I cannot forbear transcribing; because it is as directly against him, as 'tis possible for words to be : That every Knee

on Heb. 13, 8.

# $\mathrm{N}^{0}$ 3c.

HEB. XIII, 8. Jesus Christ, the same T e- rsterday, and to day, and for ever.

That the Person of Christ, is the same yesterday, and to day, and for ever; is certainly True, but not the Meaning of This Text. For the Apostle is here speaking, not of the Person, but of the Doctrine of Christ.

Of the Two Arguments your Learned Friend alleges for understanding these words concerning the Person of Christ, the first is the Context or Connexion of the words. The Apostle in the preceding Verse, encourages Christians to imitate their own Pastors in the Constancy of their Faith; the Object of which was Jesus Christ, who was no Person of a modern Date, but the same Yesterday, and to day, and for ever. Thus your Learned Friend. But Where now is the Force of This Argument? Imitate your own Pastors, because the Person of Christ is always the same. The Context, I think, plainly shows the other to be the True Sense: Adhere to the Faith of the Apostles who first instructed you, and be not carried about with divers new Doctrines; For the Doctrine of Christ is always One and the same,

169

In the Scripturedrine, N° 662. In the Answer, pag. 88.

pag. 89.

170

A Commentary

same, and cannot be changed by Men. This is a very Clear, and a very good Argument. I set down the Words themselves, and leave the Reader to judge which is the Truer Interpretation : Remember (fays the Apostle) Them which have the rule over you, who have spoken unto you the word of God; whose Faith follow, considering the End of their Conversation : Jesus Christ, the same yesterday, and to day, and for ever: Be not carried about with divers and strange Doctrines.

The second Argument your Friend urges, to prove that the Person of Christ is here meant, and not his Doctrine; is a new rendring of the Words of the Text, which he

pag. 88. thinks should rather be translated thus; Jesus Christ, yesterday, and to day, and for ever, He. For the Cabbalistick Jews (he says) placed [ἀνε] Among the Divine Names. As to the Cabbalistick Men, I acknowledge there is no Arguing with Them: For who can fight with a Shadow? or who can reason with the Phrenzies of a mystical Imagination? But to your Friend himself, I answer; that the Words of the Original cannot possibly bear His Translation. For the Word is not [ὁωις] He, but [οῦωις] The Same; Which Two Words, are never put one for the other. And therefore his rendring, in the two Passages which he cites out of Origen, the Words, Σύ οῦωις, Thou art He, instead of, Thou art the

same,

on Mark 13, 32.

171

same, was a Defect of Skill in the Greek Language.

## N° 31.

MARK XIII, 32, But of That day and In the hour knoweth no Man, no, not the Angels Scripture which are in Heaven, neither the Son, N° 773. but the Father; [but my Father only, In the Answer, pag. Matt. 24, 36.]

I think the Intention of our Saviour in this Passage, is to declare, that, as the Father Alone is ['Aυτοῦε] God unoriginate and of Himself; and ['Αυτοαγαὶν] The Alone Underived Fountain of Goodness; upon which account it is affirmed in Scripture, that There is None Good, but One, that is God the Father, as alnoft All Primitive Writers unanimously cite That Text, (see above, No 1, at the latter End:) And as He is also the Alone Fountain of All Power, infomuch that our Lord says concerning himself, The Son can do nothing of himself, Joh. V, 19 & 30: So He is likewise the Alone Fountain of All Knowledge, infomuch that no one knows any thing, no not even the Son himself, but by communication from Him.

## A Commentary

Words of the Text itself, he differs not much from me.

His Comment upon it, is nothing but an Inquiry, in eight very dark and obscure Pages, what the particular Manner was of Christ's devesting himself of his former Glory. The Circumstances whereof not being revealed to us, 'tis in vain to indeavour to explain them by Philosophical Conjectures, and uncertain Metaphysical Speculations. Sufficient it is, that the Thing itself is revealed very clearly and intelligibly; that That Divine Person, who had before appeared as Lord of All, exercising the Dominion of the Invisible Father, and being thereby in the Form of God; in the fulness of time condececended, in Obedience to the Good Pleasure of his Father, and with his own Free Will and Consent, to take upon him our Humane Flesh, and appear for our fakes in the Form of a Servant, as a Man of like Infirmities with Other Men, mean and humble and subjected even to Death.

Lastly, He tells us, pag. 109, 110,

111, (out of Athenagoras, Theophilus and

Tatian, the Antient Founders of Sabellianism,) that God the Father is an eternal Mind; and because an eternal Mind can never be supposed to be void of Thought, it was eternally Thoughtful, or the Thought co-existent with the Mind: That this eternal Thought came forth as a Voice, uttered and spoken forth in order to create; and is the Son of God: That since the Thought of such a Mind, must be the Off-spring of the Whole Person of God the Father; therefore the Thought or Word coming forth to create, must have been as Real and Personal a Subsistence before, as it was after the coming forth; and after his coming forth, when he was doubtless a Person, he was still the very same Thought, that eternally existed in the Mind of God; which evidently infers him to have been a Person before. Now All This is, in reality, nothing else but playing with the equivocal Signification of words. For, of Necessity, One only of these Two Notions can be True: Either the Term [λόγος, the Word,] signifies in Scripture [That λόγος which is first ἐνδιαγένετο, and M. thefti

177

178

A Commentary

then කාර්යාලිය] the internal Reason of God, and his external Word spoken forth, represented only figuratively as a Person; and then, in reality, it is No person at all, but Christ will be only (according to the Socinian way of Explication) a mere Man, in whom dwells the Wisdom of the Father; (Which Notion was of old condemned in Photinus at the Council of Sirmium, and amounts in the whole to the very same with the Sabellian Hereby:) Or else, on the other side, the Term [කාර්යාලිය, the Word] signifies in Scripture a real divine Person by whom God acts and reveals himself to his Creatures, represented only figuratively as the Word or Wisdom of the Father; and then, in reality, he is not at all [the metaphysical කාර්යාලිය, කාර්යාලිය] the internal Wisdom of the Father, or his Word spoken forth, but the PERSON by whom the Father's Commands are executed and his Wisdom and Will is Manifested and Revealed; Upon which Account, his Name is called the WORD of God, Rev. 19, 13. Your Learned Friend joins these two Notions together; making that which is literal, to be at the same time figurative; and that which is only figurative, to be at the same time literal also: Which is a manifest Confusion and Impossibility. The latter of the Two Notions I now mentioned, is (I think) the more agreeable to the New Testament.

on Col. 1, 15.

179

Testament. The Son of God is That Divine Person, by whom the Wisdom of the Father is manifested, and his Will revealed to all his Creatures; And upon That Account (as I observed,) His Name (says the Scripture) is called The WORD of God, ἀγλείτην ὑνομας ἄπτῶν, ὁ λόγος τὸ θεῶν, Rev. 19, 13. And if the Gospel of St John was written after the Revelation, (as is not improbable;) then the Reason of Christ's being called The Word, or That Word, in the first Verse of that Gospel, is the same with regard to this passage in the Revelation; as his being so frequently stiled in the Gospels, The Son of Man, or That Son of Man, is with regard to the person so stiled and described in the prophecy of Daniel, ch. 7, 13. Now because the word [λόγος] happens also to signify, Reason; hence the Authors your Friend cites, conclude, that Christ is literally the Reason or Wisdom of the Father; and, by Consequence, no Person at all. There are several other Passages in the New Testament, wherein Christ is called likewise the Power and the Righteousness of God: Very elegantly and emphatically; because by Him is manifested the Power, and by him is set forth in a most conspicuous manner The Righteousness [διαγγοσυν, πρυχ, The Mercy] of the Father. From hence the same Authors might have argued.

argued,

180

## A Commentary

argued, in like manner as in the other case, that the Son of God is literally That Attribute which is called the Power, and That Attribute which is called The Mercy of God. And by the same Argument that they infer in the other case, that the Father considered distinctly without the Son, would be [αλο-γεὺς ἐπὶ αφθ] a Being void of Reason and Wisdom; by the same Argument they might here likewise have inferred, that the Father considered distinctly without the Son, would be a Being void of Power and void of Mercy. And then all Men would have plainly seen the weakness of their Reasoning.

# $\mathrm{N}^{\circ}$ 36.

LUKE IV, 18. The Spirit of the Lord is in the upon Me, because He hath anointed me to preach the Gospel to the Poor. Scripture doctrine, N° 1005.

Your learned Friend says not a word upon this Text; but only unfortunately cites one passage out of Irenæus, upon which he makes a wrong Observation. Requiescebat Spiritus Dei super Eum; The Spirit of God (faith Irenæus) rested upon him, viz. upon Christ. "EUM, (says "your Learned Friend,) the Man, not the "Word". He had forgot, it seems, that he was citing a Latin Translation; and that Irenæus wrote in Greek, in which Both the words [λῶν and αὐτῶντος] are equally Masculine. But This is of no great Moment: For in the whole passage, he does indeed represent Irenæus's Sense rightly; though he mistook in judging where the Emphasis of the Expresfion lay.

# $\mathrm{N}^{\circ}$ 37.

ACTS XXVIII, 25, 26, 27, Well spake the Holy Ghost by Isaiah the Prophet unto our Fathers, saying, Go unto this people, and say c.——and I should heal them.

The Meaning of This Text, I said, seemed to Me to be This: Esaias, by the Revelation of the Holy Ghost, saw God sitting upon the Throne of his Glory, and heard him saying Go &c. Esai. 6; 1, 9. Thus St John in the Apocalypse, being in the Spirit, (Rev. 1, 10,) saw God sitting upon his Throne, and beheld Christ in his Glory. And the words which Christ himself spake, (Rev. ch. 2, & ch. 3,) are in the very same Chapters said to be what the Spirit faith unto the Churches. So here likewise, what Esaias in the Spirit heard God speaking, is said to be spoken by the Holy Ghost. Whatever God speaks, may very properly be said to be spoken by the Holy Ghost, because God always speaks to his Prophets by the Inspiration of his Holy Spirit: And whatever is spoken by the Holy Ghost, may no less properly be said to be spoken by God, because 'tis spoken to the Prophets by That Spirit which God has

---

on Acts 28: 25, 26, 27. has given them. In the Revelation, ch. II, ver. I, 3, even an inferiour Angel is introduced as speaking in the Perfon of God : The Angel stood, saying ; I will give Power unto MY two Witnesses, &c.

Your learned Friend fairly acknowledges, that the Glory which appeared to Isaiah, ch. 6, is the Glory of the Father; even the same which appeared to St John, Rev. 4; 2, 8. But the same Glory (he says) is also called the Glory of the Son, Joh. 12, 41: How That Text is to be understood, I have shown above, in my Note upon the place, No 27. And the same Glory, (your Friend goes on,) is, in the place now before us, said to be the Glory of the Holy Ghost: That's very wonderful; for there is not one Syllable about Glory, either in the Text or Context. But he adds his reason for what he affirms; For the Glory or Lord, (faith be,) which spake to the Prophet, is called here the Holy Ghost: But That cannot be; because neither in the Old Testament nor in the New, is the Holy Ghost at any time stiled Lord; but, on the contrary, the Prophets are always represented as seeing those Visions by the Inspiration of the Holy Ghost, in which Visions they hear God or the Lord speaking. However, from hence your Friend collects with great Probability, (so he expresses himself,

183

184

## A Commentary

himself, much more modestly than many Writers,) the Unity or Identity of Nature of the Three Persons. But the Confusion that appears in This Expression, shows there is not That Clearness there should be in his Notion. For though between Two Individuals there may be an UNITY of Nature, as Two Men are (ομοθισιοι) of the same Common humane Nature; yet Two Individuals cannot, without an express Contradiction, have an IDENTITY of Nature; as Two Persons cannot be (ταυπτεσοι or μονθεσιιοι) of the same Individual or Identical Nature, because Then One of those Two Persons would be the same Person which the Other of them is.

The Passages he cites out of the Jewish Rabbies, (if they prove any thing,) prove either that those Rabbies took the Holy Ghost to be only another Title for God the Father himself, or else that they understood him to be a Person speaking in the Name of God the Father. And if there be any places in the Scripture itself, wherein the Spirit of God seems to be put figuratively for God himself, in like manner as the Spirit of a Man signifies the Man himself; yet such places do not at all favour your Friend's Hypothesis, but much rather the Sabellian or Socinian Notion; 'till, by being compared with other parallel places, their True Meaning

E A.

## on Acts 28; 25, 26, 27.

Meaning appears to be That which I have already set forth in my Explication of the Text at present before us.

The Texts in the second and third Chapters of the Revelation, where the words of Christ are said to be what the Spirit faith unto the Churches, are thus explained by your Friend: Because (faith he) the One Glory of the Father, Son, and Holy Ghost, was manifested in the Humane Nature of Christ, though the Word only was personally united to it; are the words of Christ attributed to the Spirit. To Me, the Reason of This Expression [what the SPIRIT faith unto the Churches,] seems rather to be This; that 'twas by being in the Spirit, (ch. 1, 10,) 'twas by the Inspiration of the Spirit, that St John saw That Vision, wherein Christ was represented as speaking to him those words. Which of these two Interpretations is the more reasonable and intelligible, must be left to the Reader to judge.

The Testimonies of Fathers, which your Friend alleges to confirm his Explication of the Text before us, (SOME Antient Writers, he calls them ;) the Reader must not suppose to be Citations out of Antient Writers indeed. For they are only two Writers at the Latter End of the Fourth Century. The more Antient Writers, always

JOH. III, 5. Except a Man be born----of the Spirit, he cannot enter into the Kingdom of God.

To be Born of the Spirit, is, according to the same Evangelist, to be Born of God: It is probable then, (says your learned Friend,) that God and the Spirit were believed by Him to be One. But the Scripture always speaks more distinctly, and without

## on John 3, 5.

187

without any such confusion of Perfons. Rom. 8; 11, 14, If the Spirit of him that raised up Jesus from the dead, dwell in you; he that raised up Christ from the dead, shall also quicken your mortal Bodies, by his Spirit that dwelleth in you: For as many as are led by the Spirit of God, they are the Sons of God. 1 Joh. 4; 12, 13, God dwelleth in us, and his Love is perfected in us; hereby know we that we dwell in him, and be in us, because he hath given us of his Spirit. This is very distinct and intelligible: But, according to your Friend's Argument, the Spirit of God will be the Spirit of Himself; Which is hard to be understood.

The Passage of Nemesianus a Thubunis, which your Friend cites upon this occasion, is (what He was not aware of) a Corruption introduced in the Latin Church into the Text itself of the Verse next following That which we are now upon. Job. 3, 6: That which is born of the Flesh, is Flesh; and That which is born of the Spirit, is Spirit; For God is the Spirit, and such a Person is therefore born of God. These are the words your Friend cites from Nemesianus: But they are indeed (what He did not perceive,) the very words of the Text itself, as it was corruptly read by Nemesianus and Tertullian, and some other Latin Fathers. ways speak in a different manner. Thus Justin Martyr : When in the Writings of the Prophets (faith he) you find a Person introduced speaking, do not then think that the things are spoken by the Inspired Persons themselves, but by the Divine word [the Prophetick Spirit he calls it presently after,]

which moves them. For sometimes it [viz. the Prophetick Spirit] directly foretells things to come; Sometimes it speaks, as in the Person of God the Supreme Lord and Father of All; And sometimes it speaks, as in the Person of Christ.

JOH. III, 5. Except a Man be born——of the Spirit, he cannot enter into the Kingdom of God.

To be Born of the Spirit, is, according to the same Evangelist, to be Born of God: It is probable then, (says your learned Friend,) that God and the Spirit were believed by Him to be One. But the Scripture always speaks more distinctly, and without

## on John 3, 5.

without any fuch confusion of Persons. Rom. 8; 11, 14, If the Spirit of him that raised up Jesus from the dead, dwell in you; he that raised up Christ from the dead, shall also quicken your mortal Bodies, by his Spirit that dwelleth in you: For as many as are led by the Spirit of God, they are the Sons of God. 1 Joh. 4; 12, 13, God dwelleth in us, and his Love is perfected in us; hereby know we that we dwell in him, and be in us, because he hath given us of his Spirit. This is very distinct and intelligible: But, according to your Friend's Argument, the Spirit of God will be the Spirit of Himself; Which is hard to be underftood.

The Passage of Nemesianus a Thubunis, which your Friend cites upon this occasion, is (what He was not aware of) a Corruption introduced in the Latin Church into the Text itself of the Verse next following That which we are now upon. Job. 3, 6 : That which is born of the Flesh, is Flesh ; and That which is born of the Spirit, is Spirit ; For God is the Spirit, and such a Person is therefore born of God. These are the words your Friend cites from Nemesianus : But they are indeed (what He did not perceive,) the very words of the Text itself, as it was corruptly read by Nemesianus and Tertullian, and some other Latin Fathers. Ambrose, with some very remarkable

187

188

A Commentary

able Circumstances worth your Friend's Notice, cites the corrupt words a little other-

Quod natum est ex Carne,

Caro est; quia de Carne natum est; Et quod natum est de Spiritu, Spiritus est; QUI A DEUS SPIRIFUSEST.

ambrof. de Spiritu Sancto,

ib. 3, cap. 11.

wife: For He reads the Text in the following manner : That which is born of the Flesh, is Flesh; BECAUSE IT IS BORN OF THE FLESH: And That which is born of the Spirit, is Spirit ; BECAUSE THE SPIRIT IS GOD. So Ambrose in his Comment understands the interpolated words. For Thus

Quem locum ita expresse Ariani testificamini esse de Spiritu, ut eum de vestris codicibus auferatis : Atq; utinam de vestris, & non etiam de Ecclesiae codicibus tolleretis. Et fortafse hoc etiam in Oriente fecistis. Et literas quidem potuiftis abolere ; sed fidem non potuiftis auferre. ibid.

he argues : This place (fays he) ye Arians do so expressly testify to be spoken of the Spirit, that ye take it away out of your Copies ; And I wish ye struck it out of your own Copies only, and not out of the Books of the Church. [the Church of Milan.] And perhaps ye have done

the same thing in the East too. But though ye have been able to take away the Words, yet ye cannot take away the Faith. These words of Ambrose demonstrate, (quite contrary to what He imagined,) that there had been indeed a Corruption of the Text, not by the Arians taking away any words, but by Others (either carelessly, or in the overheat of their Zeal against the Arians,) having added to the Latin Text, words which

## on Matt. 12, 31.

in the Original never were there. And 'tis a strange Instance of the Ignorance of that great Man, and of the Latin Church at that time; to be imposed upon so far, as to receive even into the publick Books of the Church, so manifest an Interpolation.

# $\mathrm{N}^{\circ}$ 39.

MATT. XII, 31. The Blasphemy against the Holy Ghost, shall not be forgiven unto Men : c.

By the Holy Ghost here, was meant (I said) not the Person, but the Works of the Holy Ghost : For no reason can be given, why Blaasphemy against the Person of the Spirit of God, should be more unpardonable, than Blaasphemy against the Person of the Son of God, or than Blaasphemy against the Person of God [the Father] himself. But the Works of the Spirit, being the greatest and last Means of Conviction that God ever intended to afford Men ; the rejecting of Them, was consequently the Highest Aggravation of Guilt.

To this, your Friend replies : Where This Doctrine, [that Blasphemy against God the Father, and the Son of God, are pardonable,]

189

In the Scripture doctrine, N° 1121.
In the Answer, pag. 125.

pag. 125.

190

## A Commentary

ble,] is to be found, is hard to tell; The Text does not seem to infer it, nor the parallel places in the rest of the Evangelists. I answer: 'Tis to be found in the Words immediately going before the Text: ALL manner of Sin and BLASPHEMY shall be forgiven unto Men; [In St Mark it is, All Sins shall be forgiven unto the Sons of Men, and Blasphemies wherewith soever they shall blaspheme:] But the Blasphemy against the Holy Ghost shall not be forgiven unto Men.

But he proceeds: Blasphemy against the Father (he says) is unpardonable, according to Levit. XXIV, 16, He that blasphemeth the Name of the Lord, shall surely be put to Death. I answer: A Capital Crime, is one thing; and what our Saviour says shall never be forgiven, is another.

Your Friend replies : The Jewish Doctors were of another Mind : They had a Tradition among them, that there were certain Crimes, that excluded the Sinners from having Any Portion in the World to come : Among which, was pronouncing the Name Jehovah according to its Letters : Now they looked upon the Egyptian to have cursed God, by such a Prominciation of the Sacred Name : The Consequence of which is, that his Blasphemy was irremissible in the World to come. But this their Reasoning was grounded up-

011

on Matt. 12, 31.

on so weak a Superstition, that he does not think fit to insist much upon it.

He goes on therefore to another Argument : If Any Crime is unpardonable, Blasphemy doubtless against the God of Heaven, or the Person of the Father, is so ; as being an Act of open Defiance and most provoking Insolence. I answer : Though no Crime is, in its own Nature, greater than Blasphemy against God ; yet a Crime may, upon account of certain Circumstances, become more unpardonable, as being more remote from Repentance. Of This Kind is Blaspheming the Greatest and Last Means of Conviction ; ascribing the most beneficial , as well as most powerful Works, to an unclean Spirit ; and thereby taking away all possible means of distinguishing Good from Evil : Which therefore our Saviour does accordingly in express Words here declare to be more unpardonable than Any other Blasphemy whatsoever.

The Texts he alleges out of the Epistle to the Hebrews, and out of St John, are by almost All Divines understood, not of a Sin absolutely unpardonable, but of a Sin exceedingly difficult to be amended and pardoned; namely Total Apostacy from Christianity. And if it was indeed absolutely unpardonable, yet it would be upon the same Account as the Sin in the Text is declared to be so; namely, because it is blasphemously renouncing the last Means of Conviction and Amendment.
