## An Answer to the Author

' ftinction of Persons; EXCEPTING onrily implies such Distinction : Or else,

- ly where the Construction itself neceffarily implies such Distinction : Or else,

if we take in the Notion of different Perof God absolutely, or the One God, the

' fons ; then where-ever mention is made

- True God, or the like; the meaning muft*

be, that the Father, considered with his

Son and Spirit, is God, the One God, the

True God &c. This seems to be the plain

natural Construction of all the passages

cited in these Three Sections.

ANSW. In the second Section alone of my Scripture-Doctrine, (besides the Texts cited in the first and third Sections,) there are more than three Hundred Texts, wherein the word God, by the Construction of the Text itself, is of necessity confined to the Person of the Father singly, because expressly contradistinguished in the very same Sentence, either from the Son or the Spirit or Both. As when St Paul says, 1 Cor. 6; 4, 5, 6, The same Spirit, the same Lord, the same GOD: And Ephes. 4; 4, 5, 6, One Spirit, One Lord, One GOD and Father of All: And the like. Now to allege, as this Learned Author does, that the word God, in all the passages I cited in those Three Sections, ought not to be confined to the Person of the Father singly, EXCEPTING only where the Construction itself necessarily so requires; What is this, but alleging,

## of Some Confiderations &c.

leging, that ALL the Texts I cited, are misapplied, EXCEPTING only somewhat more than THREE HUNDRED, which the Construction itself necessarily shows to be rightly applied? The Reader will observe This to be a considerable Exception; even though some Few of the other Texts had been really misapplied by me; as this Learned Author has not shown that Any of them are. Again: To affirm (as this Learned Author does,) that according to the plain natural Construction of those numerous passages I cited, wherein the Father is stiled God and the One God, in express Contradiction to the Son and Spirit in the very words of the Text itself; to affirm (I say) that the meaning of these Texts must be, that the word God absolutely, or the One God, signifies the Father considered with his Son and Spirit; What is This, but affirming the Father alone, to be Father and Son and Spirit? Besides: When he says that the word God is applicable to the Divine Nature considered absolutely, without any distinction of Persons; is not This begging the Question directly? For Where does it appear that the Divine Nature considered absolutely, ever signifies any thing else, but the Nature of God, of the One God and Father of all, considered only abstract perhaps from the Relation of Paternity? Though even That indeed, can hardly be

S 3 abstraced

277

278

## An Answer to the Author

abstracted. For it seems to be an infeparable Character of the Divine Nature, that is, of Him whose That Nature is; (For Nature itself, is nothing but an Abstract Name;) to be Father of all, κατὰ πάντων, Eph. 4, 6.

## Fig. 97. OBf. ' If Chrift is Supreme over All,

though not absolutely Supreme over All;—

wherein is Christ distinguished from the

Father, but only as he is a Son? For

- take away the Distinctions of Father and

- Son, and Christ is----Supreme Lord of

the Universe.

ANSW. Christ is never stiled either Supreme over all, or absolutely Supreme over all. He is indeed over All, and Lord of all; but still always excepting (as St Paul directs us) Him that did put all things under him. Supremacy therefore, is an Attribute of the Father, absolutely incommunicable. And to say, that, taking away the Distinctions of Father and Son, Christ is Supreme Lord of the Universe; is only saying in other words, that the Distinction between the Father and the Son is inconsiderable and to be neglected, because the Son also is Supreme, excepting only that he is not Supreme.

pag. 93 OBf. ' If the word God, when mentioned absolutely, is applied to the Son in

Scripture,

## of Some Confiderations &c.

Scripture, then not only the passages collected in the second Section of Dr Clarke's Scripture-Doctrine, are very improperly ranged under their Title, but the first and third Sections are altogether useless. For if Christ be God absolutely, he is also the Only God, the Living God, etc.

ANSW. It should here have been added, by the very same reason; that the Son is also the Father of All, the One God and Father of All: And then the Force of the Argument, would have been evident to every Reader. The Truth of the matter, is plainly This: The word, God, when used absolutely, always signifies the Father: I cited, in that second Section which this Learned Author here refers to, more than three Hundred Texts, wherein the Construction of the words necessarily and confessedly determines it so to signify. Were there now any Texts, wherein the same word, used in the same Manner and in the same Sense, clearly signified the Son; it would follow of necessity, that the Father was the Son, and the Son the Father. But the Case is not so. There are, in all, thirteen Texts, wherein the word God has by some been thought to signify the Son;

[Matt. 1, 23; Luke 1, 16; John. 1, 1; 10, 33; 20, 28; Acts 20, 28; Rom. 9, 5; 1 Tim. 3, 16; Tit. 2, 13; Heb. 1, 8; 2 Pet. 1, 1; 1 John. 3, 16; 5, 20.] In

S 4 Five

279

280

Five of these Texts, [viz. Luke 1 16; Tit. 2, 13; 2 Pet. 1, 1; 1 Job. 3, 16; & 1 Job. 5, 20;] it certainly signifies, not the Son, but the Father. Six of them, [viz. Matt. 1, 23; Job. 10, 33; 20, 28; Aetys 20, 28; Rom. 9, 5; & 1 Tim. 3, 16;] are justly contested as ambiguous: That is to say; Either it is ambiguous whether the Word [θεὶς, God,] was originally in the Text at all; as Aetys 20, 28; Rom. 9, 5; & 1 Tim. 3, 16; Or else it is ambiguous whether it refers to the Father or the Son; and, if it refers to the Son, yet it is then clearly understood in a sense different from what this Learned Author supposes; as in the Three last-cited Texts, and in Matt. 1, 23; Job. 10, 33; & Job. 20, 28. The Two remaining Texts, [Job. 1, 1; & Heb. 1, 8;] wherein the Son is clearly stiled God, do each of them at the same time no less clearly distinguish him from ὀ θεὶς the God whom he was with, whom he came from, and who is stiled His God anointing him, &c. Which Distinction, is also (as I now observed) no less evident in every one of the contested Texts, supposing the Reading of them to be true, and the Son to be there spoken of: As may be seen in my Notes upon each of the Texts themselves, both in the fore-going Papers, and in my Scripture-Doctrine. Add to this the numerous places, wherein Christ declares that the Son can do nothing of himself, but lives and

## of Some Confiderations &e.

and is sent and acts in all things by the Will and Authority of the Father. See Scripture-Doctrine, Part II, § 34 36.

OBf. ' Had not Christ been God in the fame Sense, in which the word was understood before his Appearance in the Flesh; [That is to say, had not the Son been, πατρὰ πάντων, The Father of All;] it is very reasonable to believe, that he would have been stiled God, (if he had taken upon him That Name at all,) with some lower Titles, Epithets, or Attributes, than the Father was: As, Divine Person, and the like: Or, at least, with some Exceptions; as God, but not the most High, &c.

ANSW. This is the Very manner, in which He is stiled God in the New Testament. For thus Origen, (in the passage I cited above, pag. 65, 66, 67;) shows that θεός in the first Verse of St John's Gospel, as there distinguished from οἱ θεός, signifies a Divine Person. And Eusebius, in his Theolog. Ecclesiast. lib. 2, cap. 17, does, in a professed Discourse upon That very Text, largely explain the same Distinction. Now 'tis well known that Origen and Eusebius understood Greek better, and had more Learning, than all the other Fathers that ever wrote. Nay, and even Clemens Alexandrinus himself, who (as I observed above,

281

pag.

282

## An Answer to the Author

pag. 67,) spake in his Pedagogus more confusedly; in his Strom. 3, thus expresses the same Notion with great Distinctness; [ὑ γαρ Θεον ἀσθλως θεοσειπεν ο τῆς τῶι αρρυρε θεοταξει τον παντοκρατομεν δηλαώς,] The Scripture (faith he, speaking of a particular Text,) does not here use barely the word Θεος, but, by adding the Article ο Θεος, signifies that it means Him who is Supreme over All.

Then as to the Exceptions which he thinks ought to have been made; St Paul answers, that the Exception this Learned Author wants, is and ought always to be made. 'Tis manifest, says he, 1 Cor. 15, 27, (that is, 'tis Manifest to common Sense, without needing to be often repeated,) that He is [always] excepted, which did put all things under him. And our Saviour himself, in all his Discourses in the Gospel, constantly speaks of Himself with Lower Titles and Epithets, than of the Father; as being sent forth from Him, speaking and acting by His Authority, and doing all things to His Honour and Glory.

not the eternal Godhead here meant, as

' Rom. 1, 20 ; but Divine Power, as the

- word Θεόττης is rendred by *D' Clarke*.

## of Some Confiderations &c.

ANSW. In That very Passage it self, Rom. 1, 20, θεότns signifies nothing else but Divine Power or Dominion; not the Substance of God, which this learned Author seems erroneously to think the Word Godhead signifies. The Words are, ἡτε αὐτῶι δὲν τῆς δύναμης ἡ δείότης, his eternal Power and Divinity or Divine Dominion. Θείοτns, is as much an Attribute, as δύναμnis; not the Substance, but the Divine Dominion of God. So likewise therefore in That other Passage, Col. 2, 9, In him dwelleth all the Fulness of the Godhead; the meaning is, the Fulness of Divine Power, Dominion and Authority: In like manner, as Col. 1, 19, It pleased the Father, that in Him should all Fulness dwell; and Job. 14, 10, The Father that dwelleth in Me, he doth the Works; That is, not the Person, but the Divine Power, [ἡ θεότns] of the Father, dwelleth in Christ. Thus the Word [θεότns Divinity,] Always signifies; in the same manner as αὐθρωπότεns, and all other Words of the like Formation. And 'tis as great an Abuse of Language, to suppose [θεοτns] the Deity or Divinity, that is, the Dominion of God, to signifie the Substance of God; as it would be to understand [αὐθρωπότεns] Manhood, to signifie the Substance of Man. Where Deity is put (by a mere Idiom of the English Language) for God himself,

283

284

## An Answer to the Author

self, as Acts 17, 29; 'tis in the Greek, not ἡ θεότης, but τὸ θεῶν. Further: 'Tis worth observing upon this occasion, that not only θεότητα, the Divinity, or Supreme Dominion, of God; but even This other Word itself, θεοὺς God, has in Scripture, and in all Books of Morality and Religion, a relative Signification; and not, as in metaphysical Books, an Absolute One. As is evident from the relative Terms, which in moral Writings may always be joined with it. For instance: In the same manner as we say, my Father, my King, and the like; so it is proper also to say, my God, the God of Israel, the God of the Universe, and the like: Which Words are expressive of Dominion and Government. But, in the metaphysical way, it cannot be said, My Infinite Substance, The Infinite Substance of Israel, or the like. Which plainly shows in what Sense the Word Godhead, always ought to be understood in Theological Matters.

An Answer to the Author

tween the Mediatour and the Perfon mediated to? Have we an Advocate with the Father and with the Son too? Is it in vain that the Apostle directs us to come unto God by Christ, as by our great High-Priest, Heb. 7, 25; 4, 14? Is it in vain that St Paul exhorts us to give thanks to God, even the Father, by Christ, Col. 3, 17? When we return Thanks to God for so loving the World; that he gave his only-begotten Son; would it be as proper to return Thanks to the SON for sending his only-begotten Son? When St Paul blesses the God and Father of our Lord Jesus Christ, for having predefinuted us by Jesus Christ to himself, and made us Accepted in the beloved; and praiseth—the God of our Lord Jesus Christ, the Father of Glory,—for the working of his mighty Power, which he wrought in Christ, when he raised him from the dead, and set him at his own right Hand in heavenly places, Eph. 1; 3, 2c. might ALL the SAME Praises and Thanksgivings have with equal Propriety been offered to Christ himself? When in our Devotions we say, Thou, O Christ, art most High in the Glory of God the Father; would it have been as proper and as true, to say, Thou, O Father, art most High in the Glory of the Son? The Third Synod of Carthage decreed, on purpose to prevent all Confusion in Divine Worship, and agreeably to the constant Practise of the whole Priini-

## of Some Confiderations &c.

Primitive Church; [ut cüm ad Altare assifitur, Semper ad Patrem dirigatur oratio,] that when the Priest stands at the Altar, he should Always direct his Prayer to the Father: And can this Learned Author imagine, that the Memorial of the Sacrifice of the Death of Christ, may as properly be offered to the Son, who was himself That Sacrifice; as to the Father, to whom, and to whom only, that Sacrifice was once offered as an Oblation and Propitiation for the Sins of the World? I confess, I can no way see, how this Confusion of Persons can possibly be reconciled with the Scripture-Notion of Christ's being our Great High-Priest, our Mediatour and Advocate with (not what This Learned Author calls the Divine Nature, but, as St John expresses it,) the FATHER; 1 Joh. 2, 1.

pag. 107. OBf. ' According to This Scheme, God is a word of Office only, as Master and

ANSW. That the word God in Scripture, is indeed always a relative word of Office, signifying personal Dominion, Dignity, or Government; is evident from hence; that in like manner as we say, My Master, My Father, My King, and the like; so the Scripture teaches us to say also, MY God, The God of Israel, and the like: Whereas on the other side we cannot say, My Divine Nature, the Divine Nature of Israel, or the like. Which evidently shows, that the word God in Scripture is always used to express a Person, and not That which this Learned Author stiles the Divine Nature.

OBf. In this passage [I and my Father are One,] 'why may not [ἐν] signify the same as [εἰς,] viz. One and the same God, or, (in Dr Clarke's Sense of the word Person,) one and the same Person, that is, one and the same Being?—If [τοὺς ἀυτῶγεννήδέν, That which is conceived in her, Matt. 1, 20,] signifies the same as [ὁ γεννηθέις, He which is conceived;] and [τὸ γεννώμενον αἴγιον, That holy Thing which shall be born, Luke 1, 35,] signifies the same as [ὁ αἴγίσ, That Holy Person;] and [δύο τὴν υπόστασις πράγμμαπ, Two Things in Substidence,] is ufed by Origen to signify [two real distinct Persons:]

pag. 1c9.

- Why then may not the word $[ \varepsilon^{\nu} ]$ in S<sup>c</sup>

- John, mean One and the fame Being, as

- well as One and the same Thing ?

ANSW. The Learned Bishop Bull tells us, [Defens. Sect. 2, cap. 9, §11,] that [res singularis per se subsistens, in rebus intellectu præditis idem est quod persona,] a particular Being, subsisting by itself, is, in things induced with Understanding, the same as Person. The reason therefore, (besides that the whole Thread of his Discourse leads to another Interpretation,) why our Saviour could not here mean to affirm, that He and his Father were One Being, is because he would thereby have affirmed that they were One Person; Which this Learn- T 2 ed

## An Answer to the Author

ed Author, (though his Interpretation necessarily infers it, yet) cares not to affirm. As to the critical Distinction between εἰς and εἴν, the Truth is evidently This. Because every Person is a Being, but every Being is not a Person; therefore εἰς, which is of the more limited Signification, and expresses intelligent Being or Person only, can never possibly be extended to signify Being in general: But εἴν, which signifies Being in general, may also be used of such a Being, as is an intelligent Being, that is, a Person. Therefore πὸ γεννησέν and πὸ αγον might properly be used by St Matthew and St Luke, as well as οὐαγωγο and οὐγεννησείς; because the Subject mentioned, (besides that perhaps πὸ πασιδίου is referred to), was equally both a Holy Thing and a Holy Person. But εἴν, in this place of St John, cannot signify One Being; because the Subject spoken of, being an Intelligent Being, it would consequently have meant the same as εἰς One Person: Which confessedly not being our Saviours intention to affirm, it follows of Necessity that the words must be understood figuratively, in the manner that has been above explained at large, pag. 144, ἀνc.

Because the Arians allege This Text, to prove Christ to be God in their Sense; does it therefore follow, that it Cannot be understood of him as Man? Do Arians never allege more Texts, than are conclusive of what they allege them for? And is it not at least Possible, that Christ might mean the same, when he says, I and my Father are One; as when he says, They [the Disciples] may be One, even as We are One; I in Them, and Thou in Me, &c.

## OB7. There being many Passages, where- pag. 122?

in Dr Clarke owns it to be doubtful or ambiguous whether the Father or Son be meant [by the word, Lord;] This is a very good Argument, that it is all one, of which those passages are understood.

ANSW. The Subject-matter of some particular Texts, is indeed such, as makes it ambiguous whether Christ or God the Father be spoken of in those particular Texts. But it does not at all from thence follow, (which is the general Inference this Learned Author every where aims at,) that therefore in the whole Scripture there is no Distinction made between the Father and the Son. The word, Lord, T 4 in

## An Answer to the Author

in St Paul's Epistles, generally signifies Christ: And 'tis a manner of speaking intirely new in the Christian Church, to

fay that thereby is meant The One Supreme God, the God of Heaven and Earth, the God and Lord whom we Christians are to adore, without considering whether the words refer more properly to the Son or to the Father. It is also to be observed, that One of the Texts he here cites, is by no means ambiguous; Tit, 2, 13, The glorious appearing of the Great God. For if he had cited the Whole text, The glorious appearing of the Great God, and our Saviour Jesus Christ; the Reader would naturally have perceived That to be the true Sense of it, which I have largely explained above, pag. 85. Yet This Text, and Luke 1, 16, (which is also largely explained above, pag. 119;) are the Only Two among the ambiguous Texts, wherein the word God is mentioned at all. The rest have only the word Lord in them. And therefore he had not sufficient ground

OBF. ' Though Two Men are individually distinct from one another, BE CAUSE they are Finite Beings, etc.

ANSW. Two Men are individually distinct from one another, not because they are finite, not because they are temporary, nor because of any other Quality whatsoever; but because they are Two. God is a Being infinite and eternal: Does it therefore follow that nothing can be distinct from him, because his Immensity includes all Space, and his Eternity exhausts all Duration? This Arguing, if it was just, (as indeed it is no Argument at all,) would lead further than this Learned Author seems to perceive.

ANSW. These words, if I understand their Meaning, are an express Giving up of the whole Question. For if there is no Difference between us, SO FAR AS WE take Scripture for our Guide; then Why should any of us indeavour to impose his Opinion upon another, beyond the limits of this agreed Rule? And if all the Forms of speaking used upon this Account in Scripture, are transcribed into our Publick Devotions, in Those Passages of the Liturgy which I cited in my Third Part, § 1, 2, 3, 4 and 5; that is, in those Passages which I cited as expressing clearly and explicitly the same Doctrine with mine; then Why should this learned Author be displeased with any Man, for desiring that these unexceptionable Expressions, which he allows to contain All the Forms of speaking used upon this Account in Scripture, were uniformly adhered to; without the Addition of Any Others, more difficult

## of Some Confiderations &c.

difficult to be understood, and more easy to be excepted against? Putting which case, the whole remaining Dispute between us would THEN INDEED be only about Metaphysical Speculations, and by all means to be wholly laid aside as needless and of no importance.

OBJ. 'That Father, Son, and Holy

Ghoft, are not three Names only, we all

agree : But that they should be Three different Beings, I can no ways comprehend.

ANSW. This (I think) is an express Contradiction: For between Being and not Being there can be no middle. Whatever is not a Being, is no Being; and whatever is no Being, can at most be only a Name or Denomination, a Mode, Quality, Relation, or the like; things which have no Existence but in Our Conceptions, distinct from the Existence of the Substance to which they belong. The Schoolmen have endeavoured to find out something intermediate between Being and not Being, by means of what they call an internal Production: And the Learned Bp. Bull himself is forced finally to have recourse to this unintelligible Term of Art, (Sect. 4, cap. 4,) in order to maintain an Explication of the Unity of God upon such a Foot, as can very hardly be reconciled with his own Discourse in the rest of that excellent Section concerning the

299

pag. 129.

300

## An Answer to the Author

the Subordination of the Son. For a Subordination of one Person to another, necessarily supposes the two Persons to be distinct. But now the Notion of internal Production, can in reality mean nothing else but to destroy That Distinctness; and consequently ends in an unavoidable Contradiction. For if internal Production was meant only in opposition to being extrinsic to God; in That Sense 'tis manifest that internal Production belongs equally to All Beings whatsoever; because In Him All things have their Being: But if internal Production means such a Production, as gives the thing produced no distinct Existence of its own; (which is what the Schoolmen aim at;) then 'tis manifest it signifies a Production of Nothing, that is, No Production at all. See a remarkable passage of Justin Martyr upon this Head, cited above, pag. 157, 158. Neither can it here reasonably be alledged, that these Expressions are mysterious: For though, if God himself had been pleased to reveal any thing in such Words as we could not distinctly understand, it would indeed have been very reasonable to believe firmly that those words had a distinct Meaning, though we apprehended it not: yet when Men invent Words, whose Signification They themselves cannot distinctly explain; it is then, on the other side, no less reasonable

of Some Confiderations &c. unable to believe, that these words have really no signification at all.

OBf. 'Dr Clarke affures us, intelligent

- Being and Person are equivalent Terms;

though he oftentimes ufes the word, Per-

' son, as if they were not.

ANSW. Dr Clarke No-where uses the word, Person, as if intelligent Being and Person were not equivalent Terms. In That expression, (which is the only one I can think of, to which this Learned Author may here possibly allude,) that the Son appeared under the Old Testament in the Person of the Father; the meaning evidently is, that he appeared and spake, as representing the Person of the Father.

OBJ. The Distinctions of original and derived, make no manner of Difference in

- our Notion of Eternity.

ANSW. The Question is not about Duration, or any other metaphysical Qualities, but about Dominion and Authority; which Alone is That which makes God to be God, (in the moral or religious sense of the Word,) ο πάντωνκράτωρ, Supreme over all. For Dominion and Duty are Relatives between Him that Governs and them which are governed. Metaphysical and Absolute Qualities, may be Objects of Admiration; but relative Dominion only, is the Foundation of Duty. Space

301

pag. 131.

pag. 131.

302

## An Answer to the Author

Space is Eternal, but it is not therefore God. If Matter were Infinite and Eternal, still it would not at all be our God. And if any other Being had any other Attributes whatsoever, yet still if it had not Dominion over Us, Neither should We owe any Duty to It. 'Tis Dominion only, that makes God to be God to Us; and therefore the Scripture so frequently uses the word τωντοκραττωρ, Supreme over all, as equivalent to the Title, God. Now in the Notion of Dominion, 'tis very evident that original and derived are Distinctions which make a plain and necessary Difference. Which is all that I contend for.

- very body strictly to this Rule, so as not

' prefessions of their own, or to draw any

remote Consequences from those of Scrip-

ture; why does he himself lay down such

' Propositions, and use such Terms and

- Forms of speaking, as are no where to be

- found in the Sacred Writings? --- Tis

' plain,

303

of Some Confiderations &c.

plain, that Dr Clarke's Scripture-doctrine pag. 136.

of the Trinity, is not the Doctrine of

' Chrift and his Apoftles, declared Neceffary

in their OWN WORDS.——Dr Clarke

- appears to be Self-condemned for ufing unthat he condemns----introducing the like

Scriptural Expressions,at the same time

unscriptural Expressions into the Publick

Forms of Faith and Worship.

ANSW. In explaining of Scripture, it is impossible not to use unscriptural Expressions; but then those unscriptural Expressions are to be look'd upon only as the private Opinions of Men, and are of no further Authority than what the weight of Reason and Argument gives them. All that is desirable in this case, is, that as few unscriptural Expressions as possible, be used in Creeds or in such other Forms, as are supposed to have an Authority in determining Mens Opinions, distinct from that of mere Reason and Argument. The Inconveniences of which, are very largely and learnedly set forth by Bp. Taylor, in his Liberty of Prophecying, and in his Epistle before it. God forbid that I should ever desire any Explication or Opinion of mine, so far as the Expressions are unscriptural, to be lookt upon as Necessary Doctrine. Nothing is Necessary, but what God has made so: Nothing is Necessary, but what Christ and his Apostles, have taught in their own words. Men ought

pag. 137.

to

304

```markdown

```

## An Answer to the Author

to indeavour to explain these Doctrines to Themselves and Others; and in such Explications there is no hurt in using (nay, Men cannot but use) unscriptural Expressions. But then all such Explications ought to be lookt upon only as Humane Opinions, not as of Authority to determine Mens Faith, but as Assistances to convince their Reason and Judgment. And then all Controversies would either soon be at an End; or (which is as well,) would do no hurt to any fincere Christian.

pag. 138. OBf. If those Expressions which the Arians first presumed to introduce, are

- unjustifiable Phrases :—Why may we not

- former be false, (and if they are not, they

- should not be condemned,) the latter must

- as well ufe the [contrary] positive Expresfi-

ons, as condemn the other; when, if the

needs be true, as being directly contra-

- dictory to them?

ANSW. Of two contradictory Propositions, one indeed must of necessity be true, and the other false. But does it from thence follow, that if a Mans affirming one thing be justly blameable, as being a presuming to be wise above what is written, and an intruding into things which he hath not seen; therefore it will presently be right and commendable to affirm and impose the Contrary? Are there not many cafes in Nature,

## of Some Confiderations &c.

ture, wherein it would be presumptuous to determine with too much assurance, on either side of a Question? and much more so, to impose such Determinations upon Others?

OBJ. 'We may as well presume to say, that Christ is, by an ineffable Communication of the Divine Nature, God;

as that he is, by an ineffable Communication of Divine Powers and Dignity God.

ANSW. There is This Difference between the Two Manners of Expression; that the latter is very clear and intelligible, the former is very dark and ambiguous. For if by the Divine Nature be meant Divine Powers, Dominion and Dignity; then they are only Two different manners of expressling One and the Same Thing. But if by The Divine Nature be meant (as this Learned Author seems to understand it,) individually God himself; then the Father's communicating the Divine Nature to the Son, is as much as to say, his communicating HIMSELF [his individual Self] to the Son. Which, far from being an Explication of Scripture, is on the contrary infinitely harder to understand, than any thing we meet withal in Scripture.

An Answer to the Author

p. 140. OBf. ' Though we ought not to pre- fume to explain, in what manner Christ

derived his Being, Power, Knowledge,

and other Attributes and Authority from

' the Father ; yet we ought to explain

' WHAT BEING he derived ; whether

the SAME or another, &c.

ANSW. That is to say: We ought to explain whether Christ derived from the Father, THE FATHERS Being, or his OWN Being. I could not have imagined that This was a Question which needed any Explication. But this Learned Author determines it (pag. 141,) that the Son's Being is the same with the Father's; that is, that the Son's Being is the Father's Being. Which, I acknowledge, is what I cannot understand.

- ligious Account, to any other Being what-

ANSW. That there is but One God; and Whom the Scriptures expresfly declare to be

That

## of Some Confiderations &c.

That One God; and how Christ is nevertheless consistently stiled God; I have shown at large in my Scripture-Doctrine, and in the fore-going Papers. Whether this Learned Author's Notion be more plain, and less embarrassed with difficult and dubious Expressions, I leave our Readers to judge. As to the Question concerning Worship, I only repeat That Text before-cited, Rev. 1, 5, Unto him that loved us and washed us from our Sins in his own Blood, and bath made us Kings and Priests to God and his Father, to Him be Glory and Dominion for ever and ever: And ch. 5, 9, Thou wast Slain, and hast redeemed us to God by thy Blood,——and hast made us to our God Kings and Priests:——Worthy is the Lamb that was slain:——Blessing and Honour——be unto him that sitteth upon the Throne, and unto the Lamb for ever and ever: And I ask, whether here be not a kind or degree of Worship, plainly paid to Christ upon a religious Account; which is different from the Worship paid to God the Father, and which cannot possibly be paid to God the Father, any more than the Worship of God the Father can be paid to the Lamb that was Slain?

OBf. 'Hereticks will not be kept with in the Rule of Scripture, by a bare Con U2 demnation

307

p48 142.

308

## An Answer to the Author

- demnation of their Opinions, but will

' continue to use fuch unscriptural Expreffions as are brought for the Defense of

their Errours, notwithstanding their being required by those in Power to forbear

the Ufe of them. If Dr Clarke be of

another Opinion, I wish, for our Con-

' viction, this Method were Now taken;

' &c.

ANSW. I am entirely of Opinion, that who soever is not willing to forbear the Use of any unscriptural Expression whatsoever, which happens at any Time to give Offense to the Church, and is barely forbidden by his Superiours; is a very bad Christian. And if This Method only, had always been taken on all sides, without imposing contrary unscriptural Expressions; I am fully perfwaded it would have prevented numberless Controversies in the Christian World, and been of infinite Benefit to the Church. As to the words, Hereticks and Herefies, which (since the first and purest Ages) have been so perpetually abused in an unscriptural Sense, to signify only Differences of Opinion, (though This Learned Author is indeed generally very Candid in forbearing uncharitable Cenfures;) I shall only add That wife Sentence of the Learned and Pious Bishop Taylor, in the Epistle before his Liberty of Prophesying:

of Some Confiderations &c.

Prophesying: I am sure, says he, that I know what Drunkenness &c, is; but I am not sure that Such an Opinion is Hereby; Neither would Other Men be so sure as they think for, if they did consider it aright, and observe the infinite Deceptions, and Causes of Deceptions, in wise Men, and in most Things, and in all doubtful Questions; and that they did not mistake Confidence for Certainty. His large Discourse upon the same Subject, in the Second Section of his Liberty of Prophesying, is also well worth the perusal of every serious and sober-minded Man.

309

And now, having given a distinct and (I hope) satisfactory Answer to all the Objections that have hitherto been urged against me: I take leave upon this occasion to declare once for all; that as I have upon mature and long Consideration, and with the utmost Sincerity, drawn up my Thoughts upon This Subject, and laid them before the Publick, with my Reafons for them, as clearly as I am able; so I willingly submit what I have written, to the judgment of every honest and impartial Reader, who is capable of examining things of this Nature,

310

## An Answer to the Author

ture. To whom, if what I have offered has Weight and Truth on its side, it will, I make no doubt, approve itself: If it has not, I am content it should find no better Reception than it deserves. I desire not, to impose my Opinion upon any Man; but only to prevail with Men to study the Scripture, and to make It their only Rule in matters of Revelation; and that inquisitive Persons would be pleased to Compare what has been or shall be objected against me, with what I have said either in Answer to, or in order to obviate, such Objections. It would be endless for me to repeat the same things again and again, as often as any one shall please to tell the World he differs from me, without offering any New Arguments for such his Opinion. Whenever either my own further Searches, or the learned Enquiries of Others, convince me that I am mistaken in my present Notions, I shall think my self obliged to own it to the World. In the mean time I hope I may without Offense desire, that if I am Silent hereafter, it may be interpreted as a Declaration, that I see no reason to change my Sentiments. I have no Concern, but for Truth; and from whatever hand it comes, shall readily receive it: But what is not Truth, or does not to me appear so, I cannot embrace. And This I shall for the

## of Some Confiderations &c.

the future think I sufficiently declare, by giving no Answer to any thing that shall be writ; which, upon a careful Examination, I cannot find to contain Arguments either in themselves really weighty, or at least not clearly answered already.

# ADVERTISEMENT.

THE Third Volume of the MEMOIRS OF LITERATURE for the Year 1713; containing an Account of the Present State of Learning, both at Home and Abroad, is now compleat with an Index to it.

The Memoirs for January, February, and March, of this present Year 1714; contain, besides an Account of some Books published in England, and consequently better known than foreign Books: I. An Account of Dr Vitringa's Commentary upon Isaiah. II. An Account of some Differtations upon several Subjects relating to Religion and Philology, by Mr Hewet, heretofore Bishop of Auranches. III. A Letter written by a Professor in the University of Alcala, concerning a Method of Reading the Hebrew Tongue without Points. IV. An Account of Mr Bassnage's Critical Remarks upon the Republick of the Hebrews. V. An Account of Jerome of Prague's Trial. VI. An Account of a Treatise of the Gout, containing an easy and infallible Method of curing it, tried by the Author himself, who was affiliated with that Disease. VII. An Account of some other foreign Books, which are omitted here for Brevity-sake. VIII. Several Pieces of News relating to Learning.

This last Article contains, the Projects of three several Works, to be published by Mr Chamberlaine, Mr Difney, and another English Gentleman. 2. A short Account of the following Books. Philologia. Biblica. Observationes Sacrae ad Evangelium Matthæi, by Mr Olearius. A New Edition of Kepler's Works. Father Calmet's Literal Commentary upon the Proverbs, Ecclesiaftes, the Song of Songs, and the Wisdom of Solomon. De Linguæ Latinæ in Germania per amplius XVII fæcula fatis Commentarii. De Hypocriti Gentilium circa cultum Deorum Schediasma Literarium. 3. Several other new Things of the same Nature.

These Memoirs, printed for the Author, may be had of the Bookfellers of London and Westminster, and at the News-shops at Temple-Bar and Charing-Cross.

BOOKS

BOOKS Written by the Reverend Dr Clarke ; And Printed for James Knapton, at the Crown in St Paul's Church-Yard.

A Discourse concerning the Being and Attributes of God, the Obligations of Natural Religion, and the Truth and Certainty of the Christian Revelation. In Answer to Mr. Hobbs, Spinoza, the Author of the Oricles of Reason, and other Deniers of Natural and Revealed Religion. Being sixteen Sermons preach'd at the Cathedral-Church of St. Paul, in the Years 1704, and 1705. at the Lecture founded by the Honourable Robert Boyle Esq; pr. 6 s.

A Paraphrase on the Four Evangelifts. Wherein, for the clearer Understanding the Sacred History, the whole Text and Paraphrase are printed in separate Columns, over-against each other. Together with critical Notes on the more difficult Passages. Very useful for Families. In two Volumes, 8vo. The Second Edition. pr. 12s.

Three Practical Essays on Baptism, Confirmation and Repentance : Containing full Instructions for a holy Life, with earnest Exhortations, especially to young Persons, drawn from the Consideration of the Severity of the Discipline of the Primitive Church: The third Edition. This new Edition makes 11 Sheets in Twelves, on good Paper, and a fair Letter. pr. 1s. and for the Encouragement of the Charitable, 112 for 51.bound.

A Letter to Mr. Dodwell ; Wherein all the Arguments in his Epistolary Discourse against the Immortality of the Soul, are particularly answered, and the Judgment of the Fathers concerning that matter truly represented. Together with Four Letters in Answer to the Author of Remarks on the Letter to Mr. Dodwel. To which is added, Some Reflexions on that Part of a Book called Amyntor, or, The Defence of Milton's Life, which relates to the Writings of the Primittive Fathers, and the Canon of the New Testament. pr. 5 s.

THE Measures of Submission to the Civil Magistrate consider'd. In a Defense of the Doctrine deliver'd in a Sermon preach'd before the Right Honourable the Lord-Mayor, Aldermen, and Citizens of London, Sept. 29.1705. The Fourth Edition. In which are added, 1. An Accession-Sermon, preached March 8.1704-5. 2. A Sermon concerning the Unhappiness of Absolute Monarchy, &c. 3. A Sermon concerning St. Paul's Behaviour towards the Civil Magistrate: Pr. 3 s.

Atterbury's Charge of Rebellion: In which the Substance of his late Latin Sermon is produced, and fully examined. The Second Edition. pr. 5s.

Several Discourses concerning the Terms of Acceptance with God. In which, 1. The Terms themselves are distinctly laid down; as they are proposed to Christians in the New Testament. And, 2. Several false Notions of the Conditions of Salvation are considered, particularly of being saved by Faith. Of trusting to external performances. Of the power of Charity to cover Sins. Of relying upon the Merits of Christ. Of Man's Weakness, and God's Grace. Of Repentance. Of the Example of the Thief upon the Cross. Of trusting to a Death-bed Sorrow. Of the Parable of the Labourers in the Vineyard. Of depending upon Amendment in Time to come. pr. 5 s.

The Reasonableness of Conformity to the Church of England. In two Parts. With the Defense of it; and the Persuasive to Lay-Conformity. The Third Edition. To which is added, The brief Defense of Episcopal Ordination. Together with the Reply to the Introduction to the Second Part; and a Postscript relating to the Third Part, of Mr. Calamy's Defense of Moderate Non-conformity. pr. 6s.
